jim-ch01-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q1</div>🟢 Is Java compiled or interpreted?	Both. <code>javac</code> compiles source to platform-neutral bytecode. At runtime, the JVM first <b>interprets</b> bytecode, then <b>JIT-compiles</b> hot methods to native code (C1, then C2 with profile-guided speculation). It can also <b>deoptimise</b> back to the interpreter when a speculation fails. Since JDK 24/25, Project Leyden's AOT cache can additionally store pre-loaded and pre-linked classes and method profiles from a training run. Saying "interpreted" alone is wrong; saying "compiled" alone misses the most interesting part.	ch01 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch01-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q2</div>🟢 Why is <code>main</code> public static void? Can it be different?	<code>public</code> so the launcher can call it, <code>static</code> so no instance is needed, <code>void</code> because exit codes go through <code>System.exit</code>. You can overload <code>main</code>, but the launcher only picks the standard signature (<code>String...</code> varargs also works). Since <b>Java 25 (JEP 512)</b>, the launcher also accepts instance <code>main()</code> methods and compact source files without a class declaration. It tries the classic <code>static void main(String[])</code> first.	ch01 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch01-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q3</div>🟡 What does <code>long x = 1_000_000 * 1_000_000;</code> hold and why?	<code>-727379968</code>. Both operands are <code>int</code>, so the multiplication happens in 32-bit arithmetic, overflows silently (two's complement wrap), and only <i>then</i> is the result widened to <code>long</code>. Fix: make one operand a <code>long</code> (<code>1_000_000L * 1_000_000</code>) or use <code>Math.multiplyExact</code> to fail loudly.	ch01 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch01-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q4</div>🟡 Why does <code>Integer a = 128, b = 128; a == b</code> return false but 127 returns true?	Autoboxing calls <code>Integer.valueOf</code>, which returns cached instances for −128…127 (the upper bound is configurable with <code>-XX:AutoBoxCacheMax</code>). 128 creates two distinct objects, and <code>==</code> compares identity. Always compare wrappers with <code>equals</code> (or unbox explicitly). Bonus: <code>Long</code>, <code>Short</code> and <code>Byte</code> cache the same range, but that isn't configurable.	ch01 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch01-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q5</div>🟡 Explain the NPE in <code>Integer v = flag ? map.get(k) : 0;</code>	The conditional operator's type is determined by its operands. With <code>Integer</code> and <code>int</code>, JLS §15.25 makes the whole expression type <code>int</code>, so <code>map.get(k)</code> is unboxed. If it returns <code>null</code>, you get an NPE, even though the target variable is an <code>Integer</code>. Fix: <code>Integer.valueOf(0)</code> for the other branch, or <code>map.getOrDefault(k, 0)</code>.	ch01 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch01-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q6</div>🟡 Is Java pass-by-reference for objects?	No. Java is strictly pass-by-value. For objects, the <i>reference value</i> is copied. You can mutate the object through the copy, but reassigning the parameter doesn't affect the caller. Proof: you can't write a <code>swap(a, b)</code> that swaps the caller's variables.	ch01 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch01-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q7</div>🟡 Why should you never use <code>double</code> for money? What about <code>new BigDecimal(0.1)</code>?	Binary floating point can't represent most decimal fractions exactly (0.1 repeats forever in binary), so sums drift. <code>new BigDecimal(0.1)</code> faithfully captures that already-inexact double: 0.1000000000000000055511151231257827021181583404541015625. Use <code>new BigDecimal("0.1")</code> or <code>BigDecimal.valueOf(0.1)</code>. Also remember <code>equals</code> compares scale (<code>2.0</code> ≠ <code>2.00</code>), so compare with <code>compareTo</code>, and specify a <code>RoundingMode</code> for division.	ch01 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch01-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q8</div>🟡 What's printed: <code>System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3);</code> and how do you compare doubles?	<code>false</code> (the sum is 0.30000000000000004). Compare using a domain-appropriate tolerance (absolute, relative, or ULP-based), use <code>BigDecimal</code> for exact decimal arithmetic, or use integers in minor units. Know the special values too: <code>NaN != NaN</code>, and <code>0.0 == -0.0</code> is true while <code>Double.equals</code> distinguishes them.	ch01 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch01-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q9</div>🔴 Why can <code>Math.abs()</code> return a negative number?	<code>Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE)</code> is <code>Integer.MIN_VALUE</code>. In two's complement, the range is asymmetric (−2³¹ … 2³¹−1), so there's no positive counterpart, and negation overflows back to itself. This breaks code like <code>Math.abs(hash) % buckets</code>. Use <code>Math.floorMod(hash, buckets)</code> or mask the sign bit. <code>Math.absExact</code> (Java 15) throws instead.	ch01 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch01-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q10</div>🔴 When does a class get initialized? Name two things that look like they'd trigger it but don't.	Initialization happens on: <code>new</code>, a static method call, a static field read or write (unless it's a constant variable), reflection via <code>Class.forName</code>, subclass initialization, or being the main class. Things that don't trigger it: reading a <b>compile-time constant</b> (<code>static final int X = 5</code>, which javac inlines into the caller), creating an array <code>new T[10]</code>, a <code>T.class</code> literal, and accessing a superclass's static field through a subclass (only the declaring class initializes). This laziness is what makes the holder-class singleton idiom thread-safe without locks.	ch01 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch01-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q11</div>🔴 What goes wrong if a constructor calls an overridable method?	The subclass override runs <i>before</i> the subclass's field initializers and constructor body, so it sees default values (null/0/false), even for <code>final</code> fields. This causes NPEs and broken invariants, and lets <code>this</code> escape before construction finishes (which is also a concurrency hazard). Make such methods <code>private</code>, <code>static</code> or <code>final</code>, or move the logic into a factory method. Java 25's flexible constructor bodies let a subclass initialize its own fields before <code>super(...)</code>, which narrows the problem.	ch01 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch01-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q12</div>🔴 Why is <code>int i = 5; i = i++;</code> still 5?	<code>i++</code> evaluates to the <i>old</i> value (5) and increments <code>i</code> to 6 as a side effect. Then the assignment stores the expression's value (5) back into <code>i</code>. Java's strict left-to-right evaluation makes this deterministic. In C, the equivalent is undefined behaviour.	ch01 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch01-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q13</div>🔴 Explain array covariance and why generics are invariant.	<code>String[]</code> is a subtype of <code>Object[]</code>, so <code>Object[] o = new String[1]; o[0] = 1;</code> compiles but throws <code>ArrayStoreException</code> at runtime. That forces a runtime type check on reference-array stores. Generics were made invariant (<code>List&lt;String&gt;</code> is not a <code>List&lt;Object&gt;</code>) to catch this at compile time. Because erasure removes type arguments at runtime, there would be no way to check them later. Wildcards (<code>List&lt;? extends Object&gt;</code>) restore safe read-only covariance.	ch01 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch01-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q14</div>🔴 Why prefer <code>javac --release 8</code> over <code>-source 8 -target 8</code>?	<code>-source/-target</code> only control language level and bytecode version. You still compile against the <i>current</i> JDK's class library, so you can silently link to APIs or signatures that don't exist on Java 8. The classic case is <code>ByteBuffer.flip()</code> returning <code>ByteBuffer</code> (a JDK 9 covariant override), which leads to <code>NoSuchMethodError</code> on JDK 8. <code>--release</code> uses the recorded API signatures of the target release.	ch01 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch01-q015	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q15</div>🔴 A library changed <code>public static final int LIMIT</code> from 10 to 20, but clients still see 10. Why?	Constant variables of primitive or <code>String</code> type are inlined into the client's bytecode at compile time, so clients carry the old literal until they're recompiled. Fix it by exposing the value via a method, or by making it a non-constant expression. Gate library releases with a binary-compatibility checker (japicmp, Revapi).	ch01 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch01-q016	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q16</div>🔴 What does <code>"😀".length()</code> return and why does it matter?	<code>2</code>. Java <code>String</code>/<code>char</code> use UTF-16 code units, and code points above U+FFFF are encoded as surrogate pairs. Naive <code>substring</code> or <code>charAt</code> logic can split pairs and corrupt text. Use <code>codePoints()</code>, <code>codePointCount</code>, <code>offsetByCodePoints</code>, or <code>BreakIterator</code> for user-perceived characters (grapheme clusters).	ch01 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch01-q017	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q17</div>🔵 How would you plan a JDK upgrade across hundreds of services?	Inventory first (versions, frameworks, internal-API usage via <code>jdeps --jdk-internals</code>). Then change the <b>toolchain</b> (new JDK, old <code>--release</code>), then the <b>runtime</b> (run on the new JVM, watch GC ergonomics, illegal reflective access, deprecations), then the <b>language level</b>. Roll out by risk tier with before/after dashboards (CPU, RSS, GC pauses, p99). Keep the base container image and CI matrix on a single, automatically updated patch version. And make the next upgrade cheaper: canary builds on the latest feature release, plus a standing upgrade cadence.	ch01 part::I purpose::execution
jim-ch01-q018	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 01 · Java Platform Fundamentals · Q18</div>🟣 Stack vs heap: are objects always heap-allocated?	Semantically, yes: objects have heap lifetime and identity. Physically, no. C2's escape analysis can prove an object doesn't escape a compilation unit and <i>scalar-replace</i> it into registers or stack slots, eliminating the allocation entirely. Lock elision follows the same reasoning. So "avoid small objects for performance" is often outdated. Profile allocation (JFR, async-profiler <code>-e alloc</code>) before optimising.	ch01 part::I purpose::decisions
jim-ch02-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q1</div>🟢 What's the difference between overloading and overriding?	Overloading: same name, different parameter lists, in the same or a related class. It's resolved at <b>compile time</b> from static types, in three phases (exact/widening → boxing → varargs). Overriding: a subclass redefines an inherited instance method with the same signature. It's resolved at <b>runtime</b> from the receiver's actual class (dynamic dispatch via the vtable/itable). Static methods and fields are hidden, never overridden.	ch02 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch02-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q2</div>🟡 Why must <code>hashCode</code> be overridden whenever <code>equals</code> is?	Hash-based collections first find a bucket using <code>hashCode</code>, then compare candidates with <code>equals</code>. If two equal objects return different hash codes, they land in different buckets, so <code>contains</code> and <code>get</code> miss and <code>HashSet</code> stores logical duplicates. The contract is: equal objects ⇒ equal hash codes. The reverse doesn't have to hold.	ch02 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch02-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q3</div>🟡 What happens if a <code>HashMap</code> key is mutated after insertion?	If the mutation changes <code>hashCode</code>, lookups go to a different bucket and miss. Even a lookup with an "original-looking" key finds the right bucket but fails <code>equals</code>. The entry is orphaned: still counted in <code>size()</code>, unreachable through <code>get</code>, and effectively leaked. Keys should be immutable, or at least their equality-relevant fields should be.	ch02 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch02-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q4</div>🔴 What does <code>m(null)</code> print given <code>m(Object)</code>, <code>m(String)</code> and <code>m(Integer)</code>?	It doesn't compile: the reference is ambiguous. <code>null</code> is applicable to all three. <code>String</code> and <code>Integer</code> are both more specific than <code>Object</code>, but neither is more specific than the other. With only <code>m(Object)</code> and <code>m(String)</code>, it would print "String", because the most specific applicable method wins.	ch02 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch02-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q5</div>🔴 <code>void f(long)</code> and <code>void f(Integer)</code>: which runs for <code>f(5)</code>?	<code>f(long)</code>. Overload resolution phase 1 allows only identity and widening conversions (int → long), with no boxing. Phase 1 finds <code>f(long)</code>, so phase 2 (boxing) is never considered. This preserved backwards compatibility when autoboxing was added in Java 5.	ch02 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch02-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q6</div>🔴 Can you extend a class, add a field and keep <code>equals</code> correct?	Not if the new field participates in equality and the superclass is instantiable. Using <code>instanceof</code> breaks symmetry. Ignoring the new field when comparing with the parent breaks transitivity. Using <code>getClass()</code> keeps the contract but violates Liskov (subclass instances never equal parent instances, so they fail in <code>Set&lt;Parent&gt;</code>). The standard fix is composition with an <code>asParent()</code> view, or making the base abstract or sealed.	ch02 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch02-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q7</div>🟡 Abstract class or interface, and how do you decide?	Use an interface for a <i>type or capability</i> that many unrelated classes can have, and when you need multiple inheritance of type. It can evolve via default methods but holds no state. Use an abstract class when implementations share <i>state</i> or a skeletal algorithm (template method) and the hierarchy is under your control. Idiomatic Java often uses both: interface <code>List</code> + skeleton <code>AbstractList</code>.	ch02 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch02-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q8</div>🟡 Two interfaces define the same default method. What happens?	The implementing class fails to compile until it overrides the method (it can delegate via <code>A.super.m()</code>). The resolution rules are: (1) a method from the class hierarchy beats any interface default; (2) a more specific sub-interface beats its parent; (3) otherwise the class must resolve the conflict explicitly.	ch02 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch02-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q9</div>🔴 How does the JVM dispatch a virtual call, and why are megamorphic call sites slow?	<code>invokevirtual</code> indexes into the receiver class's vtable (a fixed slot per method). <code>invokeinterface</code> searches an itable. The JIT profiles receiver types per call site: monomorphic and bimorphic sites get inlined behind type guards, sometimes devirtualized entirely via class hierarchy analysis. A megamorphic site (three or more receiver types) falls back to a real table dispatch, which <b>prevents inlining</b>. That loses the escape analysis, constant folding and loop optimisations that inlining enables.	ch02 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch02-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q10</div>🟡 What's the memory-leak risk with non-static inner classes?	Each instance holds a hidden reference to its enclosing instance (<code>Outer.this</code>). If the inner object outlives the outer one (it's cached, registered as a listener, or returned to a long-lived caller), it pins the entire outer object graph. Prefer static nested classes. Since javac 18, inner classes that never use the outer instance no longer get the <code>this$0</code> field, but don't rely on that.	ch02 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch02-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q11</div>🟡 How do you make a class immutable? What about a record holding a <code>List</code>?	Make it final (or give it a private constructor), make all fields <code>private final</code>, add no mutators, defensively copy mutable inputs and outputs (copy <i>before</i> validating), and don't let <code>this</code> escape the constructor. Records give you the final fields but only shallow immutability, so normalise in the compact constructor: <code>members = List.copyOf(members);</code>.	ch02 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch02-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q12</div>🔴 What problems do records solve, and when would you NOT use one?	They remove boilerplate for transparent, immutable data carriers and get <code>equals</code>, <code>hashCode</code> and <code>toString</code> right. Deserialization goes through the canonical constructor, so invariants hold. Don't use them for JPA entities (mutable, proxied, need no-arg constructors), for types whose representation should be hidden (records expose their state as their API), or for anything that needs inheritance.	ch02 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch02-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q13</div>🔴 What do sealed types give you that enums and plain interfaces don't?	A closed set of <i>types</i>, each with its own state and behaviour, plus compiler-checked exhaustiveness in pattern-matching switches. Adding a variant becomes a compile error in every unhandled switch instead of a production bug. Enums are closed sets of <i>singleton instances</i>. Open interfaces can't be exhaustively matched. Sealed records model algebraic data types (sum of products).	ch02 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch02-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q14</div>🔴 Why is the enum singleton considered the best singleton?	The JVM guarantees one instance per constant, created thread-safely during class initialization. Reflection can't instantiate enums (<code>Cannot reflectively create enum objects</code>). Serialization writes only the name and resolves it with <code>valueOf</code>, so deserialization can't create a duplicate. The downside: it can't extend a class, and it's eagerly initialized when the enum class initializes.	ch02 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch02-q015	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q15</div>🔴 Why shouldn't you persist <code>ordinal()</code>? What's another enum hashing pitfall?	Ordinal is declaration order. Insert or reorder constants and stored values silently change meaning, so persist the name (<code>EnumType.STRING</code>) or an explicit code field. Enum <code>hashCode</code> is the identity hash, which differs between runs, so <code>HashMap</code>/<code>HashSet</code> iteration order over enum keys isn't stable across JVM restarts. Use <code>EnumMap</code> and <code>EnumSet</code> (also much faster).	ch02 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch02-q016	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q16</div>🟡 Explain Liskov substitution with an example that isn't Rectangle/Square.	Say <code>ReadOnlyList extends ArrayList</code> and throws <code>UnsupportedOperationException</code> from <code>add</code>. Code that receives an <code>ArrayList</code> and calls <code>add</code> breaks, even though the types check. LSP is about <i>behavioural</i> contracts: preconditions can't be strengthened, postconditions can't be weakened, and invariants must be preserved. The JDK's own <code>List.of()</code> returning a <code>List</code> whose <code>add</code> throws is a deliberate, documented trade-off. The contract marks <code>add</code> as an "optional operation".	ch02 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch02-q017	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q17</div>🟡 Why is <code>clone()</code> considered broken?	<code>Cloneable</code> is a marker that changes the behaviour of the protected <code>Object.clone()</code>. The copy is created without running a constructor (invariants bypassed) and is shallow by default (shared mutable state). It doesn't work with final fields that need deep copies, and it throws a checked exception callers don't want. Use copy constructors or static factories. Arrays are the exception, where <code>arr.clone()</code> is idiomatic.	ch02 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch02-q018	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 02 · OOP &amp; the Object Model · Q18</div>🟣 How would you use the type system to reduce production bugs in a payments domain?	Wrap primitives in value types (<code>Money</code> with a normalised scale and currency, <code>PaymentId</code>, <code>MerchantId</code>) so arguments can't be mixed up. Model states as a sealed hierarchy of records (<code>Authorized</code>, <code>Captured</code>, <code>Refunded</code>, <code>Failed</code>) so invalid combinations can't be represented, and so pattern-matching switches are compiler-checked when a new state is added. Make objects immutable, with state transitions as methods that return new states, so the history stays auditable. Keep entities (identity equality) separate from value objects (structural equality).	ch02 part::I purpose::decisions
jim-ch03-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q1</div>🟢 Why is <code>String</code> immutable in Java?	Security (strings are passed to class loaders, file and network APIs, so changing them after a check would be exploitable), thread safety without synchronization, hash code caching (strings are the most common map keys), and literal pooling, which is only safe because no one can mutate a shared instance.	ch03 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch03-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q2</div>🟢 Difference between <code>==</code> and <code>equals</code> on strings? Give a case where <code>==</code> is true for two separately written strings.	<code>==</code> compares references, and <code>equals</code> compares contents. <code>==</code> is true for two identical literals (both interned), for compile-time constant expressions such as <code>"hel" + "lo"</code>, for <code>final</code> constant variables concatenated with literals, and for <code>intern()</code> results. It's false for <code>new String(...)</code> and for runtime concatenation of non-constant variables.	ch03 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch03-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q3</div>🟡 How many objects does <code>new String("abc")</code> create?	Up to two <code>String</code> objects: the interned literal (if it doesn't already exist when the <code>ldc</code> is resolved) and the new instance. In modern JDKs, the new instance shares the literal's backing <code>byte[]</code>, so there's only one array.	ch03 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch03-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q4</div>🟡 Where does the string pool live, and is it garbage collected?	Since Java 7, interned <code>String</code> objects live on the regular heap. The JVM's string table (a native hash table, sized with <code>-XX:StringTableSize</code>) holds references to them. Unreferenced interned strings are collected like any other objects. Before Java 7, they lived in PermGen, which was a common source of <code>OutOfMemoryError: PermGen space</code>.	ch03 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch03-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q5</div>🔴 What are compact strings?	JEP 254 (Java 9). <code>String</code> stores a <code>byte[]</code> plus a <code>coder</code> byte instead of a <code>char[]</code>. If all characters fit in Latin-1, it uses 1 byte per char, otherwise UTF-16 (2 bytes per char). This roughly halves string memory for Latin-1-heavy workloads, and it's transparent to the API.	ch03 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch03-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q6</div>🔴 How is <code>a + b + c</code> compiled today, and why is <code>+=</code> in a loop still bad?	Since Java 9 (JEP 280), it compiles to one <code>invokedynamic</code> bootstrapped by <code>StringConcatFactory</code>, which builds an exactly sized result, and the strategy can improve with JDK upgrades without recompiling. But that optimises a single expression. In a loop, each iteration creates a new intermediate string and copies everything accumulated so far, which is O(n²). Use a presized <code>StringBuilder</code> or <code>String.join</code>/<code>Collectors.joining</code>.	ch03 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch03-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q7</div>🟡 <code>StringBuilder</code> vs <code>StringBuffer</code>?	Same API. <code>StringBuffer</code> synchronizes every method. That makes individual calls thread-safe but not <i>sequences</i> of calls, so it rarely gives the atomicity people expect. Use <code>StringBuilder</code> with thread confinement. <code>StringBuffer</code> is legacy.	ch03 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch03-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q8</div>🔴 What was the <code>substring</code> memory leak?	Before Java 7u6, <code>substring</code> shared the parent's <code>char[]</code>, using an offset and a count. Keeping a small substring of a huge string retained the whole array. The JDK changed <code>substring</code> to copy, trading O(1) for O(n) to remove the hidden retention. The same class of problem still exists with views such as <code>subList</code> and <code>ByteBuffer.slice()</code>.	ch03 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch03-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q9</div>🟡 What does <code>"a.b.c".split(".")</code> return?	An empty array. <code>split</code> takes a regex, and <code>.</code> matches any character, so every character is a delimiter, every token is empty, and trailing empty strings are removed. Use <code>split("\\.")</code> or <code>Pattern.quote(".")</code>. Also remember that <code>split(",", -1)</code> keeps trailing empties and <code>"".split(",")</code> returns <code>[""]</code>.	ch03 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch03-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q10</div>🔴 Explain the "Turkish I" problem.	<code>toLowerCase()</code>/<code>toUpperCase()</code> without arguments use the default locale. In Turkish, <code>I</code> lowercases to dotless <code>ı</code> and <code>i</code> uppercases to dotted <code>İ</code>, so <code>"TITLE".toLowerCase().equals("title")</code> is false on a Turkish-locale server. This breaks header parsing, enum lookups and config keys. Always pass <code>Locale.ROOT</code> for machine-readable text.	ch03 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch03-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q11</div>🔴 What is ReDoS and how do you prevent it in Java?	<code>java.util.regex</code> backtracks, so ambiguous patterns can take exponential time on crafted inputs. Modern JDKs short-circuit many classic nested-quantifier cases, but bounded repetitions like <code>^(\w+\s?){1,100}$</code> still blow up (over a minute on about 10 words on JDK 21). Prevent it by writing unambiguous patterns, using possessive quantifiers or atomic groups, capping input length, using a linear-time engine (RE2/J) for untrusted patterns, and adding a timeout via a deadline-checking <code>CharSequence</code> wrapper.	ch03 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch03-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q12</div>🟡 Why should you specify a charset when converting bytes ↔ strings? Did Java 18 change this?	Without one, the platform default is used, which historically differed by OS and locale and caused mojibake. Java 18 (JEP 400) made UTF-8 the default everywhere, but being explicit (<code>StandardCharsets.UTF_8</code>) documents intent and protects code that runs on older JVMs. Also, decoding is lenient by default (malformed input becomes U+FFFD). Use a <code>CharsetDecoder</code> with <code>REPORT</code> when corruption must fail.	ch03 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch03-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q13</div>🔴 Should you call <code>intern()</code> to save memory?	Rarely. The string table is a fixed-size native structure, and interning large numbers of dynamic values adds contention and GC work. Prefer GC string deduplication (<code>-XX:+UseStringDeduplication</code>, available for all major collectors since JDK 18) or an application-level, bounded interner for a known set of keys. First confirm with a heap histogram that duplicate strings really are the problem.	ch03 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch03-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q14</div>🔴 Why do some security APIs use <code>char[]</code> for passwords instead of <code>String</code>?	A <code>String</code> can't be cleared. It stays in memory until GC (and possibly in the string table), and it can show up in heap dumps. A <code>char[]</code> can be overwritten right after use. In practice, frameworks often hand you a <code>String</code> anyway, so also keep secrets out of logs, keep their lifetimes short, and treat heap dumps as sensitive.	ch03 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch03-q015	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 03 · Strings · Q15</div>🟣 A service's heap is dominated by <code>byte[]</code> and <code>String</code>. How do you approach it?	Take a class histogram, then a heap dump, and in MAT check the duplicate-strings report and the dominator tree for what retains them. Typical root causes: caching whole payloads instead of parsed fields, unbounded caches keyed by long concatenated strings, loading large files whole, and many duplicates of a small set of values. Fixes, in order: change what's retained (architecture), deduplicate a bounded value set, turn on GC string deduplication, and only then tune the heap. Measure before and after with the same workload.	ch03 part::I purpose::decisions
jim-ch04-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q1</div>🟢 Checked vs unchecked exceptions: what's the difference and when do you use each?	Checked exceptions (subclasses of <code>Exception</code> but not <code>RuntimeException</code>) must be caught or declared, and the compiler enforces it. Unchecked exceptions (<code>RuntimeException</code> and <code>Error</code>) don't need to be. The rule of thumb is checked for recoverable conditions the immediate caller can act on, unchecked for programming errors. Modern practice uses checked exceptions sparingly because they don't compose with lambdas and leak implementation details across layers. Expected business outcomes are often better modelled as return types.	ch04 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch04-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q2</div>🟡 Does <code>finally</code> always execute?	It runs on every normal or abrupt exit from the <code>try</code> (return, break, continue, exception). It doesn't run if <code>System.exit</code>/<code>Runtime.halt</code> is called, if the JVM crashes or is killed, if the thread is a daemon and the JVM exits, or if the <code>try</code> never completes. A <code>return</code> in <code>finally</code> overrides the try's return value and swallows any in-flight exception.	ch04 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch04-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q3</div>🟡 What does <code>int f(){ int x=1; try { return x; } finally { x = 2; } }</code> return?	<code>1</code>. The return value is evaluated and stored before <code>finally</code> runs, and changing the local afterwards doesn't affect it. If the method returned a mutable <i>object</i>, mutations made in <code>finally</code> would be visible, because the saved value is the reference.	ch04 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch04-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q4</div>🟡 What are suppressed exceptions?	When the body of a try-with-resources throws and <code>close()</code> throws too, the body's exception stays primary and the close exception is attached via <code>addSuppressed</code>, retrievable with <code>getSuppressed()</code>. Before Java 7, a hand-written <code>finally { close(); }</code> let the close exception <i>replace</i> the original and hide the real cause. Resources close in reverse order of declaration.	ch04 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch04-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q5</div>🔴 <code>ClassNotFoundException</code> vs <code>NoClassDefFoundError</code>?	<code>ClassNotFoundException</code> is checked and is thrown by explicit dynamic loading (<code>Class.forName</code>, <code>ClassLoader.loadClass</code>) when the class can't be found. <code>NoClassDefFoundError</code> is an <code>Error</code> thrown when the JVM resolves a reference to a class that was present at compile time but can't be defined at runtime: either the jar is missing, or, very commonly, the class's static initialization failed earlier (the first attempt throws <code>ExceptionInInitializerError</code>, and later ones throw <code>NoClassDefFoundError: Could not initialize class X</code>).	ch04 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch04-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q6</div>🔴 Why are exceptions expensive and how can you make them cheaper?	Most of the cost is in construction: <code>fillInStackTrace</code> walks the whole stack, and building <code>StackTraceElement</code>s later costs more. For high-frequency expected failures, use the 4-argument constructor with <code>writableStackTrace=false</code> (stackless exceptions), or avoid exceptions with return types. When the throw and the catch are inlined into the same compiled method, the JIT may reduce the throw to a jump, but you can't rely on that.	ch04 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch04-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q7</div>🔴 Your logs show thousands of <code>NullPointerException</code> lines with no stack trace. Why?	<code>-XX:+OmitStackTraceInFastThrow</code> (on by default): after the same implicit exception is thrown repeatedly from JIT-compiled code at one site, HotSpot throws a preallocated exception with no stack trace or message. Find the earliest occurrences in the logs (they still have full traces) or run with <code>-XX:-OmitStackTraceInFastThrow</code>.	ch04 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch04-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q8</div>🟡 What happens to an exception thrown inside a task passed to <code>ExecutorService.submit()</code>?	It's captured in the returned <code>Future</code>. Nothing is logged, and the exception only resurfaces as an <code>ExecutionException</code> when someone calls <code>get()</code>. With <code>execute()</code>, the exception kills the worker thread and reaches the <code>UncaughtExceptionHandler</code> (the pool replaces the worker). Fire-and-forget <code>submit</code> is a classic source of silent failures.	ch04 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch04-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q9</div>🟡 How should you handle <code>InterruptedException</code>?	Treat it as a cancellation request. Either propagate it (declare <code>throws InterruptedException</code>) or restore the interrupt flag with <code>Thread.currentThread().interrupt()</code> and stop the current work promptly. Swallowing it breaks thread-pool shutdown and task cancellation.	ch04 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch04-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q10</div>🟡 How do you deal with checked exceptions in streams?	Wrap them in an appropriate unchecked exception inside the lambda (<code>UncheckedIOException</code>), use a small adapter that turns a throwing function into a <code>Function</code>, or model per-element failures as data (a sealed <code>Result</code>) when partial failure is expected. Avoid sneaky-throw hacks that hide checked exceptions from the type system.	ch04 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch04-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q11</div>🔴 What's wrong with <code>catch (Exception e) { log.error("failed", e); throw e; }</code> in every layer?	The same failure gets logged many times with full traces, which creates noise, inflates log costs, and makes alert deduplication harder. It also adds no context. Instead, translate exceptions at layer boundaries (adding context and preserving the cause), and log exactly once, at the boundary that actually handles the error (HTTP handler, message consumer, job runner).	ch04 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch04-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q12</div>🔴 Should you catch <code>OutOfMemoryError</code>?	Almost never. When it's thrown, other threads may have died mid-operation, leaving locks held or data structures inconsistent. Prefer fail-fast: <code>-XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError</code> plus <code>-XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError</code>, with an orchestrator restarting the process. A narrow exception is a deliberately bounded allocation where an OOM is expected and recoverable (for example, trying to allocate a huge optional buffer), and even that is better solved by sizing checks.	ch04 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch04-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q13</div>🟣 Design the error-handling approach for a payments API.	Use a sealed result type for business outcomes (approved, declined with a reason, pending), so callers have to handle them. Use unchecked exceptions with stable codes and a <code>retryable</code> flag for technical failures. Map them to RFC 9457 Problem Details once, at the API boundary. Require idempotency keys so clients can retry safely. Retry only at one layer, with backoff, jitter and a budget. Log once with structured fields and trace IDs, and put no PII in messages. Track error rates by code against the SLOs, and page on bugs (5xx) and SLO burn, not on client errors.	ch04 part::I purpose::decisions
jim-ch04-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 04 · Exceptions &amp; Error Handling · Q14</div>🔴 What does javac generate for a <code>finally</code> block?	It duplicates the finally code inline on every normal exit path (each <code>return</code>, <code>break</code> or fall-through), and adds an exception-table handler that catches any <code>Throwable</code>, runs another copy, and rethrows. The old <code>jsr</code>/<code>ret</code> subroutine instructions are no longer used and aren't allowed in modern class files. Large finally blocks therefore inflate method size, which can affect JIT inlining decisions.	ch04 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch05-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q1</div>🟢 What is type erasure?	The compiler checks generic types, then erases them: it replaces type parameters with their first bound (or <code>Object</code>), inserts casts at use sites, and generates bridge methods. At runtime, <code>List&lt;String&gt;</code> and <code>List&lt;Integer&gt;</code> are both just <code>List</code>. This kept binary compatibility with pre-Java-5 code and JVMs.	ch05 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch05-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q2</div>🟡 Why isn't <code>List&lt;Integer&gt;</code> a subtype of <code>List&lt;Number&gt;</code>?	If it were, you could add a <code>Double</code> through the <code>List&lt;Number&gt;</code> reference into what is really a <code>List&lt;Integer&gt;</code>, which breaks type safety. Generics are invariant to prevent this at compile time. Use <code>List&lt;? extends Number&gt;</code> when you only need to read Numbers.	ch05 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch05-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q3</div>🟡 Explain PECS with a JDK example.	Producer-Extends, Consumer-Super. If a parameter <i>produces</i> <code>T</code>s that you read, use <code>? extends T</code>. If it <i>consumes</i> <code>T</code>s that you write, use <code>? super T</code>. <code>Collections.copy(List&lt;? super T&gt; dest, List&lt;? extends T&gt; src)</code>: <code>src</code> produces elements and <code>dest</code> consumes them. Comparators and consumers are almost always <code>? super T</code>.	ch05 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch05-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q4</div>🔴 What is a bridge method and when does javac generate one?	A synthetic method that keeps overriding and covariant returns working after erasure. When <code>IntNode extends Node&lt;Integer&gt;</code> overrides <code>setData(Integer)</code>, the erased superclass method is <code>setData(Object)</code>, so javac generates <code>setData(Object)</code> in <code>IntNode</code> that casts and delegates. Calling it through a raw type with the wrong argument fails with <code>ClassCastException</code> inside the bridge.	ch05 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch05-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q5</div>🔴 If generics are erased, how does Jackson deserialize into <code>List&lt;User&gt;</code>?	With a super type token: <code>new TypeReference&lt;List&lt;User&gt;&gt;() {}</code> creates an anonymous subclass. The class file records its generic superclass in the <code>Signature</code> attribute, and erasure doesn't remove that. Jackson calls <code>getClass().getGenericSuperclass()</code>, casts to <code>ParameterizedType</code>, and reads the actual type argument <code>List&lt;User&gt;</code>.	ch05 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch05-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q6</div>🔴 Why can't you create <code>new T[]</code> or <code>new List&lt;String&gt;[10]</code>?	Arrays are reified and covariant: they know and check their element type at runtime. Generic types are erased, so the runtime can't enforce <code>List&lt;String&gt;</code>. Allowing generic array creation would let heap pollution pass every array store check. Workarounds: <code>IntFunction&lt;T[]&gt;</code> (<code>String[]::new</code>), <code>Array.newInstance</code> with a <code>Class&lt;T&gt;</code>, or use lists instead of arrays.	ch05 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch05-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q7</div>🔴 What is heap pollution? Give an example with varargs.	It's when a variable of a parameterized type refers to an object of a different parameterization. Example: <code>static void m(List&lt;String&gt;... ls) { Object[] a = ls; a[0] = List.of(42); String s = ls[0].get(0); }</code> throws <code>ClassCastException</code> later. <code>@SafeVarargs</code> is the author's promise not to write to or expose the varargs array.	ch05 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch05-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q8</div>🔴 Why is the signature of <code>Collections.max</code> <code>&lt;T extends Object &amp; Comparable&lt;? super T&gt;&gt;</code>?	<code>Object &amp;</code> makes the erasure of <code>T</code> be <code>Object</code>, preserving the pre-generics binary signature <code>Object max(Collection)</code>. <code>Comparable&lt;? super T&gt;</code> allows types whose comparability is declared on a supertype, such as <code>LocalDate implements Comparable&lt;ChronoLocalDate&gt;</code>.	ch05 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch05-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q9</div>🔴 What is wildcard capture and how do you work around capture errors?	The compiler gives each <code>?</code> a fresh unknown type (<code>CAP#1</code>), so it can't prove that a value read from a <code>List&lt;?&gt;</code> can be written back. Fix it with a private generic helper, <code>&lt;T&gt; void helper(List&lt;T&gt; l)</code>, that names the captured type. The public API keeps the clean wildcard signature.	ch05 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch05-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q10</div>🟡 <code>List&lt;?&gt;</code> vs <code>List&lt;Object&gt;</code> vs raw <code>List</code>?	<code>List&lt;?&gt;</code> is a list of some unknown specific type: type-safe, read-only except for <code>null</code>, and it accepts any <code>List&lt;X&gt;</code>. <code>List&lt;Object&gt;</code> holds anything, but <code>List&lt;String&gt;</code> isn't assignable to it. Raw <code>List</code> turns off generic checks entirely (unchecked warnings) and exists only for legacy compatibility.	ch05 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch05-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q11</div>🔴 Explain the "simulated self type" pattern and where it's used.	An abstract builder declared as <code>Builder&lt;T extends Builder&lt;T&gt;&gt;</code> with <code>abstract T self()</code>. Base-class fluent methods return <code>T</code>, which is the concrete subclass's builder type, so chained calls keep access to subclass methods. Used for builder hierarchies, fluent assertion libraries (AssertJ's <code>SELF</code> type parameter) and <code>Enum&lt;E extends Enum&lt;E&gt;&gt;</code>.	ch05 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch05-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 05 · Generics · Q12</div>🟣 What's your stance on primitive-heavy generic collections in a high-throughput service?	Boxing costs memory (object headers plus references) and adds GC pressure and pointer-chasing cache misses. For hot paths or large datasets (millions of entries), use primitive specialisations (<code>int[]</code>, <code>IntStream</code>, fastutil, Eclipse Collections, Agrona) behind a domain interface, and measure with JOL/JFR before and after. Valhalla will reduce the need over time, but today it's an architectural decision that affects heap sizing and GC behaviour.	ch05 part::I purpose::decisions
jim-ch06-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q1</div>🟢 How does <code>HashMap.get()</code> work?	It computes <code>hash(key)</code> (<code>hashCode()</code> XOR its upper 16 bits), picks the bucket with <code>(n-1) &amp; hash</code>, then checks the first node: cached hash equal, and key <code>==</code> or <code>equals</code>. On a miss, it walks the chain (O(chain length)) or searches the tree bin (O(log n)). A null key always maps to bucket 0.	ch06 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch06-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q2</div>🟡 Why is <code>HashMap</code>'s capacity always a power of two?	So the bucket index can be computed with a bitwise AND (<code>hash &amp; (n-1)</code>) instead of a slower modulo, and so resizing can split each bucket into "stay at i" or "move to i + oldCap" by testing a single bit (<code>hash &amp; oldCap</code>) without rehashing. The downside is that only the low bits pick the bucket, which is why <code>hash()</code> XORs the high 16 bits into the low ones.	ch06 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch06-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q3</div>🔴 Explain treeification, including the numbers.	When a bucket's chain reaches 8 nodes (<code>TREEIFY_THRESHOLD</code>) <i>and</i> the table has at least 64 buckets (<code>MIN_TREEIFY_CAPACITY</code>), the chain becomes a red-black tree of <code>TreeNode</code>s. If the table is smaller than 64, the map resizes instead. During a resize split, a tree with ≤ 6 nodes (<code>UNTREEIFY_THRESHOLD</code>) goes back to a list. The gap is hysteresis. Trees are ordered by hash, then by <code>compareTo</code> for <code>Comparable</code> keys of the same class, then by a tie-breaker. With a good hash, a chain of 8 has a probability of about 6 × 10⁻⁸, so trees mostly appear with bad hash functions or collision attacks.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q4</div>🔴 Why was <code>HashMap</code> in Java 7 dangerous under concurrent modification, and is Java 8 safe?	Java 7 inserted at the head and reversed chains during resize. Two threads resizing concurrently could link a chain into a cycle, after which <code>get</code> looped forever at 100% CPU. Java 8 uses tail insertion and order-preserving lo/hi splits, which removes the infinite loop, but <code>HashMap</code> is still not thread-safe: concurrent puts can lose entries, corrupt <code>size</code> and leave readers seeing inconsistent state. Use <code>ConcurrentHashMap</code>.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q5</div>🔴 How does <code>ConcurrentHashMap</code> achieve thread safety in Java 8+?	Lock-free reads (volatile/acquire reads of slots and node fields). CAS to insert into an empty bucket. <code>synchronized</code> on the bucket's head node for non-empty buckets, so contention is per bucket. Tree bins for long chains. Cooperative multi-threaded resizing with <code>ForwardingNode</code>s that redirect readers to the new table. Striped counters (<code>CounterCell</code>s) for size. Compound operations like <code>compute</code>, <code>merge</code> and <code>computeIfAbsent</code> are atomic per key.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q6</div>🟡 Why doesn't <code>ConcurrentHashMap</code> allow nulls?	Because in a concurrent context <code>get(k) == null</code> is ambiguous (absent, or mapped to null?), and you can't disambiguate with a follow-up <code>containsKey</code>: the map may change between the two calls. Banning nulls makes <code>null</code> an unambiguous "absent" signal for <code>get</code>, <code>putIfAbsent</code>, <code>compute</code> and friends.	ch06 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch06-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q7</div>🔴 What are the pitfalls of <code>computeIfAbsent</code> on <code>ConcurrentHashMap</code>?	The mapping function runs while holding the bucket lock. Long or blocking functions stall every other writer to that bucket (and readers of tree bins in some paths), so never do I/O inside it. It must not update other keys of the same map: that used to hang in Java 8 and throws <code>IllegalStateException: Recursive update</code> since Java 9. In Java 8 it locked even when the key was present, which Java 9 fixed with a fast path.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q8</div>🟡 What does fail-fast mean, and how do you remove elements while iterating?	Iterators compare their <code>expectedModCount</code> against the collection's <code>modCount</code> and throw <code>ConcurrentModificationException</code> on mismatch. It's a best-effort bug detector, not a thread-safety mechanism. Remove with <code>Iterator.remove()</code>, <code>Collection.removeIf</code>, or <code>entrySet().removeIf</code> for maps. Concurrent collections use weakly consistent or snapshot iterators that never throw CME.	ch06 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch06-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q9</div>🔴 Removing an element inside a for-each over an <code>ArrayList</code> sometimes doesn't throw. When and why?	When you remove the second-to-last element. After <code>next()</code> returns it, <code>cursor == size - 1</code>. The removal shrinks <code>size</code> by one, so <code>hasNext()</code> (<code>cursor != size</code>) returns false and the loop ends before <code>next()</code> can check <code>modCount</code>. No exception, but the last element is silently skipped.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q10</div>🟡 <code>List.of</code> vs <code>Collections.unmodifiableList</code> vs <code>Arrays.asList</code>?	<code>List.of</code> is truly immutable, rejects nulls (even <code>contains(null)</code> throws NPE), and is compact. <code>unmodifiableList</code> is a read-only <i>view</i>, so changes to the backing list show through. <code>Arrays.asList</code> is fixed-size and write-through to the array: <code>set</code> works, <code>add</code>/<code>remove</code> throw. Also, <code>Stream.toList()</code> is unmodifiable and allows nulls, while <code>Collectors.toList()</code> makes no mutability promise.	ch06 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch06-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q11</div>🟡 How would you implement an LRU cache in Java?	For a single thread: <code>LinkedHashMap</code> with <code>accessOrder=true</code> and an overridden <code>removeEldestEntry</code> that returns <code>size() &gt; max</code>. Note that <code>get</code> mutates the structure in access-order mode, so concurrent use needs external locking. For production: Caffeine (bounded, concurrent, W-TinyLFU eviction, expiry, stats). In an interview from scratch: a <code>HashMap&lt;K, Node&gt;</code> plus a doubly linked list with sentinel nodes, giving O(1) get/put (Chapter 26).	ch06 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch06-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q12</div>🟡 <code>HashMap</code> vs <code>TreeMap</code> vs <code>LinkedHashMap</code>?	<code>HashMap</code>: O(1) average, no order. <code>LinkedHashMap</code>: O(1), predictable insertion or access order, slightly more memory. <code>TreeMap</code>: O(log n), sorted, with navigation and range-view APIs (<code>floorKey</code>, <code>subMap</code>), and it uses <code>compareTo</code> rather than <code>equals</code> for key equality.	ch06 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch06-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q13</div>🔴 Why does <code>new HashMap&lt;&gt;(100)</code> still resize?	The constructor argument is the initial <i>capacity</i>, rounded up to a power of two (128), not the number of entries. The threshold is 128 × 0.75 = 96, so the 97th insertion triggers a resize. Use <code>HashMap.newHashMap(100)</code> (Java 19+), or <code>ceil(n / 0.75)</code> as the capacity.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q14</div>🔴 A <code>TreeSet</code> and a <code>HashSet</code> containing the "same" elements report different sizes. How?	<code>TreeSet</code> treats elements as equal when <code>compareTo</code>/<code>compare</code> returns 0. <code>HashSet</code> uses <code>equals</code>/<code>hashCode</code>. If the two disagree, the sets disagree: <code>BigDecimal("2.0")</code> vs <code>"2.00"</code> (compare equal, not <code>equals</code>), or a case-insensitive comparator. That's why comparators <i>should</i> be consistent with <code>equals</code>.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q015	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q15</div>🟡 Is <code>PriorityQueue</code>'s iterator sorted?	No. It walks the heap array in level order, which is only partially ordered. Only repeated <code>poll()</code> yields priority order. Also: <code>offer</code>/<code>poll</code> are O(log n), <code>peek</code> is O(1), <code>remove(Object)</code> is O(n), and building from a collection is O(n) heapify.	ch06 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch06-q016	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q16</div>🔴 What's wrong with <code>(a, b) -&gt; a - b</code> as a comparator?	Integer overflow: when <code>a</code> is very negative and <code>b</code> is positive (or the reverse), the subtraction wraps around and returns the wrong sign, which breaks transitivity. TimSort may then throw "Comparison method violates its general contract!" Use <code>Integer.compare</code> or <code>Comparator.naturalOrder()</code>/<code>comparingInt</code>.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q017	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q17</div>🔴 When would you use <code>IdentityHashMap</code> or <code>WeakHashMap</code>?	<code>IdentityHashMap</code> (open addressing, <code>==</code>, identity hash) for identity-based bookkeeping: visited sets in graph traversal, serialization or deep copy, where equal-but-distinct objects must be tracked separately. <code>WeakHashMap</code> to attach metadata to objects you don't control without keeping them alive. But values are strong (a value referencing its key leaks), and entries vanish at GC rather than under memory pressure, so it isn't a cache.	ch06 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch06-q018	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 06 · Collections Framework · Q18</div>🟣 A service holds a 20M-entry <code>HashMap&lt;Long, Long&gt;</code> and suffers long GC pauses. What would you do?	Quantify it first: roughly 85 bytes per entry, so about 1.7 GB across tens of millions of objects, with expensive marking and a resize that allocates a giant new table. Options, roughly in order: presize to avoid resize spikes; switch to a primitive map (fastutil <code>Long2LongOpenHashMap</code>, about 16–20 bytes per entry with no per-entry objects); shard or partition; move to an off-heap or memory-mapped store; or question whether it needs to be in-process at all (an external cache or store). Then re-evaluate GC choice and settings (G1 region size, ZGC) with measurements, not guesses.	ch06 part::I purpose::decisions
jim-ch07-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q1</div>🟢 What is a functional interface? Is <code>Comparator</code> one, given that it has two abstract methods?	An interface with exactly one abstract method (SAM), which can be the target of a lambda or a method reference. <code>Comparator</code> declares <code>compare</code> and <code>equals</code>, but <code>equals</code> matches a public <code>Object</code> method, so it doesn't count. Default and static methods don't count either. <code>@FunctionalInterface</code> makes the compiler enforce the rule.	ch07 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch07-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q2</div>🔴 How is a lambda compiled and created at runtime?	javac moves the body into a private synthetic method (<code>lambda$enclosing$N</code>, static if it doesn't use <code>this</code>) and emits an <code>invokedynamic</code> at the creation site. On first execution, the bootstrap method <code>LambdaMetafactory.metafactory</code> spins a hidden class implementing the functional interface that forwards to the synthetic method, and links the call site. Non-capturing lambdas typically yield a cached singleton. Capturing ones allocate an instance holding the captured values.	ch07 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch07-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q3</div>🟡 Why must captured local variables be effectively final?	Lambdas capture values, not variables. They may run later or on another thread, after the stack frame has gone. Allowing mutation would require heap-allocating the variable and synchronizing access, which reintroduces data races. The design pushes you towards expressing accumulation as a reduction instead of mutating shared state.	ch07 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch07-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q4</div>🟡 What's the difference between <code>name::length</code> and <code>() -&gt; name.length()</code> when <code>name</code> is null?	A bound method reference evaluates its receiver at creation time, so <code>name::length</code> throws NPE immediately. The lambda evaluates <code>name</code> each time it's invoked, so the NPE happens only when it's called. The same timing difference applies to expressions like <code>service.getClient()::send</code>.	ch07 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch07-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q5</div>🟡 <code>orElse</code> vs <code>orElseGet</code>?	<code>orElse(x)</code> takes a value, so the argument is always evaluated, even when the Optional is present. <code>orElseGet(supplier)</code> evaluates lazily, only when empty. Use <code>orElseGet</code> whenever computing the default is expensive or has side effects (a database call, object creation, logging).	ch07 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch07-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q6</div>🟡 Where should you NOT use <code>Optional</code>?	As fields (not serializable, adds allocation, can still be null), as method parameters (forces callers to wrap, and creates three states), wrapping collections (use empty collections), and in tight hot loops (allocation). It's designed as a return type for "maybe no result".	ch07 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch07-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q7</div>🔴 Explain laziness and vertical processing in streams. Why can <code>peek</code> not run at all?	Intermediate operations only build pipeline stages. The terminal operation creates a chain of <code>Sink</code>s and pushes each element through every stateless stage before taking the next, which is why short-circuiting operations can stop early. Stateful operations (<code>sorted</code>, <code>distinct</code>) buffer. Since Java 9, <code>count()</code> on a <code>SIZED</code> stream with no size-changing stages returns the size directly without traversing, so <code>peek</code> never runs. Never rely on <code>peek</code> for side effects.	ch07 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch07-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q8</div>🔴 When do parallel streams help, and what are the risks?	They help for large N × cost-per-element (the NQ &gt; 10,000 rule of thumb) with splittable sources (arrays, ArrayList, ranges), CPU-bound stateless operations and cheap combining. Risks: overhead on small data, poor splitting (LinkedList, iterate, I/O-backed sources), ordering constraints, shared mutable state, and above all <b>blocking calls on the shared common pool</b>, which starves other parallel streams and default-async <code>CompletableFuture</code>s across the whole JVM.	ch07 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch07-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q9</div>🟡 What can go wrong with <code>Collectors.toMap</code>?	Duplicate keys throw <code>IllegalStateException</code> unless you provide a merge function. Null values throw NPE. The result is a <code>HashMap</code> with no ordering guarantee unless you pass a map supplier (<code>LinkedHashMap::new</code>, <code>TreeMap::new</code>). Use the 4-argument overload when any of these matter.	ch07 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch07-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q10</div>🔴 <code>reduce</code> vs <code>collect</code>: why is <code>reduce(new ArrayList&lt;&gt;(), ...)</code> wrong?	<code>reduce</code> is designed for immutable values: the identity is shared and must be a true identity for an associative combiner. Mutating an <code>ArrayList</code> identity means every parallel chunk writes into the same list, causing races, duplicates and exceptions. <code>collect</code> is mutable reduction: the supplier creates a fresh container per chunk, and the combiner merges them.	ch07 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch07-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q11</div>🔴 What are Stream Gatherers and why were they added?	Java 24 (JEP 485) added <code>Stream.gather(Gatherer)</code> for user-defined intermediate operations. Previously you couldn't add custom ones like windowing, scanning or distinct-by-key without awkward collector tricks. A gatherer has an initializer, an integrator (it can emit 0..n elements and short-circuit), an optional combiner and a finisher. Built-ins include <code>windowFixed</code>, <code>windowSliding</code>, <code>fold</code>, <code>scan</code> and <code>mapConcurrent</code> (bounded concurrency on virtual threads, order-preserving).	ch07 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch07-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q12</div>🟡 Why should <code>Files.lines()</code> be used in try-with-resources?	The stream holds an open file handle until it's closed, and streams don't close themselves when the terminal operation completes. In long-running services, forgetting this leaks file descriptors until "Too many open files". The same applies to <code>Files.list</code>, <code>walk</code> and <code>find</code>.	ch07 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch07-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 07 · Lambdas, Streams &amp; Functional Java · Q13</div>🟣 Your team wants to "use parallel streams to speed up" an endpoint that calls three services per item. What do you say?	No. That's blocking I/O on the JVM-wide common pool, which will starve other workloads and gives uncontrolled concurrency against downstream services. Use explicit concurrency with limits: virtual threads with structured concurrency, <code>Gatherers.mapConcurrent(n, …)</code>, or <code>CompletableFuture</code>s on a dedicated bounded executor. Add timeouts, per-dependency bulkheads and metrics. Then measure whether latency actually improves and whether the downstream services can handle the extra concurrency.	ch07 part::I purpose::decisions
jim-ch08-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q1</div>🟢 What were the most important features of Java 8?	Lambdas and method references, the Streams API, default and static interface methods, <code>Optional</code>, the <code>java.time</code> API, <code>CompletableFuture</code>, and the replacement of PermGen with Metaspace. Together they enabled a functional style and fixed the long-standing date/time and async problems.	ch08 part::I purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch08-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q2</div>🟡 <code>Instant</code> vs <code>LocalDateTime</code> vs <code>ZonedDateTime</code>: when do you use each?	<code>Instant</code> is a point on the UTC timeline: use it for event timestamps and storage. <code>LocalDateTime</code> is a wall-clock value with no zone: use it for things like store hours. It can't identify a moment and is ambiguous during DST changes. <code>ZonedDateTime</code> combines local time with zone rules, for user-facing scheduling and DST-aware arithmetic. For <i>future</i> local events, store <code>LocalDateTime</code> + <code>ZoneId</code> rather than an <code>Instant</code>, because time-zone rules can change before the event.	ch08 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch08-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q3</div>🟡 What's the difference between <code>Duration</code> and <code>Period</code>?	<code>Duration</code> is exact elapsed time (seconds and nanos). <code>Period</code> is a calendar amount (years, months, days). Across a DST transition, <code>zdt.plus(Period.ofDays(1))</code> keeps the wall-clock time, while <code>zdt.plus(Duration.ofDays(1))</code> adds exactly 24 hours and the wall-clock time shifts by an hour.	ch08 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch08-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q4</div>🟡 What broke for most teams moving from Java 8 to 11, and from 11 to 17?	8 → 11: the Java EE and CORBA modules (JAXB, JAX-WS, <code>javax.annotation</code>) were removed from the JDK, so applications failed with <code>ClassNotFoundException</code>/<code>NoClassDefFoundError</code> until they added explicit dependencies. 11 → 17: JDK internals became strictly encapsulated (JEP 403), so libraries doing deep reflection into <code>java.*</code>/<code>sun.*</code> failed with <code>InaccessibleObjectException</code> until they were upgraded or given <code>--add-opens</code>.	ch08 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch08-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q5</div>🔴 Explain pattern matching for <code>switch</code>: exhaustiveness, dominance, guards, null.	Cases can be type patterns (<code>case Circle c</code>) and record patterns (<code>case Point(var x, var y)</code>, nestable), optionally refined with <code>when</code> guards. Switches using patterns must be exhaustive. Over sealed hierarchies the compiler verifies coverage without a <code>default</code>, and guarded cases don't count towards coverage. Cases are checked top-down, and a case dominated by an earlier, more general one won't compile. <code>null</code> throws NPE unless there's an explicit <code>case null</code>. Since Java 22, <code>_</code> marks unnamed patterns and variables.	ch08 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch08-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q6</div>🔴 What problem do scoped values solve that <code>ThreadLocal</code> doesn't?	<code>ThreadLocal</code> is mutable, has unbounded lifetime (it leaks in thread pools unless removed), and copying it to child threads (<code>InheritableThreadLocal</code>) is expensive. That's a bad fit for millions of virtual threads. Scoped values are immutable, bound for a dynamic scope only (automatically unbound when <code>run</code>/<code>call</code> returns), cheap to read, and efficiently inherited by structured-concurrency subtasks. They're final in Java 25.	ch08 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch08-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q7</div>🔴 What did JEP 491 (Java 24) change for virtual threads, and why does it matter for adoption?	Before Java 24, a virtual thread blocking inside <code>synchronized</code> (or in <code>Object.wait()</code>) <i>pinned</i> its carrier thread, so legacy code and libraries full of <code>synchronized</code> could exhaust the small carrier pool and deadlock or stall. JEP 491 let virtual threads release the carrier while blocked on monitors. Java 25 is the first LTS with this fix, which makes it the safer target for large-scale virtual-thread adoption. Pinning still happens for native frames and some class-initialization cases.	ch08 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch08-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q8</div>🔴 What are flexible constructor bodies and what bug do they help prevent?	Since Java 25 (JEP 513), a constructor can run statements before <code>super(...)</code>/<code>this(...)</code>, as long as they don't read <code>this</code>. That allows argument validation before delegating, and <i>assigning the subclass's own fields</i> before the superclass constructor runs. That's exactly what's needed when a superclass constructor calls an overridable method that reads subclass state (which previously saw <code>null</code>/0).	ch08 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch08-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q9</div>🟣 What changed in Java 27 that could affect your production dashboards?	Compact object headers are on by default (JEP 534), so heap usage per object drops and GC behaviour shifts. G1 is now the default GC everywhere (JEP 523), so small containers that previously got Serial GC now get G1, with different pause and footprint characteristics. Re-baseline heap, RSS, GC pause and CPU metrics after the upgrade, and check any native code, agents or tools that assumed the old header layout.	ch08 part::I purpose::decisions
jim-ch08-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q10</div>🔵 How would you justify upgrading a fleet from Java 21 to 25?	Risk reduction and capability. JEP 491 removes virtual-thread pinning on <code>synchronized</code>, which unblocks safe Loom adoption. Scoped values, flexible constructors and compact headers (opt-in, with measurable heap savings) arrive, along with Leyden AOT improvements for startup, generational Shenandoah, newer JFR profiling features, and 25 being the current LTS with a long support window. Costs: the Security Manager is gone, plus new warnings for <code>Unsafe</code> and JNI. The plan is toolchain → runtime → language level, by risk tier, with before/after SLO dashboards.	ch08 part::I purpose::execution
jim-ch08-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q11</div>🟡 Are String Templates available in Java?	No. They were previewed in Java 21 and 22 and withdrawn in Java 23 for redesign. For now, use <code>formatted</code>/<code>String.format</code>, <code>MessageFormat</code>, or concatenation. For SQL, use bind parameters. Templates would never have been a replacement for those anyway.	ch08 part::I purpose::practice
jim-ch08-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 08 · Modern Java: 8 → 27 · Q12</div>🔴 What is Project Valhalla and why are wrapper classes "value-based" today?	Valhalla adds value classes: objects without identity that the JVM can flatten into fields and arrays (no headers, no pointers), improving memory density and cache locality, and eventually enabling generics over primitives. To prepare, the JDK designated classes like <code>Integer</code>, <code>Optional</code> and <code>LocalDate</code> as <i>value-based</i>: you shouldn't rely on their identity (<code>==</code>, <code>synchronized</code>, identity hash), and the wrapper constructors are deprecated for removal. Code that synchronizes on an <code>Integer</code> already gets warnings.	ch08 part::I purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q1</div>🟡 What are the JVM's runtime data areas?	Shared: the heap (objects), Metaspace (class metadata, native memory), and the code cache (JIT code). Per thread: the JVM stack (frames with locals and an operand stack), the PC register, and the native method stack. Direct buffers, GC structures and thread stacks use native memory too, which is why process RSS exceeds <code>-Xmx</code>.	ch09 part::II purpose::practice
jim-ch09-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q2</div>🟡 Describe the class loading process.	Loading (find the bytes, create the class) → Linking: verification (type safety via StackMapTable), preparation (statics set to defaults), resolution (symbolic → direct references, lazily) → Initialization (<code>&lt;clinit&gt;</code>, run once under a lock with happens-before guarantees). Classes unload only when their defining loader becomes unreachable.	ch09 part::II purpose::practice
jim-ch09-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q3</div>🔴 What is parent-first delegation and when is it intentionally broken?	A loader asks its parent first and only searches its own sources if the parent fails. That protects core classes from being replaced and keeps one copy of shared classes. It's deliberately broken by servlet containers (child-first, so each webapp can bring its own library versions), by OSGi (a graph of loaders), and indirectly by SPIs that use the thread context class loader, so core code loaded by the bootstrap loader can find application-provided implementations.	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q4</div>🔴 How can "X cannot be cast to X" happen?	Runtime class identity is the pair (name, defining class loader). If the same class is loaded by two different loaders (for example, once by the application loader and once by a webapp or DevTools restart loader), the resulting <code>Class</code> objects are different types, and a cast between them fails. Fix it by making sure the shared class is loaded by a common parent loader.	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q5</div>🔴 Explain tiered compilation.	Methods start in the interpreter with counters. Once hot, C1 compiles them with profiling instrumentation (tier 3). If they stay hot, C2 recompiles them (tier 4) using the profile for speculative optimisations: inlining based on observed receiver types, pruning branches that were never taken, null-check elimination. If an assumption breaks, the code deoptimises back to the interpreter and may be recompiled later. Trivial methods go straight to C1 without profiling (tier 1).	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q6</div>🔴 What is escape analysis and what does it enable?	C2 analyses whether an object can escape the compiled scope (after inlining). If not, it can scalar-replace the object (no heap allocation; fields live in registers), elide locks on it, and coarsen adjacent locks. It depends on inlining and can fail on complex control flow, so verify allocation claims with profiling.	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q7</div>🔴 Why might a performance-critical method never get JIT-compiled?	If its bytecode exceeds <code>HugeMethodLimit</code> (8000 bytes), HotSpot doesn't compile it (<code>DontCompileHugeMethods</code> is true by default). Generated code is the usual culprit. Other causes: the code cache filled up ("CodeCache is full.
