Compiler has been disabled"), compilation failures that got the method excluded, or a method that's simply not hot enough during the measurement window.	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q8</div>🔴 What are compressed oops and what is the 32 GB cliff?	Compressed oops store 64-bit references as 32-bit offsets scaled by the 8-byte alignment, which addresses up to about 32 GB of heap with 4-byte references. At <code>-Xmx32g</code> and above they're disabled, so references become 8 bytes and the effective capacity can <i>drop</i>. Stay below about 31 GB, or go substantially higher, or increase <code>ObjectAlignmentInBytes</code> to 16.	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q9</div>🔴 What are compact object headers, and when are they on?	They're 8-byte headers where the class pointer (compressed to 22 bits) shares the 64-bit word with the identity hash, GC age and lock bits, replacing the classic 12-byte header (8-byte mark word plus 4-byte compressed class pointer). Product feature (opt-in) in Java 25 via JEP 519, default in Java 27 via JEP 534. They save heap (double-digit percentages for object-heavy apps in published measurements) and improve cache density.	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q10</div>🔴 What is a safepoint, and what is time-to-safepoint?	A safepoint is a state where a thread's references are at known locations, so the GC (and some VM operations) can proceed safely. Global safepoints stop all Java threads. Time-to-safepoint is how long the JVM waits for the slowest thread to reach a poll. Long counted loops without polls historically inflated it. Loop strip mining and thread-local handshakes reduce the problem. Diagnose with <code>-Xlog:safepoint</code>.	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q11</div>🔴 Why does Java use <code>invokedynamic</code> for lambdas, string concatenation and records?	It moves implementation strategy from compile time to link time. javac emits a call site plus a bootstrap method reference, and the JDK library decides how to implement it at runtime (<code>LambdaMetafactory</code>, <code>StringConcatFactory</code>, <code>ObjectMethods</code>). That avoids class-file bloat (no inner classes per lambda) and lets future JDKs improve performance without recompiling application code.	ch09 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch09-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q12</div>🟣 Your service takes 3 minutes to reach full throughput after every deploy. What can you do?	First diagnose whether it's JIT warmup (JFR compilation events, CPU on compiler threads), lazy initialization (caches, connection pools), or class loading. Options: warmup traffic or canary ramp-up before full load; more CPU during warmup (CPU limits that allow bursting, so the JIT gets more compiler threads); AppCDS or Leyden AOT cache with recorded profiles (JDK 24/25+) to skip class loading and speed up the move to C2; CRaC snapshots for near-instant warm starts; and eagerly initializing pools and caches at startup. Measure time-to-steady-state p99, not just startup.	ch09 part::II purpose::decisions
jim-ch09-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 09 · JVM Internals · Q13</div>🟣 Native image vs JVM for a new microservice platform?	Native image gives millisecond startup and lower RSS, which suits scale-to-zero and serverless. The costs: long builds, reflection and proxy configuration, library compatibility, lower peak throughput without PGO, and a different debugging and profiling story. A JVM with Leyden AOT caching keeps full JIT peak performance and the standard tooling, with much better startup than before. Decide based on workload shape (short-lived vs long-running), team skills, and framework support (Spring Boot, Quarkus and Micronaut all support both). Pilot before you standardise.	ch09 part::II purpose::decisions
jim-ch10-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q1</div>🟢 How does Java decide an object is garbage?	By reachability, not reference counting. The GC traces from roots (thread stacks, static fields, JNI handles, monitors, JVM internals). Anything not reachable through strong references is garbage, including unreachable cycles.	ch10 part::II purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch10-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q2</div>🟡 Why is object allocation in Java so cheap?	Each thread allocates from its own TLAB (a chunk of Eden) by bumping a pointer: a bounds check and an add, with no locks or free-list search. Copying young collections then reclaim dead objects at zero per-object cost, since only survivors are touched. The expensive part is objects that <i>survive</i> (copying and promotion), not allocation itself.	ch10 part::II purpose::practice
jim-ch10-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q3</div>🟡 Explain the generational heap and the minor GC process.	Young (Eden + two survivor spaces) and Old. New objects go to Eden. A minor GC copies live objects from Eden and the "from" survivor into the "to" survivor, increments their age, and promotes objects older than the tenuring threshold (max 15) or that overflow the survivor space. It's efficient because most objects die young and the cost is proportional to survivors.	ch10 part::II purpose::practice
jim-ch10-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q4</div>🔴 Soft vs weak vs phantom references?	Soft: cleared at the GC's discretion when memory is tight, guaranteed cleared before OOM. Weak: cleared at the next GC once only weakly reachable (<code>WeakHashMap</code>, canonicalizing maps). Phantom: <code>get()</code> always returns null. It's enqueued when the object is about to be reclaimed and is used for cleanup (<code>Cleaner</code>). Soft-reference caches are discouraged in servers because eviction is driven by GC heuristics rather than access patterns.	ch10 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch10-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q5</div>🔴 How does G1 work, and what does "Garbage First" mean?	The heap is divided into equal regions with per-region remembered sets. Young GCs evacuate Eden and Survivor regions. When old occupancy crosses the (adaptive) IHOP, G1 runs concurrent marking with SATB barriers, then remark and cleanup. Mixed GCs then evacuate young regions plus the old regions with the most reclaimable garbage, chosen to fit the pause target. Humongous objects (≥ half a region) get contiguous regions. A full GC is a fallback when evacuation fails.	ch10 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch10-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q6</div>🔴 How does ZGC achieve sub-millisecond pauses on huge heaps?	It does marking, relocation (compaction) and reference updating concurrently. Colored pointers carry GC metadata in the references, and JIT-inserted load barriers check every loaded reference, fixing stale ones in place (self-healing) by consulting forwarding tables. The remaining stop-the-world work (stack scanning, phase transitions) doesn't scale with heap size. The costs are barrier overhead, concurrent CPU use, and allocation stalls if the application outpaces the GC. The generational mode (21+) reduces both.	ch10 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch10-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q7</div>🔴 What is the tri-colour invariant problem and how do SATB and incremental update solve it?	During concurrent marking, the application could store a reference to a white (unvisited) object into a black (finished) object while removing the last grey path to it, so the object would be wrongly freed. Incremental update (a write barrier on store) re-greys the black object. SATB (a pre-write barrier) records the overwritten reference so everything reachable at the start of marking stays live. G1 and Shenandoah use SATB.	ch10 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch10-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q8</div>🔴 What are humongous objects in G1 and why do they matter?	Objects of at least half a region size, allocated directly into contiguous old regions. They can trigger concurrent cycles (<code>G1 Humongous Allocation</code> in logs), waste region tails, need contiguous free space (fragmentation can cause full GCs), and are only reclaimed by marking or eager reclaim. Mitigate by streaming large payloads, reusing large buffers, or increasing <code>G1HeapRegionSize</code>.	ch10 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch10-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q9</div>🔴 A pod is OOM-killed (exit 137) but the heap never exceeded 60% of the limit. What's going on?	The kernel killed the process because the <i>total</i> footprint exceeded the cgroup limit. That includes Metaspace, code cache, thread stacks, direct buffers, GC structures and malloc arenas, not just the heap. Diagnose with Native Memory Tracking compared against the limit. Fix by bounding Metaspace and direct memory, controlling thread counts, lowering <code>MaxRAMPercentage</code>, tuning malloc (<code>MALLOC_ARENA_MAX</code>), or raising the limit.	ch10 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch10-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q10</div>🟡 What's the difference between shallow and retained size in a heap dump?	Shallow size is an object's own memory (header + fields). Retained size is the total memory that would be freed if that object became unreachable, meaning everything it exclusively keeps alive. The dominator tree sorts by retained size, which is how you find the structure responsible for a leak.	ch10 part::II purpose::practice
jim-ch10-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q11</div>🔴 What does "GC overhead limit exceeded" mean?	With Parallel GC (<code>UseGCOverheadLimit</code>), the JVM throws it when more than ~98% of time goes to GC while recovering less than ~2% of the heap over several collections. The application is effectively out of memory and thrashing. Treat it like <code>Java heap space</code>: find the leak or the oversized live set.	ch10 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch10-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q12</div>🔴 How do CPU limits in Kubernetes affect GC?	The JVM sizes GC worker threads, JIT compiler threads and the common pool from detected CPUs, so a low limit means low parallelism. CFS quota throttling can stall GC threads mid-pause, stretching pause times and request latency, which shows up as <code>Real</code> time much greater than <code>User</code> time in GC logs. Watch throttling metrics and consider higher limits, bursting, or <code>-XX:ActiveProcessorCount</code>.	ch10 part::II purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch10-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q13</div>🟣 How would you choose the GC for a latency-sensitive payment API with an 8 GB heap?	Start from the SLO (say p99.9 &lt; 50 ms end-to-end). G1's default 200 ms target is too loose, and tuning it lower trades throughput for frequency. Generational ZGC gives sub-millisecond pauses independent of heap size, at the cost of a little throughput and some extra headroom (allocation stalls if under-provisioned). I'd load-test both on real traffic: compare p99/p99.9 latency, GC CPU and RSS, then pick ZGC if the tail latency gain is real, with alerting on allocation stalls. Either way, reduce allocation in hot paths first.	ch10 part::II purpose::decisions
jim-ch10-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 10 · Memory Management &amp; Garbage Collection · Q14</div>🟣 Your heap "keeps growing". How do you tell a leak from normal behaviour?	Look at occupancy <i>after</i> old/mixed/full collections over hours. A sawtooth that returns to a stable floor is normal: collectors happily use available heap. A rising floor across many cycles is a leak, or a growing live set (an unbounded cache). Confirm with class-histogram diffs over time, then a heap dump and MAT's dominator tree and path to GC roots. Verify the fix with a soak test that shows a flat post-GC floor.	ch10 part::II purpose::decisions
jim-ch11-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q1</div>🟢 What's the difference between <code>start()</code> and <code>run()</code>?	<code>start()</code> creates a new thread of execution, which then calls <code>run()</code>. Calling <code>run()</code> directly just executes the method on the current thread. Nothing runs concurrently. Calling <code>start()</code> twice throws <code>IllegalThreadStateException</code>.	ch11 part::III purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch11-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q2</div>🟡 What does <code>volatile</code> guarantee and what doesn't it?	It guarantees visibility (reads see the latest write), ordering (a volatile write is a release and a volatile read is an acquire, which creates happens-before edges that carry earlier writes along), and atomic 64-bit reads and writes. It does <b>not</b> make compound operations (<code>count++</code>, check-then-act) atomic. Use locks or atomic classes for those.	ch11 part::III purpose::practice
jim-ch11-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q3</div>🔴 Explain happens-before with an example that doesn't involve <code>volatile</code> or locks directly.	Executor submission: everything a thread does before <code>executor.submit(task)</code> happens-before the task starts running. So if you fully build a mutable object and then submit a task that reads it, the task sees the built state, even though you never wrote <code>volatile</code> or <code>synchronized</code> yourself. Similarly, <code>Future.get()</code> happens-after the task's work, and <code>t.join()</code> happens-after everything <code>t</code> did.	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q4</div>🔴 Why is double-checked locking broken without <code>volatile</code>?	<code>instance = new Singleton()</code> involves allocation, constructor writes, and publication of the reference. The constructor writes and the publication can be reordered by the JIT or the CPU. The first check reads <code>instance</code> without synchronization, so there's no happens-before edge, and a thread can see a non-null reference to a partially constructed object. <code>volatile</code> makes the publication a release and the read an acquire. Better yet, use the holder-class idiom, an enum, or (preview) <code>LazyConstant</code>.	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q5</div>🟡 Why must <code>wait()</code> be called in a loop?	Spurious wakeups are allowed. Another thread may also change the condition between the <code>notify</code> and your thread re-acquiring the monitor (a stolen condition). And <code>notifyAll</code> may wake threads waiting for different conditions. The loop re-checks the actual condition while holding the lock.	ch11 part::III purpose::practice
jim-ch11-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q6</div>🟡 <code>notify()</code> vs <code>notifyAll()</code>?	<code>notify</code> wakes one arbitrary waiter. If waiters wait for different conditions (producers vs consumers), it may wake the wrong kind and lose the signal, stalling the system. <code>notifyAll</code> wakes everyone, and each re-checks its condition. That's safer, at some cost. With <code>ReentrantLock</code>, separate <code>Condition</code> objects let you <code>signal</code> exactly the right kind of waiter.	ch11 part::III purpose::practice
jim-ch11-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q7</div>🔴 Your thread dump shows 300 threads in RUNNABLE inside <code>socketRead0</code>. Is the CPU busy?	Probably not. Threads blocked in native socket I/O show as <code>RUNNABLE</code> because the JVM considers them to be executing native code. This pattern usually means a slow or hung downstream dependency, often combined with missing read timeouts. Check socket timeouts, downstream latency, and connection-pool limits. Use CPU profiling to confirm the CPU is actually idle.	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q8</div>🔴 How would you prevent deadlock in a money-transfer system?	Acquire locks in a global, consistent order (for example, by account ID) to break circular wait. Alternatively, use <code>tryLock</code> with timeouts and backoff, avoid holding locks while calling external code, or redesign: a single-writer per account partition, or database transactions with row-level locks and consistent ordering. Then detect it anyway in production (<code>findDeadlockedThreads</code> as a health check).	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q9</div>🔴 What is safe publication? List the idioms.	Making both the reference and the object's state visible to other threads together. Idioms: initialize from a static initializer; store into a <code>volatile</code> field or <code>AtomicReference</code>; store into a <code>final</code> field of a properly constructed object; store into a lock-guarded field, including hand-off via concurrent collections or blocking queues. Immutable objects (final fields, no <code>this</code> escape) are safe even when published unsafely.	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q10</div>🔴 What's the risk of letting <code>this</code> escape from a constructor?	Other threads can see the object before construction completes. The final-field guarantees don't apply, so they can observe default values and invoke methods on a half-built object. Typical causes: registering listeners, starting threads, or publishing <code>this</code> to a static field inside the constructor. Use a private constructor and a static factory that publishes after construction.	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q11</div>🔴 How does <code>ThreadLocal</code> leak memory, and what's the fix?	Each thread holds a <code>ThreadLocalMap</code> whose entries have weak keys (the <code>ThreadLocal</code>) but strong values. Pool threads live forever, so values set and never removed stay reachable, and they can also leak to the next request, which is a correctness and security issue. In app servers they can pin class loaders (Metaspace leaks). Always <code>remove()</code> in <code>finally</code>. With virtual threads, prefer <code>ScopedValue</code>.	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q12</div>🟡 What's wrong with <code>synchronized ("LOCK")</code> or <code>synchronized (someInteger)</code>?	String literals are interned, and small <code>Integer</code>s are cached, so those objects are shared across the whole JVM. Unrelated code that locks the "same" literal or cached box contends with you or deadlocks with you. Wrappers are value-based classes (javac warns about synchronizing on them). Lock on a private final dedicated object.	ch11 part::III purpose::practice
jim-ch11-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q13</div>🔴 What is DRF-SC?	"Data-race-free implies sequential consistency." If every conflicting access to shared variables is ordered by happens-before (that is, the program has no data races), the program behaves as if all operations ran in some single interleaving consistent with program order. You only need to reason about reorderings for racy code, which you should avoid in the first place.	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q14</div>🔴 What happened to biased locking?	It made the uncontended reacquisition of a lock by the same thread nearly free, by "biasing" the object to that thread. Modern code uses <code>synchronized</code> less in hot paths (j.u.c. instead), and revoking a bias required expensive safepoints. It was disabled by default in JDK 15 (JEP 374) and later removed. HotSpot still has lightweight CAS locking and adaptive spinning.	ch11 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch11-q015	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 11 · Concurrency Foundations &amp; the Java Memory Model · Q15</div>🔵 How do you make a team's concurrent code reviewable and testable?	Prefer immutability and confinement in the design. Document the thread-safety level and use <code>@GuardedBy</code>/<code>@ThreadSafe</code> annotations, checked by static analysis (Error Prone). Keep lock scopes small and lock ordering documented. Use high-level j.u.c. primitives instead of <code>wait</code>/<code>notify</code>. Use jcstress for low-level primitives and stress or soak tests for components. Make thread dumps, deadlock detection and contention metrics (JFR) part of the operational toolkit.	ch11 part::III purpose::execution
jim-ch12-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q1</div>🟡 <code>synchronized</code> vs <code>ReentrantLock</code>?	Both are reentrant mutual-exclusion locks with the same memory semantics. <code>ReentrantLock</code> adds <code>tryLock</code> (optionally timed), interruptible acquisition, optional fairness, multiple <code>Condition</code>s and introspection, but you have to unlock in <code>finally</code>. Default to <code>synchronized</code> for simplicity and use <code>ReentrantLock</code> when you need those features. On JDK 21–23, <code>ReentrantLock</code> also avoided virtual-thread pinning, but JDK 24 removed that difference.	ch12 part::III purpose::practice
jim-ch12-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q2</div>🔴 Explain how <code>ThreadPoolExecutor</code> handles a new task. Why might <code>maximumPoolSize</code> never be reached?	If fewer than core threads are running, it starts a new thread. Otherwise it tries to queue the task. Only if the queue is full does it start threads up to max, and beyond that it rejects. With an unbounded queue (<code>Executors.newFixedThreadPool</code> uses <code>LinkedBlockingQueue</code>), the queue never fills, so max is never used and the queue grows without bound.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q3</div>🔴 Why are <code>Executors.newFixedThreadPool</code> and <code>newCachedThreadPool</code> risky in production?	Fixed: an unbounded work queue, so under overload tasks pile up, latency explodes and memory runs out. Cached: unbounded thread creation (max = <code>Integer.MAX_VALUE</code> with a <code>SynchronousQueue</code>), so under load you get thousands of threads and <code>OutOfMemoryError: unable to create native thread</code>. Use explicit <code>ThreadPoolExecutor</code>s with bounded queues, named threads, a deliberate rejection policy and metrics.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q4</div>🔴 How do you size a thread pool for I/O-bound work?	<code>N_cpu × U_target × (1 + W/C)</code>, or by Little's law: required concurrency = throughput × latency. Then cap it by downstream capacity (DB or HTTP connection pools), isolate per dependency (bulkheads), and bound the queue by acceptable waiting time. Validate under load: CPU utilisation, queue wait and p99 latency.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q5</div>🔴 <code>AtomicLong</code> vs <code>LongAdder</code>?	<code>AtomicLong</code> CASes a single variable: exact and atomic reads, but under heavy write contention it suffers CAS retries and cache-line bouncing. <code>LongAdder</code> spreads increments across padded cells per contending thread and sums on read. It's far more scalable for write-heavy counters, but <code>sum()</code> isn't an atomic snapshot. Use <code>LongAdder</code> for metrics and frequency counts, and <code>AtomicLong</code> for sequences and CAS-based state.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q6</div>🔴 What is the ABA problem?	CAS checks only that the current value equals the expected one. If it changed from A to B and back to A in between, the CAS succeeds even though the state changed. That's harmful in lock-free linked structures that reuse nodes. Mitigations: versioned references (<code>AtomicStampedReference</code>), avoiding node reuse (GC helps in Java), or locking.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q7</div>🔴 In <code>CompletableFuture</code>, which thread executes <code>thenApply</code>?	The thread that completes the previous stage, or the calling thread if that stage is already complete when <code>thenApply</code> is attached. <code>thenApplyAsync</code> runs on the default executor (the common ForkJoinPool) or a supplied executor. That's why blocking work in non-async callbacks can stall I/O threads, and why you should pass an explicit executor for blocking work.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q8</div>🔴 Does <code>CompletableFuture.cancel(true)</code> stop the running computation?	No. The future completes exceptionally with <code>CancellationException</code>, but the supplier keeps running, because CF doesn't use interrupts to control execution, and cancellation doesn't propagate to upstream stages. If you need real cancellation, use structured concurrency (which interrupts subtasks), <code>FutureTask</code> with an executor, or cooperative cancellation checks.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q9</div>🔴 How do virtual threads work, and what is pinning?	Virtual threads are JVM-scheduled threads multiplexed onto a small pool of carrier threads. On a blocking operation, the JVM unmounts the virtual thread (its stack is saved to the heap) and the carrier runs other virtual threads. When the operation completes, the virtual thread remounts on any carrier. Pinning is when it can't unmount and blocks its carrier: during native or FFM calls and some class-init paths, and, before JDK 24, inside <code>synchronized</code> or <code>Object.wait()</code>. Detect it with the JFR <code>jdk.VirtualThreadPinned</code> event.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q10</div>🔴 Should you pool virtual threads? How do you limit concurrency then?	No. They're cheap and designed to be created per task, and pooling them reintroduces the scalability limit. Limit concurrency to protected resources explicitly: semaphores, connection-pool limits with timeouts, rate limiters. The bottleneck moves to downstream resources, so size those deliberately.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q11</div>🔴 What problem does structured concurrency solve?	In unstructured async code (CF, <code>submit</code>), subtasks can outlive their caller, failures go unobserved, cancellation doesn't propagate, and observability suffers. Structured concurrency (<code>StructuredTaskScope</code>, preview) scopes subtasks to a block. Failure policies (joiners) cancel siblings and propagate errors, interrupting the owner cancels all subtasks, nothing leaks past the block, and thread dumps show the hierarchy.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q12</div>🟡 <code>CountDownLatch</code> vs <code>CyclicBarrier</code> vs <code>Semaphore</code>?	Latch: one-shot. Waiters block until the count reaches zero (for example, waiting for N services to start). Barrier: reusable. N threads wait for each other at a point, with an optional action at each trip (phased parallel algorithms). Semaphore: N permits that bound concurrent access (bulkheads, resource limits).	ch12 part::III purpose::practice
jim-ch12-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q13</div>🔴 What is false sharing and how do you fix it?	Independent variables written by different threads that sit on the same 64-byte cache line. Every write invalidates the line in the other cores, which destroys throughput with no visible lock contention. Fix it by padding (<code>@Contended</code>, inside the JDK or with <code>-XX:-RestrictContended</code>), by spreading state per thread, or with structures like <code>LongAdder</code> that already pad their cells.	ch12 part::III purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch12-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q14</div>🟣 Virtual threads or reactive for a new set of microservices?	For request/response services on Java 25+: virtual threads with structured concurrency. You get comparable I/O scalability, plain sequential code, normal debugging, and every blocking library (JDBC) still works. Choose reactive for streaming-heavy work (event streams, WebSocket fan-out, complex backpressured pipelines) or where an existing reactive stack is mature. Either way, design explicit backpressure and downstream limits, and validate with load tests. Don't rewrite working reactive services just to switch.	ch12 part::III purpose::decisions
jim-ch12-q015	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 12 · Advanced Concurrency: `java.util.concurrent` &amp; Project Loom · Q15</div>🟣 After enabling virtual threads, error rates went up with DB connection timeouts. Why, and what do you do?	The thread pool used to cap concurrency, effectively protecting the database. With virtual threads, far more requests reach the connection pool at once, exceed its size, and time out waiting. Fixes: add explicit concurrency limits (a semaphore matched to pool size) or admission control at the edge, tune the pool size and acquisition timeout to what the DB can actually handle, add load shedding (429/503) instead of queueing indefinitely, and capacity-test the downstream before rolling out.	ch12 part::III purpose::decisions
jim-ch13-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q1</div>🟢 What's the difference between <code>InputStream</code> and <code>Reader</code>?	<code>InputStream</code> reads raw bytes. <code>Reader</code> reads characters, decoding bytes with a charset (<code>InputStreamReader</code> bridges the two). Always specify the charset explicitly (UTF-8 is the default since Java 18) when converting between bytes and characters.	ch13 part::IV purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch13-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q2</div>🟡 Explain <code>flip()</code>, <code>clear()</code> and <code>compact()</code> on a <code>ByteBuffer</code>.	<code>flip()</code> switches from writing to reading: limit = position, position = 0. <code>clear()</code> prepares for writing from scratch: position = 0, limit = capacity (the data isn't erased). <code>compact()</code> moves unread bytes to the start and positions after them, so you can keep writing without losing unread data. Forgetting <code>flip()</code> before draining is the classic bug.	ch13 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch13-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q3</div>🔴 How does a selector-based server handle 10,000 connections with a few threads?	Channels are non-blocking and registered with a <code>Selector</code> for interest operations (accept, read, write). The selector (backed by epoll/kqueue) blocks until some channels are ready and returns only those keys. An event-loop thread handles each ready channel briefly and moves on. Threads never wait on idle connections, so a few event loops serve thousands of sockets, as long as handlers never block.	ch13 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch13-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q4</div>🔴 How do virtual threads make blocking socket code scalable?	Since JDK 13 the <code>Socket</code> API is implemented on NIO. When a virtual thread performs a blocking socket read with no data available, the JDK registers the socket with an internal poller, parks the virtual thread, and releases its carrier. When the socket becomes readable, the poller unparks the virtual thread, which resumes on any carrier. You write simple blocking code and get multiplexed I/O underneath.	ch13 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch13-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q5</div>🔴 What is zero-copy and how does Java use it?	Moving data between I/O endpoints without copying it through user space. <code>FileChannel.transferTo</code> to a socket maps to <code>sendfile(2)</code> on Linux: the kernel sends page-cache data directly to the network stack. It avoids user-space copies and context switches, and it's central to Kafka's consumer throughput.	ch13 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch13-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q6</div>🔴 What are the pitfalls of <code>MappedByteBuffer</code>?	A 2 GB size limit per mapping (int indexing). No explicit unmap: the mapping is released when the buffer is garbage-collected, which keeps files locked (on Windows especially) and holds address space. Page faults cause latency spikes. Java 22's FFM API (<code>FileChannel.map(…, arena)</code> returning a <code>MemorySegment</code>) supports 64-bit sizes and deterministic unmapping when the arena closes.	ch13 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch13-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q7</div>🟡 How do you write a file atomically?	Write to a temporary file in the same directory, <code>force(true)</code> (fsync) it, then <code>Files.move</code> with <code>ATOMIC_MOVE</code> (and <code>REPLACE_EXISTING</code>) onto the target. For full crash durability on Linux, also fsync the parent directory. Readers then see either the old or the new complete file, never a partial one.	ch13 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch13-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q8</div>🟡 What are the default socket timeouts in Java and why does it matter?	Infinite (0) for connect and read on <code>Socket</code> and many older clients. A hung downstream service can block threads forever, exhaust the pools, and cascade into a full outage. Always set connect, read and overall timeouts derived from latency budgets, plus a connection-pool acquire timeout.	ch13 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch13-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q9</div>🔴 Why is Java deserialization of untrusted data dangerous, and how do you defend against it?	<code>readObject</code> instantiates any serializable class named in the stream and runs its deserialization hooks. Attackers chain existing classes ("gadgets") into remote code execution (the Commons Collections chain in 2015, for example). Defences: don't use Java serialization for untrusted input (use schema'd formats like JSON or Protobuf); if unavoidable, use allow-list <code>ObjectInputFilter</code>s (JEP 290/415) with depth and size limits; keep gadget libraries off the classpath and patch dependencies.	ch13 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch13-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q10</div>🟡 What happens if you don't declare <code>serialVersionUID</code>?	The JVM computes one from the class structure. Almost any change (adding a method or field, changing an interface) changes the computed value, so deserializing previously stored data fails with <code>InvalidClassException</code>. Declare it explicitly and manage compatible changes deliberately.	ch13 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch13-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q11</div>🔴 What makes Protobuf schemas evolvable? What must you never do?	Fields are identified by numeric tags, and unknown tags are skipped, so adding optional fields is compatible in both directions. Never reuse or change a field number, or change a field's type incompatibly. Mark removed fields <code>reserved</code>. Avoid <code>required</code> (proto3 removed it). Enforce the rules automatically with breaking-change checks in CI (<code>buf breaking</code>, registry compatibility modes).	ch13 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch13-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 13 · I/O, NIO, Networking &amp; Serialization · Q12</div>🟣 A service occasionally sends traffic to terminated instances for minutes after a deploy. What do you check?	Client-side caching of endpoints. The JVM DNS cache TTL (<code>networkaddress.cache.ttl</code>, possibly set to infinite somewhere), connection pools without a max lifetime or idle eviction holding sockets to old IPs, client-side load balancers with stale membership, and missing retry or health-check logic for connection resets. Fix it with sensible DNS TTLs, a pool max lifetime and validation, service-discovery-aware clients, and graceful shutdown on the server side (stop accepting, drain, then exit).	ch13 part::IV purpose::decisions
jim-ch14-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q1</div>🟢 What's the difference between <code>getMethods()</code> and <code>getDeclaredMethods()</code>?	<code>getMethods()</code> returns all <i>public</i> methods, including inherited ones (from superclasses and interfaces). <code>getDeclaredMethods()</code> returns all methods of any access level <i>declared in that class only</i>, without inherited ones. The same distinction applies to fields and constructors.	ch14 part::IV purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch14-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q2</div>🔴 Why can <code>setAccessible(true)</code> throw <code>InaccessibleObjectException</code> on modern JDKs?	JPMS strong encapsulation: deep reflection requires the target package to be <i>opened</i> to the caller's module. Since JDK 17 (JEP 403), JDK internals aren't open to application code, so reflecting into, say, <code>String.value</code> fails. Fix it by upgrading the library, by using supported APIs (<code>VarHandle</code>, <code>MethodHandles.privateLookupIn</code> with proper opens), or temporarily with <code>--add-opens</code>.	ch14 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch14-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q3</div>🔴 Reflection vs <code>MethodHandle</code>: why are method handles faster?	Method handles check access once, at lookup time, are strongly typed (<code>invokeExact</code>), and, when held in <code>static final</code> fields, are constants the JIT can inline like direct calls. Classic reflection boxed arguments and checked access per call, and was harder to optimise. Since JDK 18, core reflection itself is built on method handles, which narrows the gap. Cache handles rather than looking them up repeatedly.	ch14 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch14-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q4</div>🟡 Explain annotation retention policies.	<code>SOURCE</code>: discarded after compilation, read by the compiler and processors (<code>@Override</code>). <code>CLASS</code> (the default): stored in the class file but not available through reflection. <code>RUNTIME</code>: stored and readable via reflection (<code>@Transactional</code>, <code>@Entity</code>). Frameworks that act at runtime need <code>RUNTIME</code>.	ch14 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch14-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q5</div>🟡 Does <code>@Inherited</code> make method annotations inherited?	No. It only affects <i>class-level</i> annotations, and only through the superclass chain, not interfaces. Frameworks like Spring implement their own lookup algorithms that walk interfaces, bridge methods and meta-annotations, which is why some annotations "inherit" in Spring but not with plain reflection.	ch14 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch14-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q6</div>🔴 How do annotation processors differ from Lombok?	Standard annotation processors (JSR 269) run during compilation, read program elements through <code>javax.lang.model</code>, and generate <i>new</i> sources or resources. They can't modify existing classes. Lombok hooks into javac internals to modify the AST of your classes directly. That's powerful, but it couples it to compiler internals and has caused breakages across JDK versions.	ch14 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch14-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q7</div>🔴 JDK dynamic proxy vs CGLIB/ByteBuddy proxy?	A JDK proxy generates a class that implements given <i>interfaces</i> and routes every call to an <code>InvocationHandler</code>, so it can only proxy interface methods. Subclass proxies generate a subclass of the concrete class and override its methods, so no interface is needed, but <code>final</code> classes and methods, and <code>private</code> and <code>static</code> methods, can't be intercepted. Spring Boot uses subclass (CGLIB) proxies by default.	ch14 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch14-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q8</div>🔴 Why doesn't <code>@Transactional</code> work when a method calls another method in the same class?	Spring applies it with a proxy around the bean. External calls go through the proxy, which starts the transaction. Internal calls (<code>this.method()</code>) go directly to the target object, bypassing the proxy and its advice. Fix it by moving the method to another bean, self-injecting the proxy, using <code>TransactionTemplate</code> programmatically, or switching to AspectJ weaving.	ch14 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch14-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q9</div>🔴 What can a Java agent do, and what are its limits?	With <code>Instrumentation</code>, an agent can transform bytecode as classes load, retransform or redefine loaded classes, measure object sizes, and add JARs to class-loader search paths. Retransformation can only change method bodies: no adding or removing fields or methods, and no signature changes. Agents start via <code>-javaagent</code> (premain) or dynamic attach (agentmain), and dynamic attach now warns (JEP 451) and will eventually require an explicit opt-in.	ch14 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch14-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q10</div>🟡 How does <code>ServiceLoader</code> discover implementations?	It reads <code>META-INF/services/&lt;fully-qualified interface name&gt;</code> files on the class path (each listing implementation classes), or <code>provides … with …</code> declarations from named modules, and instantiates providers lazily. <code>stream()</code> exposes <code>Provider</code> objects so you can filter by type before instantiating. It uses the thread context class loader by default, which matters in containers.	ch14 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch14-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q11</div>🔴 Why did the JDK add the Class-File API when ASM exists?	The class-file format changes every six months, and every tool that bundled its own ASM version had to wait for an ASM update plus its own release before supporting a new JDK. A JDK-bundled API (final in Java 24, JEP 484) always matches the JDK it ships with, so the platform and its tools can evolve together.	ch14 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch14-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 14 · Reflection, Annotations, Proxies &amp; Agents · Q12</div>🟣 Your org is evaluating a move to GraalVM native images. What reflection-related work is needed?	Native image assumes a closed world: reflection, dynamic proxies, resources and serialization must be known at build time. That means reachability metadata (configuration or annotations), framework AOT support (Spring AOT, Micronaut and Quarkus build-time processing), replacing runtime-reflection libraries with code generation where possible (MapStruct instead of reflective mappers), the tracing agent during test runs to collect metadata, and extensive testing, since missing metadata fails at runtime. Budget for CI build time and for the debugging and profiling differences.	ch14 part::IV purpose::decisions
jim-ch15-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q1</div>🟡 What problems does JPMS solve, and why haven't most applications adopted it?	It provides reliable configuration (missing modules and split packages are detected at startup) and strong encapsulation (only exported packages are accessible, and only opened packages allow deep reflection). It also enabled the JDK's own modularization and <code>jlink</code>. Adoption is low because frameworks rely heavily on reflection, many libraries lack proper descriptors, split packages exist in older ecosystems, and build-level modules plus architecture tests give most of the boundary benefits with less friction.	ch15 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch15-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q2</div>🟡 <code>exports</code> vs <code>opens</code>? <code>requires transitive</code> vs <code>requires static</code>?	<code>exports</code> grants compile-time and runtime access to public types. <code>opens</code> grants runtime deep-reflective access (including private members), which frameworks need. <code>requires transitive</code> makes your consumers also read the dependency (use it when your API exposes its types). <code>requires static</code> is required at compile time but optional at runtime (annotations, optional integrations).	ch15 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch15-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q3</div>🔴 How does Maven resolve conflicting dependency versions? How does Gradle?	Maven uses "nearest wins": the version closest to the root of the dependency tree, with first declaration breaking ties, regardless of which is higher. Gradle picks the highest requested version by default and supports rich constraints (<code>strictly</code>, <code>reject</code>), platforms and locking. In both, the robust approach is explicit alignment via BOMs or platforms plus convergence checks in CI.	ch15 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch15-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q4</div>🟡 What's the difference between <code>dependencyManagement</code> and <code>dependencies</code>?	<code>dependencyManagement</code> declares versions (and scopes or exclusions) without adding dependencies. It governs the version wherever that artifact appears, including transitively. <code>dependencies</code> actually adds artifacts to the project. BOMs are imported into <code>dependencyManagement</code> with <code>scope=import</code>.	ch15 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch15-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q5</div>🔴 Explain Gradle's <code>api</code> vs <code>implementation</code>.	<code>api</code> dependencies are part of the library's public API and leak onto consumers' compile classpath. <code>implementation</code> dependencies are internal: consumers don't see them at compile time (they're still on the runtime classpath). <code>implementation</code> improves encapsulation and enables compile avoidance: changing it doesn't force downstream projects to recompile.	ch15 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch15-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q6</div>🔴 You see <code>NoSuchMethodError</code> in production after a dependency bump. How do you diagnose and fix it?	Identify the class and method in the error, find which JAR the class was loaded from (<code>-verbose:class</code> or <code>getCodeSource()</code>), and find which artifact needs the missing method and which version won mediation (<code>dependency:tree -Dverbose</code> / <code>dependencyInsight</code>). Fix by aligning versions (BOM or platform, <code>dependencyManagement</code> pin, upgrading the lagging library). Prevent recurrence with enforcer convergence and upper-bound rules plus test coverage of integration paths.	ch15 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch15-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q7</div>🔴 What is shading and when is it appropriate?	Copying a dependency's classes into your artifact and relocating their packages so they can't conflict with other versions on the classpath. It's appropriate for libraries with heavy or conflict-prone dependencies, or for tools distributed as a single JAR. Downsides: larger artifacts, duplicate classes, CVE scanners potentially missing shaded copies, and the need to re-release to pick up security fixes. For applications, prefer version alignment.	ch15 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch15-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q8</div>🟡 How does a Spring Boot executable JAR work?	It contains the app's classes in <code>BOOT-INF/classes</code>, dependencies as nested JARs in <code>BOOT-INF/lib</code>, and Spring Boot's launcher as the manifest <code>Main-Class</code>. The launcher creates a class loader that can read nested JARs directly, then invokes the real <code>Start-Class</code>. Layered extraction (via <code>-Djarmode=tools</code>) splits it into Docker-friendly layers so dependency layers are cached across releases.	ch15 part::IV purpose::practice
jim-ch15-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q9</div>🔴 How would you use <code>jlink</code> in a container build, and what's the trade-off?	Use <code>jdeps --print-module-deps</code> to compute the required JDK modules, <code>jlink</code> them (<code>--strip-debug --no-man-pages --no-header-files --compress</code>) into a minimal runtime in a build stage, and copy it into a slim base image. The benefits are smaller images, faster pulls and fewer packages to patch. The trade-off is missing modules (reflection-only uses can be missed by <code>jdeps</code>, so test thoroughly) and missing diagnostics unless you add <code>jdk.jcmd</code> and <code>jdk.jfr</code> explicitly.	ch15 part::IV purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch15-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q10</div>🔵 How do you make builds reproducible and supply-chain-safe across an organisation?	Pinned tool versions (wrappers with checksums, toolchains), pinned dependencies (no dynamic versions; lockfiles), reproducible archive settings, a single internal artifact proxy, dependency checksum and signature verification, SBOM generation per artifact, provenance and signing (SLSA, Sigstore), vulnerability scanning with policy gates, and automated update PRs. Pair this with the ability to rebuild and redeploy the whole fleet quickly when a critical CVE lands.	ch15 part::IV purpose::execution
jim-ch15-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 15 · Modules &amp; Build Tools · Q11</div>🟣 How would you enforce module boundaries in a large Spring Boot monolith without JPMS?	Split it into build modules (Maven modules or Gradle subprojects) with <code>implementation</code> edges so internal dependencies can't leak, add ArchUnit rules (layer dependencies, no cycles, domain independent of frameworks), and consider Spring Modulith for package-based application modules with verification, event-based interaction between modules and generated documentation. Make the rules part of CI so violations fail the build rather than relying on reviews.	ch15 part::IV purpose::decisions
jim-ch16-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q1</div>🟡 Explain SRP. Isn't "a class should do one thing" enough?	"One thing" is subjective. The useful definition is "one reason to change", meaning one actor or stakeholder whose requirements drive changes. Mixing logic owned by different actors (payroll rules vs reporting rules vs persistence) means one group's change can break another's. Over-applied, SRP fragments code into tiny classes and <i>lowers</i> cohesion. Split along real axes of change.	ch16 part::V purpose::practice
jim-ch16-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q2</div>🔴 How does OCP relate to sealed types and pattern matching?	Polymorphic OCP (interface plus implementations) makes adding new <i>types</i> easy but adding new <i>operations</i> hard. Sealed hierarchies plus exhaustive switches make adding <i>operations</i> easy, while adding a <i>type</i> requires updating every switch (with the compiler pointing to each one). This is the expression problem. Choose based on which axis changes more often in the domain.	ch16 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch16-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q3</div>🔴 What's the difference between Dependency Inversion and Dependency Injection?	Injection is a wiring technique: dependencies are supplied from outside (constructor injection, a DI container). Inversion is a structural principle: high-level policy owns the abstractions it needs (ports), and low-level details implement them, so source-code dependencies point toward the policy. You can use DI with concrete infrastructure types and still violate DIP.	ch16 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch16-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q4</div>🟡 Give a real example of an ISP violation and its fix.	A 15-method <code>OrderRepository</code> used by a read-only reporting job forces it to depend on, and mock, write and archive methods. Split it into role interfaces (<code>OrderReader</code>, <code>OrderWriter</code>, <code>OrderArchiver</code>) shaped by their clients. One adapter can implement all of them. In the JDK, <code>List</code>'s "optional operations" are an acknowledged compromise in the same area.	ch16 part::V purpose::practice
jim-ch16-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q5</div>🔴 When does DRY hurt?	When it merges code that looks similar but represents different knowledge that will change for different reasons. The shared abstraction then grows flags and special cases until it couples unrelated features (the "wrong abstraction"). DRY is about not duplicating <i>knowledge</i> (business rules, schemas), not about de-duplicating similar-looking code. Tolerate some duplication until the true abstraction is clear.	ch16 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch16-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q6</div>🔴 Explain hexagonal architecture and when you wouldn't use it.	The application core (domain plus use cases) depends only on ports it defines. Inbound adapters (REST, messaging) call inbound ports, and outbound adapters (DB, payment, Kafka) implement outbound ports, so dependencies point inward. The benefits are framework-independent business logic, swappable infrastructure and fast tests. It's overkill for thin CRUD services, where the mapping layers add cost without protecting meaningful domain logic.	ch16 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch16-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q7</div>🔴 What is an aggregate, and how big should it be?	A cluster of domain objects treated as one consistency boundary, with a root that enforces invariants and is the only entry point. Changes inside it are transactionally consistent. Keep aggregates small (only what must be immediately consistent), reference other aggregates by ID, use domain events for eventual consistency between aggregates, and modify one aggregate per transaction as a rule of thumb. Large aggregates cause contention and expensive loads.	ch16 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch16-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q8</div>🟡 Entity vs value object?	An entity has a stable identity and a lifecycle, and equality is by identity (an <code>Order</code> with ID 42 stays the same order as its lines change). A value object has no identity and is defined entirely by its attributes; it's immutable and replaceable (<code>Money</code>, <code>Address</code>, <code>DateRange</code>). In Java, records are the natural fit for value objects.	ch16 part::V purpose::practice
jim-ch16-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q9</div>🔴 What is an anti-corruption layer?	A translation layer at the boundary between your bounded context and an external or legacy system. It converts their models and semantics into your own, so foreign concepts don't leak into your domain. Typical implementation: an adapter behind an outbound port that calls the legacy API and maps its responses into your domain types, including mapping or normalising errors.	ch16 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch16-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q10</div>🟣 How do you approach changing a critical legacy module with no tests?	Write characterization tests around the behaviour you're about to change, find or create seams to get the code under test, and refactor in small steps. For bigger changes, use branch by abstraction or a strangler approach behind feature flags, expand–contract for interface and data changes, and observability to compare the old and new paths (shadow traffic, diffing outputs) before switching over. Record the decision and the rollback plan.	ch16 part::V purpose::decisions
jim-ch16-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 16 · Design Principles, Clean Architecture &amp; DDD · Q11</div>🟣 What makes a good API, in your opinion?	Easy to use correctly and hard to misuse: small surface, consistent names, immutable values, typed parameters instead of primitive or boolean soup, no nulls in return values, documented contracts (nullness, thread safety, exceptions), and a planned evolution path (additive changes, deprecation windows, compatibility checks in CI). Above all, design it from real client use cases, ideally by writing client code first.	ch16 part::V purpose::decisions
jim-ch17-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q1</div>🟢 What is the best way to implement a singleton in Java?	A single-element enum (thread-safe, and immune to reflection and serialization attacks) or the initialization-on-demand holder idiom (lazy and lock-free). In application code, prefer letting a DI container manage single instances, because static singletons are global state that hides dependencies and hurts testability.	ch17 part::V purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch17-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q2</div>🟡 Decorator vs Proxy vs Adapter?	All three wrap an object. An adapter changes the <i>interface</i> (makes A look like B). A decorator keeps the interface and <i>adds behaviour</i> (caching, metrics, buffering), and is usually stacked deliberately. A proxy keeps the interface and <i>controls access</i> (lazy loading, security, remoting, transactions), and is usually inserted transparently by infrastructure. Decorator and proxy are structurally identical and differ in intent.	ch17 part::V purpose::practice
jim-ch17-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q3</div>🟡 Strategy vs State?	Both delegate behaviour to interchangeable objects. In Strategy the <i>client</i> picks the algorithm (pricing by shipping method), and strategies don't know about each other. In State, the <i>object's internal state</i> determines behaviour, and states typically trigger transitions to other states (an order lifecycle). In modern Java, strategies are often lambdas, and state machines are often enums or sealed records with transition functions.	ch17 part::V purpose::practice
jim-ch17-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q4</div>🔴 Why has the Visitor pattern become less common in modern Java?	Visitor existed to add operations to a fixed hierarchy without modifying the classes, using double dispatch. Sealed interfaces plus pattern-matching <code>switch</code> (Java 21) give the same capability with far less ceremony and compiler-checked exhaustiveness. Visitor still makes sense for open hierarchies or existing APIs (<code>javax.lang.model</code>, ASM).	ch17 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch17-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q5</div>🟡 Name patterns used in the <code>java.io</code> package.	Decorator (buffering, data and object streams wrapping other streams), Adapter (<code>InputStreamReader</code>/<code>OutputStreamWriter</code> bridging bytes and characters), and Template Method in the abstract base classes (<code>InputStream.read(byte[])</code> built on the abstract <code>read()</code>). Factory methods appear in NIO.2 (<code>Files.newBufferedReader</code>).	ch17 part::V purpose::practice
jim-ch17-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q6</div>🔴 What patterns does Spring's <code>@Transactional</code> rely on?	Proxy (an AOP proxy around the bean intercepts calls), Template Method or callback (<code>TransactionTemplate</code>/<code>TransactionInterceptor</code> define begin, invoke, commit or rollback), Strategy (<code>PlatformTransactionManager</code> implementations for JDBC, JPA, JTA, reactive), and thread-bound context through <code>ThreadLocal</code> resources (<code>TransactionSynchronizationManager</code>). The proxy explains the self-invocation pitfall.	ch17 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch17-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q7</div>🟡 What's wrong with the Service Locator pattern?	Dependencies are fetched from a global registry inside methods, so they're invisible in the class's API, tests must configure global state, and missing dependencies fail at runtime instead of at construction. Constructor injection makes dependencies explicit, verified at startup, and trivially replaceable in tests.	ch17 part::V purpose::practice
jim-ch17-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q8</div>🔴 Where does the Observer pattern cause production issues?	Leaks from listeners that are never deregistered. Latency coupling when synchronous listeners block the publisher. Exceptions in listeners propagating into the publisher (and its transaction). Unpredictable ordering and re-entrancy. Lost events on crash for in-memory events. For cross-service or must-not-lose events, use durable messaging with an outbox. In-process, make async boundaries and error handling explicit.	ch17 part::V purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch17-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 17 · Design Patterns · Q9</div>🟣 How do you decide whether to introduce a pattern?	Start from a concrete pain: duplicated conditionals across the codebase, a class changing for several reasons, a need to vary behaviour at runtime, cross-cutting concerns leaking into business code. Pick the simplest construct that removes the pain (often a lambda, an enum or a sealed type in modern Java). Weigh the indirection cost (navigation, debugging, onboarding) against the expected change frequency, and refactor towards the pattern when evidence appears rather than designing for speculative flexibility.	ch17 part::V purpose::decisions
jim-ch18-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q1</div>🟢 Why is constructor injection preferred?	Dependencies are explicit and can be <code>final</code>, the object is fully initialized after construction, it's trivially testable with <code>new</code> (no Spring or reflection), too many constructor parameters surface SRP smells, and circular dependencies fail fast at startup instead of being papered over.	ch18 part::VI purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch18-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q2</div>🔴 Walk through the Spring bean lifecycle.	Instantiate (constructor) → populate (field and setter injection) → Aware callbacks (<code>BeanNameAware</code>, <code>BeanFactoryAware</code>…) → <code>BeanPostProcessor.postProcessBeforeInitialization</code> (including <code>ApplicationContextAware</code> and <code>@PostConstruct</code>) → <code>InitializingBean.afterPropertiesSet</code> → custom init method → <code>postProcessAfterInitialization</code> (where AOP proxies are created) → in use → on shutdown: <code>@PreDestroy</code> → <code>DisposableBean.destroy</code> → custom destroy method. Prototype beans don't get destruction callbacks.	ch18 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch18-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q3</div>🔴 <code>BeanFactoryPostProcessor</code> vs <code>BeanPostProcessor</code>?	A <code>BeanFactoryPostProcessor</code> modifies bean <i>definitions</i> before any regular bean is instantiated (<code>ConfigurationClassPostProcessor</code> registers definitions from <code>@Configuration</code> classes; the placeholder configurer resolves <code>${…}</code>). A <code>BeanPostProcessor</code> works on bean <i>instances</i> around initialization: injection, <code>@PostConstruct</code>, and wrapping beans in AOP proxies.	ch18 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch18-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q4</div>🔴 How does Spring resolve circular dependencies, and why can't it for constructor injection?	For field or setter injection, it registers an <code>ObjectFactory</code> for a bean right after instantiation (third-level cache). When a dependent bean needs it during population, the factory produces an early reference (possibly an early AOP proxy via <code>getEarlyBeanReference</code>), cached in the second level. Once complete, the bean moves to the first level. With constructor injection, no instance exists before the dependencies are resolved, so there's nothing to expose: <code>BeanCurrentlyInCreationException</code>. Since Boot 2.6 cycles are prohibited by default. Fix the design.	ch18 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch18-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q5</div>🔴 What's the difference between <code>@Configuration</code> full and lite mode?	Full mode (<code>proxyBeanMethods = true</code>) subclasses the configuration class with CGLIB, so calls between <code>@Bean</code> methods return the container's singleton. Lite mode (<code>proxyBeanMethods = false</code>, or <code>@Bean</code> methods in a <code>@Component</code>) has no proxy, so calling a <code>@Bean</code> method directly creates a new object. Lite mode starts faster and suits native images; inject dependencies as <code>@Bean</code> method parameters instead of calling the methods.	ch18 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch18-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q6</div>🔴 Name five situations where <code>@Transactional</code> doesn't do what people expect.	(1) Self-invocation bypasses the proxy. (2) <code>private</code> (and, for CGLIB, <code>final</code>) methods aren't intercepted. (3) Checked exceptions commit by default unless you set <code>rollbackFor</code>. (4) Work on other threads (<code>@Async</code>, <code>CompletableFuture</code>, executors) isn't in the transaction. (5) A caught exception from an inner <code>REQUIRED</code> participant still leaves the shared transaction rollback-only, causing <code>UnexpectedRollbackException</code> at commit. Bonus: calling <code>@Transactional</code> methods from <code>@PostConstruct</code> runs without a transaction, because the proxy doesn't exist yet.	ch18 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch18-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q7</div>🔴 <code>REQUIRED</code> vs <code>REQUIRES_NEW</code> vs <code>NESTED</code>?	<code>REQUIRED</code> joins the existing transaction or creates one, and failures affect the whole shared transaction. <code>REQUIRES_NEW</code> suspends the current transaction and runs in an independent one that commits or rolls back on its own, using a second connection (watch pool sizing). <code>NESTED</code> uses a savepoint in the existing transaction, so an inner rollback only reverts to the savepoint. It needs a JDBC transaction manager with savepoint support.	ch18 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch18-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q8</div>🟡 How does Spring Boot auto-configuration work, and how do you override it?	<code>@EnableAutoConfiguration</code> loads candidate classes listed in <code>META-INF/spring/…AutoConfiguration.imports</code> across the classpath. Each is guarded by conditions (<code>@ConditionalOnClass</code>, <code>@ConditionalOnMissingBean</code>, <code>@ConditionalOnProperty</code>…) and ordered with <code>after</code>/<code>before</code>. They back off when you define your own beans. Override by defining the bean yourself, setting properties, or excluding the auto-configuration. Debug with <code>--debug</code> (the condition evaluation report) or <code>/actuator/conditions</code>.	ch18 part::VI purpose::practice
jim-ch18-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q9</div>🟡 How do you inject a prototype bean into a singleton correctly?	Injecting it directly gives the singleton one instance forever. Use <code>ObjectProvider&lt;T&gt;</code> (call <code>getObject()</code> each time), a <code>@Lookup</code> method, or a scoped proxy (<code>proxyMode = TARGET_CLASS</code>), which resolves a fresh instance per call. The scoped-proxy mechanism also makes request- and session-scoped beans injectable into singletons.	ch18 part::VI purpose::practice
jim-ch18-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q10</div>🔴 Describe the Spring MVC request flow.	Servlet filters (security, CORS, tracing) → <code>DispatcherServlet</code> → <code>HandlerMapping</code> finds the handler and interceptors → interceptors' <code>preHandle</code> → <code>HandlerAdapter</code> resolves arguments (path variables, request params, <code>@RequestBody</code> via <code>HttpMessageConverter</code>, validation) and invokes the controller → the return-value handler serialises the result → <code>postHandle</code>/<code>afterCompletion</code>. Exceptions go to <code>HandlerExceptionResolver</code>s (<code>@ExceptionHandler</code>/<code>@ControllerAdvice</code>), which often render <code>ProblemDetail</code>.	ch18 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch18-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q11</div>🔴 Why is a remote call inside a <code>@Transactional</code> method dangerous?	The transaction holds a database connection (and possibly row locks) for the whole duration of the remote call. Under load or when the remote service is slow, the connection pool exhausts and the service stalls, and locks block other transactions. Also, if the transaction rolls back after the remote call succeeded, the systems disagree. Keep remote calls outside transactions and coordinate with idempotency, an outbox, or sagas.	ch18 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch18-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q12</div>🟣 What did Spring Boot 4 / Spring Framework 7 change that affects a large codebase upgrade?	Jakarta EE 11 baseline, Jackson 3 by default (package renames: the largest code impact), modularized auto-configuration (starter changes), JSpecify null annotations (Kotlin and IDE nullness changes), Hibernate 7, Tomcat 11/Jetty 12 with Undertow dropped, and new first-class features (API versioning, <code>@Retryable</code>/<code>@ConcurrencyLimit</code> in core, HTTP service clients, an OpenTelemetry starter). I'd plan it platform-first: upgrade the internal starters and BOM, run OpenRewrite recipes, fix Jackson-related serialization tests, then roll out by service tier.	ch18 part::VI purpose::decisions
jim-ch18-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 18 · Spring Internals · Q13</div>🟣 Your team reports intermittent <code>LazyInitializationException</code> after disabling Open Session in View. What's your guidance?	That's OSIV doing its job: it had been hiding lazy loading in the web layer, outside service transactions. The fix is to fetch what each use case needs inside the transaction (JOIN FETCH, entity graphs, DTO projections) and return DTOs from the service layer, not entities. Keep OSIV off. It holds connections for the whole request and hides N+1 queries (Chapter 19).	ch18 part::VI purpose::decisions
jim-ch19-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q1</div>🟡 Why use <code>PreparedStatement</code> instead of <code>Statement</code>?	Bound parameters are sent separately from the SQL text, which prevents SQL injection. Statements can be parsed and planned once and reused (plus driver and server caching). Typed setters handle dates, decimals and binary data correctly. Combined with <code>addBatch</code>, they enable efficient bulk operations.	ch19 part::VI purpose::practice
jim-ch19-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q2</div>🔴 How do you size a database connection pool?	Start small. A common heuristic is <code>(DB cores × 2) + effective spindles</code>, because the database can only run about core-count queries in parallel, and more connections add contention. Multiply by the number of application instances and keep the total under the database's limits (or use PgBouncer or RDS Proxy). Set a short acquire timeout so overload fails fast, and <code>maxLifetime</code> below network idle timeouts. Tune using acquire-time and pending-connection metrics under load.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q3</div>🔴 Explain the isolation levels and the anomalies each prevents. What does PostgreSQL actually do?	Read Uncommitted allows dirty reads. Read Committed prevents dirty reads. Repeatable Read prevents non-repeatable reads. Serializable prevents phantoms and all other anomalies. PostgreSQL never allows dirty reads, its Repeatable Read is snapshot isolation (no phantoms, but write skew is possible), and its Serializable is SSI, which aborts conflicting transactions with serialization failures that the application must retry. MySQL InnoDB defaults to Repeatable Read with next-key locking for locking reads.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q4</div>🔴 What is write skew? Give an example and a fix.	Two transactions read overlapping data, each makes a decision based on it, and they write <i>different</i> rows, so no write-write conflict is detected, but together they violate an invariant. Example: two on-call doctors both see "two doctors on call" and both go off call. Snapshot isolation allows it. Fixes: Serializable isolation (SSI) with retries, <code>SELECT … FOR UPDATE</code> on the rows the decision depends on, or materialising the constraint in a single row that both transactions must update.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q5</div>🔴 Optimistic vs pessimistic locking: when do you use each?	Optimistic (<code>@Version</code>): no locks, and conflicts are detected at update time (<code>WHERE version = ?</code>, zero rows means conflict), followed by a retry or a 409. Best when conflicts are rare and you want high concurrency. Pessimistic (<code>SELECT … FOR UPDATE</code>): locks rows so contenders wait. Better under high contention on the same rows, but you must keep transactions short, set lock timeouts and order lock acquisition to avoid deadlocks.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q6</div>🔴 What is the persistence context and what does it give you?	A per-<code>EntityManager</code> (per-transaction) set of managed entities that acts as an identity map (one object per row), a first-level cache, and a unit of work: changes to managed entities are tracked by dirty checking against loaded snapshots and written in a batch at flush (before commit and before relevant queries).
