The cost: memory and flush time grow with the number of managed entities.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q7</div>🔴 What is the N+1 problem and how do you fix it?	Loading N parent entities with one query, then triggering one extra query per parent when accessing a lazy association, so N+1 round trips. Fix it per use case with JOIN FETCH or entity graphs (one query), batch fetching (<code>default_batch_fetch_size</code>, turning N into N/batch), or DTO projections for read-only views. Detect it with query-count assertions in tests and APM traces. Beware JOIN FETCH of collections combined with pagination (in-memory paging), and fetch IDs first.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q8</div>🟡 Why does Hibernate not batch inserts with <code>IDENTITY</code> IDs?	With <code>IDENTITY</code>, the database generates the ID during the INSERT, and Hibernate needs the ID immediately to manage the entity in the persistence context. So it executes each INSERT right away instead of buffering it into a JDBC batch. <code>SEQUENCE</code> with a pooled optimizer pre-allocates IDs, so inserts can be batched.	ch19 part::VI purpose::practice
jim-ch19-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q9</div>🔴 Why is Open Session in View considered an anti-pattern?	It keeps the persistence context, and a database connection, open for the whole HTTP request, including view rendering and JSON serialization. That lengthens connection hold times (pool exhaustion under load), hides lazy-loading N+1 queries in the web layer, and blurs transaction boundaries. Disable it, fetch what each use case needs in the service layer, and return DTOs.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q10</div>🔴 What's the problem with <code>save()</code> in Spring Data JPA for entities with application-assigned IDs?	<code>save()</code> decides between <code>persist</code> and <code>merge</code> using <code>isNew()</code>, which by default checks for a null ID (or null version). Assigned IDs look "not new", so <code>merge</code> is used, and it issues a SELECT before the INSERT (doubling round trips). Fix: implement <code>Persistable</code> with a correct <code>isNew()</code> (for example, a transient flag set after load or persist), or use a <code>@Version</code> field.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q11</div>🟡 <code>merge()</code> vs <code>persist()</code>?	<code>persist</code> makes a new (transient) instance managed, and that instance itself becomes managed. <code>merge</code> copies the state of a detached (or new) instance onto a managed instance, loading it if necessary, and <i>returns</i> the managed instance. The argument stays detached, so keep using the returned object.	ch19 part::VI purpose::practice
jim-ch19-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q12</div>🔴 How do you rename a column with zero downtime?	Expand–contract. Add the new column, deploy code that writes both and reads the old, backfill in batches, deploy code that reads the new column, stop writing the old one, then drop it in a later release. Every step is compatible with the code versions running side by side during a rolling deploy. Use concurrent index creation and lock timeouts to avoid blocking production traffic.	ch19 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch19-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q13</div>🟣 Read replicas were added and users complain that their changes "disappear" right after saving. Why, and what do you do?	Replication lag. The write goes to the primary, and the immediate read is routed to a replica that hasn't applied it yet. That breaks read-your-writes consistency. Options: route reads that follow a write (in the same session, or for N seconds after it) to the primary; use session tokens or LSN-based "read at least this position" routing where supported; return the written state directly from the write response; and monitor replication lag with alerting and automatic fallback to the primary when lag exceeds a threshold.	ch19 part::VI purpose::decisions
jim-ch19-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 19 · Persistence: JDBC, Transactions, JPA &amp; Hibernate · Q14</div>🟣 How would you build a reliable job queue without a message broker?	A jobs table with status, attempts, next-run time and a payload. Workers claim jobs with <code>SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED LIMIT n</code> inside a short transaction, mark them in progress (with a lease or heartbeat timestamp), process them, then mark them done or schedule a retry with backoff. Stuck jobs are recovered by lease expiry, and processing must be idempotent. Index the status and time columns, and archive or partition completed jobs. Libraries like db-scheduler and JobRunr implement this pattern.	ch19 part::VI purpose::decisions
jim-ch20-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q1</div>🟢 What's the difference between a mock, a stub, a spy and a fake?	A stub returns canned answers. A mock is set up with expectations about calls and verified afterwards (interaction testing). A spy wraps a real object and records calls. A fake is a lightweight working implementation (an in-memory repository). A dummy only fills a parameter. Prefer fakes and state verification for your own ports, and mocks for collaborators where the interaction itself is the behaviour.	ch20 part::VI purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch20-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q2</div>🟡 How does Mockito mock final classes, and why does JDK 21+ print a warning about it?	Mockito 5's default inline mock maker uses ByteBuddy plus the JVM instrumentation API to retransform classes at runtime, which allows mocking final classes and methods and, with <code>mockStatic</code>, static methods. Obtaining instrumentation by dynamically attaching an agent triggers JEP 451's warning, and future JDKs will block it by default. Configure Mockito as a <code>-javaagent</code> in the test JVM.	ch20 part::VI purpose::practice
jim-ch20-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q3</div>🔴 Why are Spring Boot test suites often slow, and how do you fix them?	Every distinct test context configuration (profiles, properties, <code>@MockitoBean</code> sets, imports) creates and caches a new application context, and many unique combinations mean many slow context startups. Others: a new container per test class, overuse of <code>@SpringBootTest</code>, and <code>@DirtiesContext</code>. Fixes: standardise a few shared test configurations, use slices, reuse singleton Testcontainers, avoid <code>@DirtiesContext</code>, run tests in parallel where they're isolated, and monitor context-cache statistics.	ch20 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch20-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q4</div>🟡 Why use Testcontainers instead of H2 for repository tests?	H2 isn't your production database: SQL dialects, functions, locking, isolation semantics, JSON support, constraint behaviour and query plans all differ, so tests can pass against H2 and fail in production (or the other way round). Testcontainers runs the same database engine and version as production in Docker, so tests exercise real SQL, migrations and constraints.	ch20 part::VI purpose::practice
jim-ch20-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q5</div>🔴 What problem do consumer-driven contract tests solve?	They catch breaking API changes between services before deployment, without a shared E2E environment. Consumers publish contracts describing the interactions and fields they actually use, and providers verify all consumer contracts in their own CI, so a provider change that breaks a consumer fails the provider's build. That enables independent deployability.	ch20 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch20-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q6</div>🔴 Why is line coverage a weak quality metric? What's better?	Coverage shows which lines executed, not whether behaviour was verified. Tests without meaningful assertions still score 100%. Mutation testing (PIT) checks whether tests fail when the code is deliberately broken, so the mutation score measures assertion strength. Combine it with outcome metrics such as escaped defects and change-failure rate.	ch20 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch20-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q7</div>🔴 What are the main pitfalls of hand-written Java microbenchmarks, and how does JMH help?	Dead-code elimination (results that aren't used get optimised away), constant folding, warmup and OSR effects, JIT profile pollution between benchmarks, loop-specific optimisations, GC noise and CPU scaling. JMH forks JVMs, runs proper warmup and measurement iterations, provides <code>Blackhole</code> and <code>@State</code> to defeat elimination and folding, and reports statistics with error bounds. It also integrates profilers to explain results.	ch20 part::VI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch20-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q8</div>🟡 What is property-based testing? Give an example property.	You state invariants that must hold for all inputs, and the framework (jqwik in Java) generates many random inputs and shrinks failures to minimal cases. Example: allocating money into N parts returns N shares whose sum equals the original amount and whose values differ by at most one cent. Other classics are encode/decode round-trips and idempotence.	ch20 part::VI purpose::practice
jim-ch20-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q9</div>🟣 How do you deal with a test suite that has become flaky and slow?	Measure first: flake rate per test, duration per test, context-cache statistics, container start times. Quarantine flaky tests with owners and deadlines, and fix root causes (time injection, Awaitility instead of sleeps, isolated data, deterministic ordering). For speed: share containers and contexts, move logic tests down to fast unit tests, use slices, parallelise isolated tests, split the CI stages (fast PR checks, deeper nightly runs). Track CI time and flake rate as team-level metrics with budgets.	ch20 part::VI purpose::decisions
jim-ch20-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 20 · Testing &amp; Quality · Q10</div>🟣 What test strategy would you propose for a 40-service platform?	Per service: unit tests for domain logic, plus the bulk as service-level integration tests with Testcontainers (real DB and broker) and WireMock for external APIs. Between services: consumer-driven contracts and schema compatibility checks in CI, instead of a large shared E2E environment. A thin layer of critical-journey E2E and synthetic tests. In production: canaries with automated rollback, feature flags and chaos experiments. Platform support through shared test starters, Testcontainers images, contract broker infrastructure and CI time budgets.	ch20 part::VI purpose::decisions
jim-ch21-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q1</div>🟡 What's Little's law and how do you use it?	L = λ × W: average concurrency equals arrival rate times average time in the system, for any stable system. Use it to size pools (500 RPS × 0.2 s = 100 concurrent requests in flight), derive maximum throughput from concurrency limits (50 workers ÷ 0.25 s = 200 RPS), and spot hidden queueing (if measured concurrency rises at the same RPS, latency rose).	ch21 part::VII purpose::practice
jim-ch21-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q2</div>🔴 Why does latency explode as utilization approaches 100%?	Queueing: random arrivals sometimes cluster, and when the server is almost always busy, clusters can't drain, so waiting time grows non-linearly. In an M/M/1 model, queue wait = service time × ρ/(1−ρ): 1× at 50%, 9× at 90%, 99× at 99%. That's why services and databases need headroom and autoscaling targets of 60–70%.	ch21 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch21-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q3</div>🔴 Why are averages bad for latency, and why can't you average p99s across instances?	Averages hide tails: a small fraction of very slow requests barely moves the mean but defines user experience, especially with fan-out where one slow dependency slows the whole request. Percentiles aren't additive: the mean of per-instance p99s isn't the fleet p99, since it depends on each instance's full distribution and traffic share. Merge histograms (HdrHistogram, Prometheus buckets) and compute percentiles from the combined distribution.	ch21 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch21-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q4</div>🔴 What is coordinated omission?	Closed-loop load generators wait for each response before sending the next request, so during a server stall they stop sending, and the latency that requests <i>would</i> have experienced is never measured. Reported tails can be understated by orders of magnitude. Use open-model, constant-arrival-rate generators (wrk2, Gatling open injection, k6 arrival-rate executors) that measure from the intended send time.	ch21 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch21-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q5</div>🔴 What is safepoint bias and which profilers avoid it?	Profilers that can only sample threads at safepoints attribute time to code near safepoint polls, so hot code without polls (counted loops) is under- or mis-reported. JFR's execution sampler and async-profiler (using <code>AsyncGetCallTrace</code> and <code>perf_events</code>) sample at arbitrary points and avoid it.	ch21 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch21-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q6</div>🟡 How do you read a flame graph?	Each box is a function; its width is the fraction of samples that include it, and stacks grow upward with the running code at the top edge. The x-axis is sorted alphabetically, <i>not</i> time. Look for wide top edges (self time) and unexpectedly wide subtrees. Use differential flame graphs to compare before and after.	ch21 part::VII purpose::practice
jim-ch21-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q7</div>🔴 CPU is low but latency is high. How do you investigate?	The time is spent waiting, not computing. Use distributed traces to find the slow span or dependency; wall-clock profiling and several thread dumps to find where threads wait (locks, pool acquisition, socket reads, <code>Future.join</code>); GC logs for pauses that line up with spikes; queue metrics for executors and connection pools; and CPU throttling metrics. Common causes are missing timeouts on a slow downstream service, connection-pool exhaustion, lock contention with I/O under a lock, and pool starvation from nested blocking.	ch21 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch21-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q8</div>🔴 How do you map a hot OS thread to a Java stack?	<code>top -H -p &lt;pid&gt;</code> shows per-thread CPU and the native thread ID. Find that ID in a <code>jcmd &lt;pid&gt; Thread.print</code> dump: recent JDKs (21 included) print it in decimal as <code>[tid]</code> and <code>nid=tid</code>, while JDK 17 and earlier print <code>nid</code> in hex, so convert first (<code>printf '%x' &lt;tid&gt;</code>). A CPU profiler (async-profiler or JFR) gives the same answer aggregated over time, which is usually more reliable than a single snapshot.	ch21 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch21-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q9</div>🟡 Explain Amdahl's law with a practical example.	Speedup is limited by the serial fraction: 1/((1−p) + p/N). If 10% of each request is serialized behind a global lock (or a single-threaded dependency), the maximum speedup is 10× no matter how many cores or instances you add. So removing serialization points (locks, single-writer bottlenecks, centralized coordinators) often matters more than adding hardware.	ch21 part::VII purpose::practice
jim-ch21-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 21 · Performance Engineering &amp; Troubleshooting · Q10</div>🔵 How would you set up performance guardrails for a large engineering organisation?	SLOs with per-hop latency budgets and timeouts. Continuous profiling and JFR on in production. Dashboards built on USE and RED. Nightly or pre-release load tests with an open workload model and trend tracking. JMH for performance-critical libraries. Query-count and allocation assertions in integration tests. Capacity models per service with known per-instance knees. Cost per request as a tracked metric. And a norm that performance work starts with a profile and ends with a measured, documented result.	ch21 part::VII purpose::execution
jim-ch22-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q1</div>🟡 What's the difference between liveness and readiness probes, and what's the classic mistake?	Liveness asks "is this process irreparably broken?", and failing it restarts the container. Readiness asks "can it serve traffic now?", and failing it only removes the pod from load balancing. The classic mistake is putting dependency checks (like the database) in liveness: a dependency blip then restarts the whole fleet at once, causing a cascading outage. Dependencies belong in readiness (carefully); liveness checks internal health only.	ch22 part::VII purpose::practice
jim-ch22-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q2</div>🔴 How do you implement zero-downtime graceful shutdown for a Spring Boot service on Kubernetes?	A <code>preStop</code> sleep (5–10 s) so endpoint removal propagates before draining. On SIGTERM: mark readiness as refusing traffic, stop accepting new requests and drain in-flight ones (<code>server.shutdown=graceful</code>, a per-phase timeout), stop message consumers and commit offsets, finish or hand off scheduled work, close pools and flush telemetry, then exit before <code>terminationGracePeriodSeconds</code> (set it longer than preStop plus the drain time). Consumers must be idempotent in case of redelivery.	ch22 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch22-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q3</div>🔴 Explain the circuit breaker pattern and its states.	It wraps calls to a dependency and tracks failure and slow-call rates over a sliding window. CLOSED: calls pass through. When the rate crosses a threshold (after a minimum number of calls), it goes OPEN: calls fail immediately (fail fast, saving resources and giving the dependency room to recover). After a wait, HALF_OPEN lets a few trial calls through. Success closes it, failure reopens it. Combine it with timeouts, fallbacks and per-dependency configuration.	ch22 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch22-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q4</div>🔴 Why can retries make an outage worse, and how do you retry safely?	Retries multiply load exactly when a dependency is struggling: nested retries across layers multiply (3 layers × 3 attempts = 27×), and synchronized retries create thundering herds. Retry safely by retrying only transient errors on idempotent operations (or with idempotency keys), at one layer, with capped attempts, exponential backoff with full jitter, retry budgets, <code>Retry-After</code> respected, and a circuit breaker to stop retrying a dead dependency.	ch22 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch22-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q5</div>🔴 What is metric cardinality and why does it matter?	Each unique combination of metric name and tag values is a separate time series. Unbounded tags (user IDs, order IDs, raw URIs) create millions of series, which blows up memory and cost in the metrics backend and slows queries, sometimes taking monitoring down. Keep tags bounded (route templates, status classes, regions) and put high-cardinality identifiers in traces and logs.	ch22 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch22-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q6</div>🟡 Why should latency SLOs use histograms rather than client-side percentiles?	Client-side (per-instance) percentiles can't be aggregated: averaging p99s across instances is mathematically wrong. Histogram buckets can be summed across instances and time, and the backend computes accurate fleet-wide percentiles and SLO compliance (the proportion of requests under a threshold) from them.	ch22 part::VII purpose::practice
jim-ch22-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q7</div>🔴 How does trace context propagate, and where does it commonly break in Java?	Across processes, the W3C <code>traceparent</code> header (HTTP, gRPC metadata, Kafka record headers) carries the trace ID, parent span ID and flags. Inside the JVM, the context is thread-bound, so it breaks at thread hops: custom executors, <code>CompletableFuture</code> async stages, Reactor schedulers, manually created threads (virtual threads included), and messaging libraries without instrumentation. Fix it with context-propagation-aware executors and wrappers, the OTel agent's instrumentation, or Micrometer's context propagation.	ch22 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch22-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q8</div>🟣 Explain error budgets and burn-rate alerting.	The error budget is the allowed unreliability implied by an SLO (99.9% over 28 days ≈ 40 minutes of full outage equivalent). Teams spend it on risk (releases, experiments), and when it runs out, reliability work takes priority by policy. Burn-rate alerts page when the budget is being consumed much faster than sustainable (for example 14.4× over 1 h, confirmed by a 5-minute window), which catches real incidents quickly without paging on blips. Slower burns create tickets.	ch22 part::VII purpose::decisions
jim-ch22-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q9</div>🔴 What's load shedding and why is it better than queueing under overload?	Rejecting excess requests early (429/503 with <code>Retry-After</code>) when the service is beyond capacity. Queueing past capacity just adds latency until requests time out, consuming resources for work that fails anyway and pushing tail latency up for everyone. Shedding keeps latency healthy for the requests you accept, lets clients back off or fail over, and can prioritise important traffic. Adaptive concurrency limits automate where the cutoff is.	ch22 part::VII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch22-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 22 · Production Readiness, Observability &amp; Resilience · Q10</div>🔵 You're the incident commander: checkout error rate jumped to 20% right after a deploy. What do you do?	Mitigate first: halt the rollout and roll back (or disable the feature flag), given the timing correlation. Assign roles (comms posts status updates, someone captures evidence: dumps, JFR, logs, traces of failing requests). Confirm recovery via the SLIs. Then investigate with the evidence: the deploy diff, config changes, dependency versions, error logs by code, traces. Afterwards, a blameless postmortem: why canary analysis didn't catch it, why the change reached 100%, plus action items with owners.	ch22 part::VII purpose::execution
jim-ch23-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q1</div>🟡 When would you NOT use microservices?	With a small team or a product still finding its domain boundaries, when you lack platform maturity (CI/CD, observability, on-call), or when strong cross-entity transactions are central to the domain. A well-structured modular monolith gives enforced boundaries and simple ACID consistency at a fraction of the operational cost. Extract services when there's a concrete need for independent scaling, deployability, isolation or technology.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::practice
jim-ch23-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q2</div>🔴 Explain CAP and PACELC with examples.	CAP: during a network partition, a replicated system must sacrifice either linearizable consistency or availability. PACELC adds that even without partitions there's a trade-off between latency and consistency. A single-leader RDBMS with synchronous replication is PC/EC. Cassandra or DynamoDB with default settings are PA/EL (tunable). Spanner pays latency for global consistency (PC/EC). The useful framing is per operation: which reads need which guarantee.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch23-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q3</div>🔴 What is the dual-write problem and how does the outbox pattern solve it?	Writing to the database and publishing to a broker are two separate operations without a shared transaction, so a crash or partial failure leaves them inconsistent (an event for a rolled-back change, or a change without its event). The outbox writes the event into an outbox table in the same local transaction as the state change, and a relay (a poller with <code>SKIP LOCKED</code>, or CDC like Debezium) publishes it afterwards. That gives at-least-once delivery, so consumers deduplicate by event ID.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch23-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q4</div>🔴 How do you implement idempotency for a payment API?	The client sends an <code>Idempotency-Key</code> per logical operation. The server <b>claims</b> the key first: it inserts a <code>PENDING</code> operation (key, request hash) under a unique constraint and commits <i>before</i> charging, so concurrent duplicates lose the insert and get <code>409</code> with <code>Retry-After</code>. Then it calls the processor with no transaction open, using a processor idempotency key that belongs to the operation, and records the outcome with a guarded update, plus ledger and outbox rows, in one transaction. Retries replay the stored response; the same key with a different payload gets <code>422</code>. A timeout leaves the operation <code>UNKNOWN</code>, never "failed": a reconciler re-sends with the <i>same</i> processor key while the processor remembers it, then looks the charge up by reference (an empty search keeps it <code>UNKNOWN</code>), and daily settlement reconciliation catches the rest. Checking for a saved response, charging, then saving is the classic bug: in the lab it charged 999 of 1,000 payments more than once under concurrent retries.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch23-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q5</div>🔴 Saga vs 2PC: why do microservices prefer sagas?	2PC blocks: participants hold locks while waiting for a coordinator that may fail. It couples everyone's availability, scales poorly, and isn't supported by brokers, most NoSQL stores or third-party APIs. A saga is a sequence of local transactions with compensating actions on failure: available and loosely coupled, but without isolation (intermediate states are visible, needing semantic locks), with compensations designed per step, idempotent steps, and durable saga state with timeouts.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch23-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q6</div>🔴 Orchestration vs choreography?	Choreography: services publish and react to events with no central coordinator. It's loosely coupled, but the flow is implicit and hard to trace as processes grow. Orchestration: a coordinator (an orchestrator service or workflow engine like Temporal) explicitly commands each step and handles compensation. The flow is visible and testable, at the cost of coupling to a coordinator. Choose orchestration for complex multi-step processes with compensation, and choreography for simple, independent reactions.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch23-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q7</div>🔴 What delivery guarantees does Kafka give, and what does "exactly-once" actually cover?	Ordering per partition. With commit-after-processing, consumers get at-least-once delivery (duplicates on crashes and rebalances). Exactly-once semantics use idempotent producers plus transactions to make a read-process-write cycle atomic <i>within Kafka</i> (output records and consumer offsets committed together, consumers reading <code>read_committed</code>). Side effects outside Kafka (databases, emails, HTTP calls) aren't covered and still need idempotent handling.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch23-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q8</div>🔴 Your Kafka consumers keep rebalancing and reprocessing messages. Why?	Most likely processing takes longer than <code>max.poll.interval.ms</code>, so the broker thinks the consumer died and reassigns its partitions. Others: session timeouts from GC pauses, frequent deploys without static membership, eager rebalancing. Fixes: smaller <code>max.poll.records</code>, faster or async processing that preserves per-key order, tuned intervals, cooperative sticky assignment, static group membership, and idempotent processing to make the remaining redeliveries harmless.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch23-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q9</div>🔴 How do you prevent a cache stampede?	Coalesce concurrent misses for the same key so only one loader hits the database while the others wait for its result (single-flight; Caffeine's <code>get(key, loader)</code> does this per key). Refresh hot keys ahead of expiry (<code>refreshAfterWrite</code> or probabilistic early expiration), jitter TTLs to avoid synchronized expiry, and protect the database with bulkheads and rate limits for cold-cache scenarios.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch23-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q10</div>🟣 Is a Redis lock safe for protecting a critical section across services?	Not for correctness on its own. A holder can pause (GC, VM stall) past the lease expiry while another client acquires the lock, so both act. Clock drift adds more failure modes (the core of Kleppmann's Redlock critique). For correctness, use fencing tokens checked by the protected resource, consensus-backed locks (etcd, ZooKeeper) with fencing, or redesign to avoid locks (single writer per key, conditional updates in the database). Lease locks are fine for efficiency, such as avoiding duplicate work that's itself idempotent.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch23-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q11</div>🟣 Why shouldn't you order events by timestamps from different services?	Wall clocks across machines drift and jump (NTP corrections, leap seconds, VM pauses), so timestamps can invert real causal order. Use a single sequencer per key (a database sequence, Kafka partition offsets, aggregate versions), logical clocks (Lamport, vector) when you need causality, or hybrid logical clocks in systems that support them.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch23-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 23 · Distributed Systems &amp; Microservices · Q12</div>🟣 How do you spot and fix a distributed monolith?	Symptoms: services that must be deployed together, shared databases or tables, long synchronous call chains on critical paths, a "core" service that every request touches, and features that always need changes across several services. Fixes: realign boundaries to bounded contexts, give each service its own data, replace synchronous queries with event-carried state or local read models, move workflows to explicit sagas, and track deploy coupling as a metric. Merging services back together is also a legitimate outcome.	ch23 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch24-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 24 · System Design for Java Architects · Q1</div>🟣 Design a rate limiter for a public API (key points).	Clarify: per API key or tenant limits, burst tolerance, global vs per-region. Use a token bucket (bursts plus a sustained rate) or a sliding-window counter. Place it at the gateway (or as a library or sidecar). State lives in Redis with atomic Lua scripts per key, optionally with local pre-checks to reduce the hot path. Return 429 with <code>Retry-After</code> and <code>RateLimit-*</code> headers. Decide fail-open vs fail-closed when Redis is unavailable. Handle hot tenants (shard keys) and multiple regions (local limits plus approximate global sync). Monitor rejections per tenant.	ch24 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch24-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 24 · System Design for Java Architects · Q2</div>🔴 Why does naive <code>hash(key) % N</code> sharding hurt when you add a node, and how does consistent hashing help?	Changing N changes the result for most keys. In our measurement, going from 3 to 4 nodes moved 75% of 100K keys, which means a cache-miss storm or a massive data migration. Consistent hashing places nodes and keys on a ring, and a key belongs to the next node clockwise, so adding a node only takes keys from its neighbours (about 1/N, 26% measured). Virtual nodes are needed for balance: with one vnode per node the split was 13% to 47%.	ch24 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch24-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 24 · System Design for Java Architects · Q3</div>🟣 How do you guarantee a customer is never charged twice?	Client idempotency keys claimed as a payment operation, under a unique constraint, <i>before</i> anything is charged. A persisted state machine with guarded transitions. One processor idempotency key per operation, reused on every re-send. Treating timeouts as <i>unknown</i>: re-send with the same key, or look the charge up by reference once the processor may have forgotten the key, treating an empty result as still unknown; never retry with a new key. Idempotent webhook processing (dedupe by event ID, verify signatures). Daily reconciliation against processor settlement reports to catch anything that slipped through.	ch24 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch24-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 24 · System Design for Java Architects · Q4</div>🟣 Fan-out on write vs fan-out on read for a news feed?	Fan-out on write precomputes each follower's timeline when a post is created: fast reads, expensive writes for accounts with millions of followers. Fan-out on read assembles the timeline at read time: cheap writes, expensive reads. Use a hybrid: fan-out on write for normal accounts, fan-out on read (merged at read time) for celebrity accounts, with cached timelines and cursor pagination.	ch24 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch24-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 24 · System Design for Java Architects · Q5</div>🟣 How would you migrate a monolith's Orders module to a separate service without downtime?	Strangler fig. Put a routing facade in front, build the new service, sync data from the legacy database via CDC, backfill history, verify with shadow reads and comparison, move reads gradually behind flags (by tenant or percentage), then move writes with a rollback path (sync back), then retire the legacy module. Keep APIs compatible throughout, record decisions in ADRs, and measure progress by traffic migrated and incidents avoided.	ch24 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch24-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 24 · System Design for Java Architects · Q6</div>🟣 Active-active multi-region: what's hard?	Concurrent writes to the same data in different regions. You need either synchronous cross-region coordination (high write latency) or conflict resolution (last-writer-wins loses updates; CRDTs only fit certain data types). Most systems assign each user or tenant a home region (partitioned active-active) and fail over per partition. Also hard: global uniqueness (IDs, usernames), cache invalidation across regions, data residency laws, and actually testing failover regularly.	ch24 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch24-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 24 · System Design for Java Architects · Q7</div>🔴 How do you prevent overselling in a flash sale with 1M users and 1,000 items?	Put a waiting room or queue in front to meter users in. Decrement inventory atomically (a conditional <code>UPDATE … WHERE stock &gt; 0</code>, or a single-writer partition or Redis <code>DECR</code> with a check) to create a time-limited reservation, and confirm on payment or release on expiry. Make checkout idempotent. Serve the product page from CDN and cache, and keep only the reservation path transactional. Load-test it, and have a kill switch.	ch24 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch24-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 24 · System Design for Java Architects · Q8</div>🟣 What's a cell-based architecture and why use it?	The system is split into many independent, identical cells, each a complete stack serving a subset of customers, with a thin routing layer mapping customers to cells. Failures, bad deploys, poison requests and noisy neighbours are contained to one cell's customers, which limits the blast radius. Deploys roll cell by cell. The costs are more infrastructure, routing complexity, and cross-cell operations (migrating customers between cells, global features).	ch24 part::VIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch25-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q1</div>🟢 How do you prevent SQL injection in Java, including in JPQL and dynamic sorting?	Always bind parameters (<code>PreparedStatement</code>, named parameters in JPQL, Criteria API, Spring Data). Never concatenate input into SQL or JPQL. Identifiers like <code>ORDER BY</code> columns and directions can't be bound, so map user input through an allow-list of known columns. Add defence in depth: a least-privilege database user, input validation, and monitoring.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch25-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q2</div>🔴 What is XXE and how do you prevent it in Java?	XML External Entity injection: a document declares external entities that the parser resolves, which lets attackers read local files, trigger SSRF or exhaust memory. Prevent it by hardening every XML factory: <code>disallow-doctype-decl</code> true, external general and parameter entities false, no external DTD loading, no XInclude, no entity expansion. Centralise parser creation, and prefer JSON for new APIs.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch25-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q3</div>🟡 How should passwords be stored?	With a slow, salted, adaptive password-hashing function (Argon2id, bcrypt, scrypt, or PBKDF2 with a high work factor), never fast hashes (MD5, SHA-*) or reversible encryption. Use Spring Security's <code>DelegatingPasswordEncoder</code> so the algorithm ID is stored with the hash and you can migrate algorithms by re-hashing on login.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::practice
jim-ch25-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q4</div>🔴 Which OAuth flow would you use for a SPA, a mobile app and a backend batch job?	SPA and mobile: Authorization Code with PKCE (implicit and password grants are deprecated). For browser apps, consider a backend-for-frontend holding tokens server-side with <code>HttpOnly</code> session cookies. Batch job (no user): Client Credentials, authenticating with a private-key JWT, mTLS or a vaulted secret, with narrowly scoped access.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch25-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q5</div>🔴 What are the most dangerous JWT validation mistakes?	Accepting <code>alg: none</code>, letting the token header pick the algorithm (RS256→HS256 confusion), not validating <code>aud</code>/<code>iss</code>/<code>exp</code>/<code>nbf</code>, long-lived tokens with no revocation strategy, putting sensitive data in the (merely signed) payload, storing tokens in <code>localStorage</code>, and hard-coded keys with no JWKS rotation.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch25-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q6</div>🔴 What is IDOR and how do you prevent it systematically?	Insecure Direct Object Reference: an endpoint takes an object ID and returns or modifies it without checking that the caller may access it. Prevent it by enforcing ownership or tenancy in the data access itself (<code>findByIdAndOwnerId</code>), denying by default, using method security for complex rules, scoping queries by tenant (plus database row-level security), returning 404 for objects the caller can't see, and writing automated tests that try cross-user and cross-tenant access.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch25-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q7</div>🔴 Why AES-GCM, and what's the one mistake that breaks it completely?	GCM is authenticated encryption: it provides confidentiality and integrity, and any tampering makes decryption fail, which unauthenticated modes like ECB or plain CBC don't. The catastrophic mistake is reusing an IV (nonce) with the same key, which leaks the XOR of plaintexts and lets attackers forge messages. Use a random 96-bit IV per message (or a deterministic unique counter), and rotate keys well before nonce-collision limits.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch25-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q8</div>🟡 Why is <code>java.util.Random</code> unsuitable for tokens, and why does comparing HMACs with <code>equals</code> matter?	<code>Random</code> is a predictable linear congruential generator: observing outputs lets an attacker predict future values. Use <code>SecureRandom</code>. <code>String.equals</code>/<code>Arrays.equals</code> return as soon as a byte differs, so response timing leaks how many leading bytes matched, which lets an attacker brute-force a MAC byte by byte. <code>MessageDigest.isEqual</code> compares in constant time.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::practice
jim-ch25-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q9</div>🔴 How do you defend against SSRF in a "fetch this URL" feature?	Allow-list destinations if possible. Otherwise resolve DNS once, reject private, loopback, link-local and metadata IP ranges, and connect to the validated IP (to defeat DNS rebinding). Handle redirects manually with re-validation, restrict schemes to HTTP(S), add timeouts and size limits, enforce egress controls at the network layer, and run the feature in an isolated service with minimal network reach.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch25-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 25 · Security for Java Engineers · Q10</div>🔵 A critical CVE like Log4Shell drops. Walk through your organisation's response.	Triage severity and exploitability. Query SBOMs and the artifact repository for affected versions, including transitive and shaded copies, and scan running images. Apply immediate mitigations (WAF rules, configuration flags, egress blocks) while patching: bump via the platform BOM, rebuild, and redeploy by risk tier (internet-facing first). Verify with scanners and monitoring for exploitation attempts. Communicate status. Afterwards, run a postmortem on detection speed, patch velocity and the inventory gaps, and invest in what was slow.	ch25 part::VIII purpose::execution
jim-ch26-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q1</div>🟡 Why <code>ArrayDeque</code> instead of <code>Stack</code> or <code>LinkedList</code>?	<code>Stack</code> extends <code>Vector</code>, so every operation is synchronized, and it carries a legacy API (its iteration order is bottom-to-top, the opposite of <code>ArrayDeque</code>'s). <code>LinkedList</code> allocates a node per element and has poor cache locality. <code>ArrayDeque</code> is a resizable circular array with O(1) operations at both ends. Its one restriction is no <code>null</code> elements.	ch26 part::IX purpose::practice
jim-ch26-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q2</div>🟡 What's wrong with <code>(a, b) -> a - b</code> as a comparator?	Integer subtraction overflows. For example, <code>1 - Integer.MIN_VALUE</code> wraps to a negative number, so the comparator claims 1 is less than <code>MIN_VALUE</code>. Sorting <code>[3, MIN_VALUE, -5, MAX_VALUE, 0]</code> with it produced <code>[0, 3, 2147483647, -2147483648, -5]</code>. Use <code>Integer.compare(a, b)</code> or <code>Comparator.comparingInt(...)</code>.	ch26 part::IX purpose::practice
jim-ch26-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q3</div>🟡 Two counts in a <code>HashMap&lt;String, Integer&gt;</code> are both 1000, but <code>map.get(a) == map.get(b)</code> is false. Why?	<code>==</code> compares <code>Integer</code> references. Autoboxing caches only the values −128 to 127 (by default), so two boxed 1000s are different objects. 127 compared equal and 1000 didn't in our run. Compare with <code>equals</code>, or unbox to <code>int</code> first.	ch26 part::IX purpose::practice
jim-ch26-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q4</div>🔴 Why mark BFS nodes visited when enqueuing rather than when dequeuing?	If you mark on dequeue, a node can be enqueued once by every neighbour that discovers it before it's processed. On dense graphs or grids, the queue then holds many duplicates, inflating time and memory, and sometimes pushing correct code past the time limit. Marking on enqueue guarantees each node enters the queue once, and the recorded distance is still the shortest, because BFS discovers nodes in nondecreasing distance order.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q5</div>🔴 How do you implement Dijkstra in Java without decrease-key?	Push a new <code>(node, distance)</code> entry whenever you find a shorter distance, and when polling, skip entries whose distance is greater than the best known (<code>if (d &gt; dist[node]) continue;</code>). That's lazy deletion. <code>PriorityQueue.remove(Object)</code> is O(n), so removing stale entries eagerly would ruin the complexity. The result is O((V + E) log V), with a heap that may hold up to E entries.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q6</div>🔴 Memoised Fibonacci with <code>memo.computeIfAbsent(n, k -> fib(k-1) + fib(k-2))</code> throws. Why, and what's the fix?	The mapping function recursively calls <code>computeIfAbsent</code> on the same <code>HashMap</code>, which modifies the map during the outer computation. Since Java 9, <code>HashMap</code> detects this and throws <code>ConcurrentModificationException</code>. <code>ConcurrentHashMap</code> is no escape: on JDK 21 the same code threw <code>IllegalStateException: Recursive update</code>, and on Java 8 it could hang forever. Fix it with explicit get-compute-put (<code>Long v = memo.get(n); if (v == null) { v = fib(n-1) + fib(n-2); memo.put(n, v); }</code>), or use an array memo or bottom-up DP.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q7</div>🔴 In a bounded blocking queue, why <code>while</code> instead of <code>if</code> around <code>await()</code>, and why two conditions?	<code>while</code>: a waiting thread can wake spuriously, or wake after another thread has already consumed the state change, so the condition must be rechecked after waking. Two conditions: producers wait on <code>notFull</code> and consumers on <code>notEmpty</code>, so a <code>signal()</code> always wakes a thread that can make progress. With a single wait set you'd need <code>notifyAll()</code> to avoid waking the wrong kind of thread and losing the wake-up.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q8</div>🔴 Three threads print 1..N in turn using <code>wait</code>/<code>notify</code>. What goes wrong with <code>notify()</code>?	<code>notify()</code> wakes one arbitrary waiting thread. If it wakes a thread whose turn it isn't, that thread rechecks its condition and goes back to waiting, while the thread that should run is never woken. Everyone ends up waiting: a lost wake-up and a liveness failure. <code>notifyAll()</code> wakes everyone, and each rechecks in its <code>while</code> loop. Alternatives are one <code>Condition</code> or <code>Semaphore</code> per thread, signalled explicitly.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q9</div>🔴 How do you prevent deadlock in dining philosophers? Give three approaches.	Break one of the Coffman conditions. (1) <b>Lock ordering:</b> always acquire the lower-numbered fork first, which breaks circular wait. Our measured run finished all 5,000,000 meals, while the naive version deadlocked after somewhere between 57K and 232K meals. (2) <b>A waiter:</b> a <code>Semaphore(n − 1)</code> allows at most n − 1 philosophers to reach for forks, so at least one can always eat. (3) <b><code>tryLock</code> with timeout and randomised back-off:</b> a philosopher who can't get the second fork releases the first, which breaks no preemption, at the risk of livelock or starvation.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q10</div>🔴 In your thread pool, how do you guarantee no task is stranded in the queue at shutdown?	Make <code>submit</code> (check the flag, then offer) and <code>shutdown</code> (set the flag) mutually exclusive with the same monitor, so after <code>shutdown</code> no new task can enter. A worker exits only when it observes <code>shutdown == true</code> <b>and</b> the queue is empty. Because every accepted offer happened-before the shutdown write, which happened-before the worker's volatile read, that condition is stable: once true, nothing can arrive later. <code>shutdownNow</code> instead drains the queue, returns the pending tasks, and interrupts the workers.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q11</div>🔴 Why can't the crawler simply rely on <code>try (var exec = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor())</code> to wait for all pages?	<code>close()</code> calls <code>shutdown()</code> before waiting. Tasks still running at that point would try to submit child tasks and get <code>RejectedExecutionException</code>, so the crawl would be cut short. You need your own completion signal: a pending counter incremented before each submit and decremented in <code>finally</code>, completing a future when it hits zero. Because children increment before their parent decrements, the counter can't reach zero early.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q12</div>🔴 Why can't you protect an access-ordered <code>LinkedHashMap</code> LRU with a <code>ReadWriteLock</code>?	In access-order mode, <code>get()</code> is a structural modification: it moves the entry to the end of the linked list. Concurrent "readers" holding a shared read lock would mutate the list simultaneously and corrupt it (lost nodes, cycles, or exceptions). Every operation needs exclusive access, so use a single lock, or a design that defers recency updates, as Caffeine does with read buffers drained under a lock.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q13</div>🟣 In the TTL cache, why <code>map.remove(key, expiredEntry)</code> instead of <code>map.remove(key)</code>?	Between reading the expired entry and removing it, another thread may <code>put</code> a fresh value for the same key. An unconditional <code>remove(key)</code> would delete that fresh value, silently losing a write. The two-argument <code>remove</code> is an atomic compare-and-remove: it deletes only if the key still maps to the exact entry we saw expire. It's the same check-then-act race as in any concurrent map code, solved with the map's atomic operations.	ch26 part::IX purpose::decisions
jim-ch26-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q14</div>🔴 How would you extend the parking lot with reservations and multiple entry gates?	Gates already work concurrently, because allocation is an atomic <code>poll</code> from concurrent free lists and exit is an atomic <code>remove</code> of the ticket. For reservations, give spots a state machine (FREE → HELD(until) → OCCUPIED → FREE), and make the hold a timed claim that a sweeper releases on expiry (or check lazily on arrival). Reserved spots are removed from the free list at hold time. Pricing for reservations is another strategy. For a multi-lot or distributed version, move the state to a database with atomic conditional updates (<code>UPDATE spot SET state = 'HELD' WHERE id = ? AND state = 'FREE'</code>) or <code>SKIP LOCKED</code> allocation.	ch26 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch26-q015	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 26 · Coding &amp; LLD Interview Patterns · Q15</div>🟣 How would you evolve the in-memory broker to at-least-once delivery that survives restarts?	Replace the per-subscriber queues with a durable, append-only log per topic (or per partition) and have each consumer group track a committed offset. Deliver from the offset, and commit only after the handler succeeds, so a crash means redelivery, not loss. Handlers must be idempotent (dedupe by message ID). Add retries with back-off and a dead-letter topic for poison messages, retention by time or size, and partitioning by key for parallelism with per-key ordering. That's the Kafka model, and saying so, with the trade-offs, is the expected finish.	ch26 part::IX purpose::decisions
jim-ch27-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q1</div>🔵 "Walk me through the most complex system you've designed." How do you structure it?	Use the deep-dive arc: context → problem (with numbers) → constraints → options considered → decision and rationale → execution (your role) → measured results → hindsight. Draw a C4 container diagram early, name the one hard part explicitly, and pre-empt drill-downs with the alternatives you rejected. Keep the first pass to about 10 minutes, then let the interviewer steer into depth.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch27-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q2</div>🔵 "Tell me about a time you disagreed with a technical decision."	Strong answers show that you (1) understood the other side's goals and constraints, (2) argued with evidence (data, a prototype, risk analysis) through the proper channel (an RFC or design review), (3) accepted the outcome, whether that meant changing your mind or disagreeing and committing, and (4) followed up on results. Avoid stories where you were simply right and others were foolish. Interviewers look for respect, rigour and the ability to move forward.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch27-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q3</div>🔵 "How do you influence teams that don't report to you?"	Build credibility by delivering and by helping. Understand their incentives and constraints. Make the desired path the easiest one (paved roads, automation, starters). Bring data instead of opinions. Pre-wire decisions in one-on-ones. Write clear RFCs. Give credit generously. Escalate rarely, and with options. Illustrate it with a real story with measured adoption.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch27-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q4</div>🔵 "How would you upgrade 200 services from Java 11 to Java 25?"	Inventory first (versions, <code>jdeps --jdk-internals</code>, <code>jdeprscan --for-removal</code>, high-risk bytecode and reflection libraries). Pave the road (base images, a BOM, a nightly next-JDK CI build, OpenRewrite <code>UpgradeToJava25</code> plus the Spring Boot recipes). Decouple the risk: build on the new JDK → run on it → bump the target → adopt features. Roll out in waves (pilot → early adopters → automated bulk → long tail) with canaries comparing p99, GC, CPU and RSS. Make it stick with a dashboard, a CI gate, a support policy and automation, so the next upgrade is trivial. Measure fleet coverage, effort, incidents and CVE patch latency.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch27-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q5</div>🟣 "Virtual threads or WebFlux for our new service?"	Ask about the workload first: request/response or streaming, the downstream dependencies (JDBC? blocking SDKs?), the team's skills, the JDK version. For request/response on JDK 21+ (ideally 24+, where <code>synchronized</code> pinning is fixed), default to virtual threads with Spring MVC, plus explicit concurrency limits toward downstreams. Choose reactive for streaming and end-to-end backpressure, or for an existing reactive estate. Validate with a load test under realistic latency, and check pinning with JFR.	ch27 part::IX purpose::decisions
jim-ch27-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q6</div>🔵 "Tell me about a significant failure."	Pick a real failure with real consequences, not a humble-brag. Own your part plainly, describe what you did during the incident, the systemic fixes (not just "we were more careful"), and how your behaviour changed afterwards. Blameless framing about others, accountable framing about yourself. Interviewers are testing self-awareness and whether failures make you better.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch27-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q7</div>🔵 "How do you balance technical debt against feature work?"	Make debt visible and quantified: tie it to incidents, lead time, cost or onboarding time. Then prioritise by the "interest" it charges, not by aesthetics. Reserve continuous capacity rather than holding occasional "debt sprints". Bundle paydown with roadmap work that touches the same code. Use ADRs to record debt taken on deliberately, with exit criteria. Talk in business terms with product partners.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch27-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q8</div>🟣 "How do you know your architecture is working?"	Use outcome metrics: SLOs and error budgets, the DORA metrics (deployment frequency, lead time for changes, change failure rate, time to restore), cost per unit of business volume, and developer-experience signals (build times, onboarding time, surveys). Add <b>fitness functions</b>, automated checks that guard architectural properties (ArchUnit rules, latency budgets in CI, dependency rules). Review them periodically, and treat a regression as an architectural signal.	ch27 part::IX purpose::decisions
jim-ch27-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q9</div>🟣 "How do you decide with incomplete data?"	Classify reversibility. Two-way doors get decided quickly, with a measurement plan and a review date. One-way doors earn more investigation (prototypes, spikes, expert review). Make your assumptions explicit, define what evidence would change the decision, and time-box the analysis. A good decision now usually beats a perfect one later. Document it in an ADR so the reasoning survives.	ch27 part::IX purpose::decisions
jim-ch27-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q10</div>🔵 "What would you do in your first 90 days here?"	Listen (one-on-ones, postmortems, ADRs, on-call shadowing, a system map, one small shipped change). Then diagnose (a state-of-the-architecture note with evidence, validated with stakeholders, with sponsorship for 1–2 high-leverage problems). Then deliver and set direction (early wins, a strategy draft, review rhythms, measurable goals). Avoid arriving with a prescription before you understand the context.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch27-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q11</div>🔵 "How do you grow senior engineers toward staff?"	Give them scope with support: delegate real, ambiguous problems instead of tasks, and coach on framing, writing and influence (review their RFCs, pre-wire with them, let them present). Sponsor them: put their names on the work in rooms they aren't in. Give specific, timely feedback against the staff rubric. Measure success by their promotions and by the problems they now solve without you.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch27-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 27 · The Staff &amp; Architect Interview Playbook · Q12</div>🔵 "Tell me about saying no to a senior stakeholder."	Show that you understood the underlying goal, quantified the cost or risk of the request (with data), offered alternatives that met the real need (a smaller scope, phasing, a different approach), and reached a decision together. Escalate respectfully if needed. Close with the outcome and how the relationship stayed intact or improved. "No, but here's how we get you what you actually need" is the pattern.	ch27 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch28-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q1</div>🟢 JDK vs JRE vs JVM?	The <b>JVM</b> executes bytecode: it's a specification, and HotSpot is the main implementation. The <b>JRE</b> is the JVM plus the class libraries needed to run programs. The <b>JDK</b> adds the development tools (<code>javac</code>, <code>jar</code>, <code>jdeps</code>, <code>jlink</code>, <code>jcmd</code>, <code>jfr</code>). Oracle stopped shipping a standalone JRE with JDK 11; today you build a trimmed runtime with <code>jlink</code> (some vendors still publish JRE builds). → Ch 01	ch28 part::IX purpose::fundamentals topic::ch01
jim-ch28-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q2</div>🟢 Is Java pass-by-value or pass-by-reference?	Always pass-by-value. For objects, the value copied is the <i>reference</i>. Reassigning the parameter inside a method doesn't affect the caller's variable, but mutating the object it points to is visible to the caller. → Ch 01	ch28 part::IX purpose::fundamentals topic::ch01
jim-ch28-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q3</div>🟡 Why is <code>0.1 + 0.2 != 0.3</code>, and how should you represent money?	IEEE 754 binary floating point can't represent 0.1 exactly, so tiny representation errors accumulate. For money, use <code>BigDecimal</code> (created from a <code>String</code> or <code>BigDecimal.valueOf</code>, never <code>new BigDecimal(0.1)</code>) with an explicit scale and rounding mode, or <code>long</code> minor units with a currency. → Ch 01	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch01
jim-ch28-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q4</div>🟡 What does <code>(int) 3_000_000_000L</code> evaluate to?	<code>-1294967296</code>. A narrowing primitive conversion keeps only the low 32 bits (3,000,000,000 − 2³² = −1,294,967,296), and no exception is thrown. Use <code>Math.toIntExact</code> when you want overflow to fail loudly. → Ch 01	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch01
jim-ch28-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q5</div>🔴 What are the class-file major versions for Java 21 and 25, and why do they matter?	65 for Java 21 and 69 for Java 25 (52 = Java 8, 55 = 11, 61 = 17, 71 = 27). An older JVM rejects newer class files with <code>UnsupportedClassVersionError</code>. Compile with <code>--release N</code> to target an older runtime. Unlike <code>-source</code>/<code>-target</code>, it also checks that you only use APIs that exist in that release. → Ch 01	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms topic::ch01
jim-ch28-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q6</div>🟢 Overloading vs overriding: when is each resolved?	<b>Overloading</b> is resolved at compile time from the <i>static</i> types of the arguments, in three phases: without boxing or varargs, then with boxing, then with varargs. <b>Overriding</b> is resolved at run time from the <i>dynamic</i> type of the receiver (virtual dispatch). → Ch 02	ch28 part::IX purpose::fundamentals topic::ch02
jim-ch28-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q7</div>🟡 State the <code>equals</code>/<code>hashCode</code> contract and what breaks if you violate it.	<code>equals</code> must be reflexive, symmetric, transitive and consistent, with <code>x.equals(null) == false</code>, and equal objects <b>must</b> have equal hash codes. Violate it and hash-based collections misbehave: lookups miss, duplicates appear, and mutating a field used in <code>hashCode</code> after insertion "loses" the entry. → Ch 02	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch02
jim-ch28-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q8</div>🟡 Why can't you subclass a value class, add a field, and keep <code>equals</code> correct?	With <code>instanceof</code>-based equality you break symmetry (point equals colorPoint, but not the reverse). With <code>getClass()</code>-based equality you break Liskov substitution (a subclass instance never equals its parent). No implementation satisfies the contract and substitutability together. Favour composition, or make value classes final (records are). → Ch 02	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch02
jim-ch28-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q9</div>🟡 What does a record give you, and what doesn't it?	It gives you private final fields, a canonical constructor, accessors, and <code>equals</code>/<code>hashCode</code>/<code>toString</code> derived from the components; the class is implicitly final. It does <b>not</b> give deep immutability: a <code>List</code> component stays mutable unless you defensively copy it in a compact constructor (<code>items = List.copyOf(items)</code>). You also can't declare extra instance fields. → Ch 02	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch02
jim-ch28-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q10</div>🔴 What's the payoff of sealed interfaces + records + pattern matching?	Algebraic data types in Java. A sealed hierarchy is closed, so a <code>switch</code> over it can be <b>exhaustive without a <code>default</code></b>, and adding a new subtype turns every incomplete switch into a compile error, instead of a runtime surprise. Records add deconstruction patterns for concise, type-safe handling. → Ch 02	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms topic::ch02
jim-ch28-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q11</div>🟢 Why are Strings immutable?	Security (class names, file paths and URLs can't change after being checked), thread safety without locks, a cacheable <code>hashCode</code> (strings make good map keys), and safe sharing through the string pool. → Ch 03	ch28 part::IX purpose::fundamentals topic::ch03
jim-ch28-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q12</div>🟡 <code>new String("a") == "a"</code>? And <code>"a" + "b" == "ab"</code>?	The first is <code>false</code>: <code>new</code> creates a distinct heap object, while the literal comes from the pool. The second is <code>true</code>, because two literals are constant-folded at compile time into one interned <code>"ab"</code>. Concatenation involving a non-final variable happens at run time and produces a new object. → Ch 03	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch03
jim-ch28-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q13</div>🟡 What are compact strings?	Since JDK 9 (JEP 254), a <code>String</code> stores its characters in a <code>byte[]</code> plus a <i>coder</i> flag: LATIN1 (1 byte per char) when every character fits, otherwise UTF16 (2 bytes). That roughly halves the memory of mostly-ASCII text without any API change. → Ch 03	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch03
jim-ch28-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q14</div>🔴 What is ReDoS, and how do you defend against it in Java?	Regular-expression denial of service: patterns with nested or overlapping quantifiers can backtrack exponentially in a backtracking engine like <code>java.util.regex</code>. In our JDK 21 measurement, a 10-word input took about 66 s. Defences: avoid nested quantifiers, use possessive quantifiers or atomic groups, bound the input length, add a timeout by wrapping the input in a <code>CharSequence</code>, and never compile untrusted patterns (or use a linear-time engine such as RE2/J). → Ch 03	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms topic::ch03
jim-ch28-q015	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q15</div>🟢 Checked vs unchecked exceptions: when do you use each?	Checked exceptions (subclasses of <code>Exception</code> that aren't <code>RuntimeException</code>) must be declared or caught. Unchecked ones (<code>RuntimeException</code>, <code>Error</code>) don't have to be. Use unchecked exceptions for programming errors and for most domain failures in modern APIs. Reserve checked exceptions for genuinely recoverable conditions that the caller must consciously handle. → Ch 04	ch28 part::IX purpose::fundamentals topic::ch04
jim-ch28-q016	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q16</div>🟡 In try-with-resources, what happens if both the body and <code>close()</code> throw?	The body's exception propagates, and the <code>close()</code> exception is attached as a <b>suppressed</b> exception (<code>getSuppressed()</code>). Resources are closed in reverse order of declaration, and every resource is closed even if an earlier <code>close()</code> throws. → Ch 04	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch04
jim-ch28-q017	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q17</div>🔴 Why can stack traces disappear in production logs?	HotSpot's <code>OmitStackTraceInFastThrow</code> optimisation (on by default) replaces frequently thrown <i>implicit</i> exceptions in compiled code (NPE, <code>ArrayIndexOutOfBounds</code>, <code>ClassCast</code>, <code>Arithmetic</code>) with preallocated instances that have no stack trace. Look for the first occurrences earlier in the logs, or run with <code>-XX:-OmitStackTraceInFastThrow</code> while debugging. → Ch 04	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms topic::ch04
jim-ch28-q018	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q18</div>🟡 What happens with a <code>return</code> inside <code>finally</code>?	It overrides the value returned by <code>try</code>, and it <b>silently discards</b> any exception thrown in <code>try</code> or <code>catch</code>. Never return (or throw) from <code>finally</code>. → Ch 04	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch04
jim-ch28-q019	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q19</div>🟢 What is type erasure?	The compiler checks generic types, then erases them to their bounds (usually <code>Object</code>) and inserts casts. At run time, <code>List&lt;String&gt;</code> and <code>List&lt;Integer&gt;</code> are the same <code>List.class</code>, so you can't do <code>instanceof List&lt;String&gt;</code>, <code>new T()</code> or <code>T.class</code>. → Ch 05	ch28 part::IX purpose::fundamentals topic::ch05
jim-ch28-q020	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q20</div>🟡 Explain PECS.	<i>Producer Extends, Consumer Super.</i> If a parameter produces values you read, use <code>? extends T</code>. If it consumes values you write, use <code>? super T</code>. The canonical example is <code>Collections.copy(List&lt;? super T&gt; dest, List&lt;? extends T&gt; src)</code>. → Ch 05	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch05
jim-ch28-q021	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q21</div>🔴 Why can't you write <code>new T[n]</code> or <code>new List&lt;String&gt;[n]</code>?	Arrays are <i>reified</i> and <i>covariant</i>: they check element types at run time. Generic types are erased, so the array couldn't enforce its element type, which leads to heap pollution and <code>ClassCastException</code>s far from the cause. Use <code>List&lt;List&lt;String&gt;&gt;</code>, or <code>Array.newInstance(componentClass, n)</code> when you have a <code>Class&lt;T&gt;</code>. → Ch 05	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms topic::ch05
jim-ch28-q022	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q22</div>🔴 What is a bridge method?	A synthetic method that <code>javac</code> generates so polymorphism survives erasure. When <code>IntNode extends Node&lt;Integer&gt;</code> overrides <code>setData(Integer)</code>, the erased parent method is <code>setData(Object)</code>, a different signature. So the compiler adds a bridge, <code>setData(Object)</code>, which casts to <code>Integer</code> and delegates, and calls through a <code>Node</code> reference still dispatch correctly. You can see it with <code>javap -p IntNode.class</code>. → Ch 05	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms topic::ch05
jim-ch28-q023	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q23</div>🟢 <code>ArrayList</code> vs <code>LinkedList</code>?	<code>ArrayList</code> is a contiguous array: O(1) random access, amortised O(1) append, excellent cache locality. <code>LinkedList</code> allocates a node per element: O(n) indexed access and poor locality. <code>ArrayList</code> wins almost every real workload; for queues and stacks, use <code>ArrayDeque</code>. → Ch 06	ch28 part::IX purpose::fundamentals topic::ch06
jim-ch28-q024	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q24</div>🟡 How does <code>HashMap</code> pick a bucket, and when does it resize?	It spreads the hash with <code>h ^ (h &gt;&gt;&gt; 16)</code>, then indexes with <code>(n - 1) &amp; hash</code>; the table size is a power of two, so the mask replaces a modulo. It resizes (doubling) when <code>size &gt; capacity × loadFactor</code> (0.75 by default), splitting each bucket into a "low" and a "high" list without rehashing. → Ch 06	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch06
jim-ch28-q025	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q25</div>🔴 When does a <code>HashMap</code> bin become a red-black tree?	When one bin reaches <b>8</b> nodes <i>and</i> the table capacity is at least <b>64</b>. With a smaller table, it resizes instead. A tree bin turns back into a list at <b>6</b> nodes. Trees bound worst-case lookups to O(log n) for colliding <code>Comparable</code> keys, which defends against hash-flooding attacks. → Ch 06	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms topic::ch06
jim-ch28-q026	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q26</div>🟡 Fail-fast vs weakly consistent iterators?	Fail-fast iterators (<code>ArrayList</code>, <code>HashMap</code>) check a <code>modCount</code> and throw <code>ConcurrentModificationException</code>, on a best-effort basis, if the collection is structurally modified other than through the iterator. Iterators of concurrent collections never throw: <code>ConcurrentHashMap</code>'s are <i>weakly consistent</i> (they reflect some updates made during iteration), and <code>CopyOnWriteArrayList</code>'s iterate a <i>snapshot</i>. → Ch 06	ch28 part::IX purpose::practice topic::ch06
jim-ch28-q027	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q27</div>🔴 How does <code>ConcurrentHashMap</code> (Java 8+) achieve thread safety?	It inserts into an empty bin with a CAS, locks only the bin's head node (<code>synchronized</code>) for other updates, and reads lock-free via volatile semantics. Resizing is cooperative: threads help transfer bins, leaving forwarding nodes behind. Size is counted with striped counter cells (the <code>LongAdder</code> idea). Null keys and values are forbidden, because <code>null</code> would make <code>get</code> ambiguous. → Ch 06	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms topic::ch06
jim-ch28-q028	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q28</div>🟢 What makes a stream pipeline lazy?	Intermediate operations (<code>filter</code>, <code>map</code>) only describe stages. Nothing executes until a terminal operation (<code>collect</code>, <code>forEach</code>, <code>reduce</code>) pulls elements through, <b>one element at a time through all the stages</b>, which also enables short-circuiting (<code>findFirst</code>, <code>limit</code>). → Ch 07	ch28 part::IX purpose::fundamentals topic::ch07
jim-ch28-q029	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q29</div>🟡 How are lambdas compiled and linked?	The lambda body becomes a private synthetic method. The creation site becomes an <code>invokedynamic</code> instruction, bootstrapped by <code>LambdaMetafactory</code>, which on first execution spins a hidden class implementing the functional interface.
