jim-ch28-q175	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q175</div>🔵 How do you move merchants from an old idempotency table to a new design without charging anyone twice, in either direction?	Make each version look up the other's records before it charges: the new path adopts completed old payments (response, charge ID and age), and a read shim in the old code, deployed first, answers from the new table. Switch each merchant through a drain: no old request in flight, then a backfill, then the switch. Go back the same way, letting the new reconciler settle unresolved operations first, because the old code has no reconciler. And test both directions: in the PostgreSQL lab, a switch without the lookup charged 100 payments again, and a rollback without the shim charged 101 again. → Ch 36 §7	ch28 part::IX purpose::execution
jim-ch28-q176	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q176</div>🔴 Why can <code>INSERT … ON CONFLICT (merchant_id, idempotency_key) DO NOTHING</code> still fail with a unique violation?	Because <code>ON CONFLICT</code> handles only conflicts on its arbiters, the constraints its target names. If the primary key is derived from the same key, a duplicate violates the primary key too, and a racing insert can collide there and raise an error (19 of 2,000 claims in the PostgreSQL lab). Omit the target, which makes every unique constraint an arbiter for <code>DO NOTHING</code>, or stop deriving the primary key from the key. → Ch 36 §6.2	ch28 part::IX purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch28-q177	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q177</div>🟣 Deliveries are unique on (event, endpoint), and replays are promised as new deliveries. What's wrong, and what fixes it?	A replay keeps the event ID, so it violates the constraint, and the <code>ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING</code> that makes fan-out safe swallows it silently: in the lab, two accepted replays sent nothing. Add a replay ID to the key, 0 for fan-out's original, and make each replay a request with its own <code>Idempotency-Key</code>, unique per tenant and written in the same transaction as its delivery. The replay's <code>webhook-id</code> comes from (event, endpoint, replay ID): new to the receiver, stable across its own retries. Eleven retries of one replay call then made one delivery. → Ch 38 §6.3	ch28 part::IX purpose::decisions
jim-ch28-q178	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 28 · The Question Bank · Q178</div>🟣 Why did the virtual-thread experiment need platform threads with the same bulkhead and deadlines?	Without that control, two things changed at once, the thread model and the limits, and the experiment couldn't say which one helped. With it, platform threads answered 671 requests per second in time and virtual threads 668: the limits fixed the overload. Virtual threads then become a separate decision, judged on what they do change: 10 platform threads instead of 1,010 for 300 concurrent slow calls, no pool to size, and behaviour on replayed production traffic. → Ch 39 §3	ch28 part::IX purpose::decisions
jim-ch29-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q1</div>🟢 What does "nothing happens until you subscribe" mean?	Building a <code>Flux</code>/<code>Mono</code> chain (assembly) only creates objects that describe the pipeline. Work starts when a subscriber subscribes: the subscription travels upstream to the source, which then emits as demand arrives. That's why <code>repository.save(x)</code> without returning or subscribing to the result does nothing, and why a cold publisher re-executes for every subscriber.	ch29 part::X purpose::fundamentals
jim-ch29-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q2</div>🟡 Explain the Reactive Streams protocol and backpressure.	A <code>Subscriber</code> subscribes to a <code>Publisher</code> and receives a <code>Subscription</code> in <code>onSubscribe</code>. It then signals demand with <code>request(n)</code>, and the publisher may send at most that many <code>onNext</code> signals, followed by at most one terminal <code>onComplete</code> or <code>onError</code>. Signals are serial, demand is cumulative, and <code>request(n &lt;= 0)</code> is a protocol error. Backpressure is this demand signal: a slow subscriber requests less, so the producer must slow down, buffer within limits, or drop according to an explicit strategy.	ch29 part::X purpose::practice
jim-ch29-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q3</div>🟡 <code>map</code> vs <code>flatMap</code> vs <code>concatMap</code> vs <code>flatMapSequential</code>?	<code>map</code> is a synchronous 1:1 transformation. <code>flatMap</code> maps each element to a publisher and subscribes to them concurrently (256 by default), emitting results in completion order. <code>concatMap</code> subscribes one at a time, preserving order. <code>flatMapSequential</code> runs concurrently but reorders the results back into source order, buffering early arrivals. <code>switchMap</code> cancels the previous inner publisher when a new element arrives, which suits search-as-you-type.	ch29 part::X purpose::practice
jim-ch29-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q4</div>🔴 <code>publishOn</code> vs <code>subscribeOn</code>?	<code>subscribeOn</code> changes the thread on which the subscription travels upstream, and therefore where a cold source emits. Its position in the chain doesn't matter, and the one closest to the source wins. <code>publishOn</code> changes the thread for all downstream signals after it, through a queue. Asynchronous sources such as Netty emit on their own threads regardless of <code>subscribeOn</code>.	ch29 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch29-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q5</div>🔴 How do you call a blocking library from a WebFlux application?	Wrap it lazily and move it off the event loop: <code>Mono.fromCallable(() -&gt; blockingCall()).subscribeOn(Schedulers.boundedElastic())</code>. <code>boundedElastic</code> is capped (10 × cores threads by default, optionally virtual threads on Java 21+), so blocking work can't exhaust the system. Verify with BlockHound in tests. If most of the service is blocking (JPA, legacy SDKs), the honest answer is to use Spring MVC with virtual threads instead.	ch29 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch29-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q6</div>🔴 Cold vs hot publishers, and how do <code>share</code>, <code>cache</code> and <code>replay</code> differ?	Cold publishers do their work per subscriber: each <code>WebClient</code> subscription is a new HTTP call. Hot publishers emit independently of subscribers, and late subscribers miss earlier items. <code>share()</code> multicasts one upstream subscription to current subscribers. <code>replay(n)</code> multicasts and replays the last n items to late subscribers. <code>cache()</code> replays everything, including a terminal error, indefinitely unless you use the TTL variant. Choose deliberately: caching an error can turn a blip into an outage.	ch29 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch29-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q7</div>🔴 How do you implement retries with a timeout correctly in Reactor?	Put <code>timeout</code> <i>before</i> <code>retryWhen</code>, so each attempt has its own timeout (the timeout cancels the in-flight call). Use <code>Retry.backoff</code> with a cap, jitter, and a <code>filter</code> that only retries transient errors (timeouts, 5xx, connection resets, never 4xx). Add an overall <code>timeout</code> after the retry for a total deadline, and a fallback with <code>onErrorResume</code> if a degraded answer is acceptable. Retries re-subscribe, so only retry idempotent operations.	ch29 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch29-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q8</div>🔴 Why do MDC and ThreadLocals break in reactive code, and what's the fix?	One request's processing hops between threads (event loop, <code>boundedElastic</code>, <code>parallel</code>), and each thread serves many requests interleaved, so a <code>ThreadLocal</code> set at the start of a request isn't there later, or belongs to someone else. Reactor's <code>Context</code> is attached to the subscription instead: write it with <code>contextWrite</code> (it applies upstream), and read it with <code>deferContextual</code>. For MDC, tracing and security, the Micrometer context-propagation library restores <code>ThreadLocal</code>s around operators automatically. In Spring Boot, set <code>spring.reactor.context-propagation=auto</code>.	ch29 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch29-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q9</div>🔴 Why does one blocking call on a Netty event loop hurt so much?	Event-loop threads are few (one per core, minimum 4, in Reactor Netty) and each multiplexes many connections. A blocking call stalls every request that shares that thread, not just its own. In our measurement, a single 200 ms block delayed 166 unrelated requests, and p99 went from about 1 ms to 190 ms. With thread-per-request, the same call would only have slowed its own request.	ch29 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch29-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q10</div>🟣 A client downloads a 10-million-row report as an NDJSON stream from WebFlux over R2DBC. Does memory stay flat? Where could it break?	Ideally yes: TCP flow control makes the Netty channel non-writable when the client reads slowly. Reactor Netty then stops requesting, demand stops propagating upstream, and R2DBC stops fetching rows from the cursor. It breaks wherever demand is ignored: an unbounded buffer (<code>onBackpressureBuffer()</code> without a size, <code>Flux.create</code> with the default <code>BUFFER</code>), <code>collectList()</code> somewhere in the pipeline, a hot source that can't be slowed, or a detached <code>subscribe()</code>. Also watch the database side: a long-open cursor holds a connection and a snapshot for the whole download.	ch29 part::X purpose::decisions
jim-ch29-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q11</div>🔴 How do you test code that uses <code>delayElements</code> or hourly intervals?	<code>StepVerifier.withVirtualTime(() -&gt; publisher)</code> replaces time-based schedulers with a virtual clock. Then <code>thenAwait(Duration.ofHours(3))</code> advances time instantly. The publisher must be created inside the supplier, so its operators pick up the virtual scheduler. Combine with <code>expectNoEvent(duration)</code> to assert silence.	ch29 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch29-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 29 · Reactive Java: Reactor &amp; WebFlux · Q12</div>🟣 Would you choose WebFlux for a new service today?	It depends on the workload. For request/response APIs on JDK 21+, I'd choose Spring MVC with virtual threads: simpler code, normal stack traces, the full blocking ecosystem, with explicit concurrency limits toward dependencies. I'd choose WebFlux for streaming (SSE, WebSockets), for end-to-end backpressure needs, for gateways with very high connection counts, or when the organisation already runs a mature reactive stack. Either way, I'd validate with a load test that uses realistic downstream latency.	ch29 part::X purpose::decisions
jim-ch30-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q1</div>🟡 Startup vs warmup vs footprint: which techniques address which?	Startup is the time to the first request. CDS, AppCDS and the AOT cache reduce class-loading time; Spring AOT and fewer beans reduce framework time; CRaC and native images remove most of it. Warmup is the time to peak JIT performance: JDK 25's AOT cache stores method profiles, and CRaC restores already-compiled code (native images have no warmup, but a lower peak). Footprint is RSS: compact object headers, right-sized heaps, fewer threads and shared CDS/AOT archives, with native images lowest of all.	ch30 part::X purpose::practice
jim-ch30-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q2</div>🟡 What is CDS, and how did it become the AOT cache?	Class-data sharing stores parsed class metadata in a memory-mapped archive, so the JVM skips parsing and verification. The default archive (JDK 12) covers JDK classes. AppCDS (JDK 10) and dynamic archives (JDK 13) add application classes from a training run. Project Leyden's AOT cache (JDK 24) also stores classes in a loaded and <i>linked</i> state. JDK 25 adds one-step creation (<code>-XX:AOTCacheOutput</code>) and JIT method profiles, and JDK 26 extends cached heap objects to any GC. In our measurement, the AOT cache cut startup by 44%, and time in <code>main</code> by over half.	ch30 part::X purpose::practice
jim-ch30-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q3</div>🔴 What are the rules for using an AOT cache, and what happens if you break them?	Use the same JDK build and the same classpath as the training run: JARs only, no directories or wildcards, same order, timestamps preserved. In default mode, a mismatch logs errors and the JVM starts <b>without</b> the cache. In our test, reordering two JARs silently brought startup back to 627 ms. With <code>-XX:AOTMode=on</code>, the JVM refuses to start instead, which makes a good CI check. Archives from a different JDK version are rejected as the wrong version.	ch30 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch30-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q4</div>🔴 How would you add the AOT cache to a Spring Boot service's container build?	Extract the executable JAR (<code>java -Djarmode=tools -jar app.jar extract</code>). In the final image stage, run a training run: <code>java -XX:AOTCacheOutput=app.aot -Dspring.context.exit=onRefresh -jar app.jar</code>, with a profile that keeps it from connecting to real dependencies. Start production with <code>-XX:AOTCache=app.aot</code>. Add a smoke test with <code>-XX:AOTMode=on</code>. If warmup matters, consider training with representative traffic so the cached profiles are useful.	ch30 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch30-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q5</div>🔴 NMT shows 187 MB committed, but RSS is 98 MB. Why the difference, and what counts against the container limit?	Committed memory is address space the JVM has reserved backing for. RSS counts only pages actually touched. The heap is committed eagerly but filled gradually, and other areas behave similarly. The kernel's memory accounting (and therefore the OOM killer) works on resident and charged pages, not on NMT's committed figures. That's why <code>-Xmx64m</code> didn't change this small app's RSS. Plan the container limit around RSS under realistic load: heap, plus metaspace, code cache, GC structures, thread stacks, direct buffers and native libraries.	ch30 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch30-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q6</div>🔴 What do compact object headers do, and why do the savings differ between classes?	They shrink the header from 12 bytes (an 8-byte mark word plus a 4-byte class pointer) to 8, by encoding the class pointer in the mark word. They're opt-in in JDK 25 and the default in JDK 27. Objects are padded to 8 bytes, so the 4 saved bytes only shrink an object if they cross an alignment boundary. <code>Point(int, int)</code> went from 24 to 16 bytes, <code>OrderLine</code> from 32 to 24, but <code>Integer</code> stays at 16. Heaps full of small objects gain the most: −25% in our test.	ch30 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch30-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q7</div>🔴 How does GraalVM Native Image work, and what typically breaks when you adopt it?	A build-time points-to analysis finds all reachable code from <code>main</code> (a closed world), compiles it ahead of time with a small runtime, and can initialise classes at build time. What breaks: reflection, proxies, resources, JNI and serialisation without metadata; runtime class loading; Java agents (APM); libraries that generate bytecode at runtime; build times and memory; and, in Spring, any assumption that a property can change <i>which</i> beans exist at runtime, since the bean graph is fixed by AOT processing.	ch30 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch30-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q8</div>🟣 A scale-to-zero service needs sub-second cold starts. AOT cache, CRaC or native image?	Start from the requirements. If "sub-second" means under about 300 ms and the dependency stack is native-friendly, a native image fits, at the cost of builds, metadata, a lower peak and the support model. If you also need warm peak performance immediately and you control the platform (or use Lambda SnapStart), CRaC gives tens of milliseconds with JIT-warm code, but you take on snapshot hygiene. If roughly a halving of startup is enough, the AOT cache is the cheapest and most compatible option. I'd measure all three on the real service and compare first-request time, steady-state p99, RSS and operational cost.	ch30 part::X purpose::decisions
jim-ch30-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q9</div>🔴 What are the main risks of CRaC?	Snapshot contents. The heap may contain secrets, tokens and keys, and every restored instance shares the same random-generator state and instance identity. You must reseed and regenerate after restore, and avoid baking long-lived secrets into checkpoints. Resources (sockets, files, pools) must be closed before the checkpoint and reopened after it, through <code>Resource</code> callbacks. CRaC also needs a CRaC-enabled JDK, Linux and CRIU privileges, and time-sensitive caches and DNS need refreshing after restore.	ch30 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch30-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q10</div>🟣 How did GraalVM's position change in 2025, and how does that affect a Java platform strategy?	Oracle announced that GraalVM for JDK 24 was the last release licensed and supported as part of Oracle Java SE products. Native Image is being discontinued for Java SE product customers, and startup and footprint work moves to OpenJDK's Project Leyden. GraalVM continues independently (Community Edition, and Oracle GraalVM under its free licence). For strategy: make standard Leyden features (the AOT cache) the fleet default, keep native images for the workloads where they clearly pay off, and review the support arrangements for those.	ch30 part::X purpose::decisions
jim-ch30-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 30 · Startup &amp; Footprint: AOT Cache, Native Image &amp; CRaC · Q11</div>🔴 Why can't a Spring Boot native image switch beans with a property at runtime?	Spring's AOT processing runs at build time. It evaluates the configuration, decides which beans exist, and generates code and reachability hints for exactly that bean graph. Conditions that determine bean existence (<code>@Profile</code>, <code>@ConditionalOnProperty</code>) are therefore fixed in the binary. You can still change ordinary property <i>values</i> at runtime, but not which beans are defined. Build separate images, or design configuration so variability lives in values rather than in bean selection.	ch30 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q1</div>🟡 PUT vs PATCH vs POST: which are idempotent, and why does it matter?	<code>PUT</code> replaces the resource at a known URI, so repeating it yields the same state: idempotent. <code>PATCH</code> applies a change that may or may not be idempotent ("set price to 10" is; "increment stock by 1" isn't). <code>POST</code> creates or triggers actions, and isn't idempotent. It matters because clients and proxies retry after timeouts: idempotent operations can be retried safely, while <code>POST</code> needs idempotency keys or client-generated IDs.	ch31 part::X purpose::practice
jim-ch31-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q2</div>🟡 401 vs 403; 400 vs 422; 409 vs 412?	401: not authenticated (send credentials). 403: authenticated, but not allowed. 400: malformed request (bad JSON, wrong types). 422: well-formed but semantically invalid (business validation failed). 409: conflicts with the current state of the resource (a duplicate, an invalid state transition). 412: a precondition the client sent (<code>If-Match</code>) failed, which is how optimistic concurrency over HTTP reports a lost-update attempt.	ch31 part::X purpose::practice
jim-ch31-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q3</div>🔴 How do you make <code>POST /payments</code> safe to retry?	Require an <code>Idempotency-Key</code> header. Claim the key <i>before</i> doing the work (insert it with a fingerprint of the request under a unique constraint, and commit), then store the final response against it. On replay with the same payload, return the stored response. With a different payload, return 422, which the client must never retry. While the first request is still in flight, return 409 with an error type such as <code>idempotency-key-in-use</code> and <code>Retry-After</code>, which the client <i>should</i> retry with the same key. Expire keys after a documented window. Downstream calls (to the payment processor) get their own deterministic keys, and timeouts are treated as unknown outcomes to reconcile, never as failures to blindly retry.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q4</div>🔴 What goes wrong with offset pagination, and what do you use instead?	Under concurrent writes, offsets shift: in our demo, an insert between pages made an item appear twice, and a delete made one silently disappear. Deep offsets are also slow, because the database reads and discards the skipped rows. Keyset (cursor) pagination asks for rows <i>after</i> the last seen sort key (with a unique tie-breaker), which is stable under writes and constant-time with the right index. The trade-off is that you can't jump to an arbitrary page number.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q5</div>🔴 How do ETags prevent lost updates? Walk through the exchange.	A client reads the resource and receives <code>ETag: "v1"</code>. It sends its update with <code>If-Match: "v1"</code>. If someone else updated first, the current ETag is <code>"v2"</code>, so the server answers 412 Precondition Failed instead of overwriting their change. The client re-reads, merges and retries. Requiring a precondition (428 when it's missing) makes the protection mandatory. On the read side, <code>If-None-Match</code> lets the server answer 304 with no body when nothing changed.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q6</div>🔴 A producer added a JSON field and a consumer broke. Why, and how do you prevent it organisationally?	The consumer used a strict deserialiser: a plain Jackson <code>ObjectMapper</code> fails on unknown properties by default, as our demo showed. Additive changes are only safe if consumers are tolerant readers. Prevent it with client guidelines and shared client libraries configured for tolerant reading (ignore unknown fields, map unknown enum values to <code>UNKNOWN</code>), contract tests that include "extra fields", and documentation stating that new fields and enum values may appear at any time.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q7</div>🔴 How should consumers handle enum values they don't know?	Treat enums in responses as open sets. Map unknown values to an explicit <code>UNKNOWN</code> (in Jackson, <code>@JsonEnumDefaultValue</code> with <code>READ_UNKNOWN_ENUM_VALUES_USING_DEFAULT_VALUE</code>; in Protobuf, unrecognised values are preserved and the <code>*_UNSPECIFIED = 0</code> convention gives a safe default). Then handle <code>UNKNOWN</code> sensibly in business logic: display a generic state, skip, or route to manual handling. Producers should document enum extensibility from day one.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q8</div>🔴 What are the rules for evolving a Protobuf schema, and what happens if you reuse a field number?	Add new fields with new numbers. Never change a field's number or type. Never reuse a number: mark retired fields <code>reserved</code> (number and name). Keep enum zero as <code>UNSPECIFIED</code>. Remember that renames break generated code and JSON mapping. Reusing a number causes silent misinterpretation: in our demo, an old service read a 15% discount as a quantity of 15, with no error at all. A type change instead made the value disappear into unknown fields. Enforce the rules with <code>buf breaking</code> in CI.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q9</div>🔴 Why does gRPC need L7 or client-side load balancing?	gRPC runs over HTTP/2, which multiplexes many requests over one long-lived TCP connection. An L4 load balancer balances <i>connections</i>, so all requests from a client stick to whichever backend got the connection, and new pods receive little traffic. Use an L7 proxy or service mesh that balances individual requests, or client-side balancing (round-robin over resolved addresses, or xDS), and set a maximum connection age on servers so clients periodically reconnect.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q10</div>🔴 What is the GraphQL N+1 problem, and how does Spring for GraphQL solve it?	A query for 50 orders with their customers resolves <code>customer</code> once per order: 1 + 50 calls. DataLoader batching collects all the keys requested in one execution step and resolves them in a single batched call. In Spring for GraphQL, <code>@BatchMapping</code> declares a method that receives the list of parents and returns a map from parent to child, so the 50 lookups become one <code>findByIds</code> call.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q11</div>🔴 BACKWARD vs FORWARD compatibility in a schema registry: which do you choose, and how do you deploy?	BACKWARD (the default in common registries) means consumers on the new schema can read old data, so you upgrade consumers first: deleting fields and adding optional ones are allowed. FORWARD means old consumers can read new data, so you upgrade producers first. FULL requires both. Use the TRANSITIVE variants when topics are retained or replayed for a long time, because a consumer may read data from many versions ago.	ch31 part::X purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch31-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q12</div>🔵 How would you introduce API governance across 60 teams without slowing them down?	Start with a short style guide covering errors, pagination, IDs, dates, money and versioning. Ship it as code: a shared starter (problem details, pagination, tracing, idempotency) and lint rules. Automate gates in CI: style lint, breaking-change detection, and contract tests for critical consumers. Keep human review for public or high-fan-out APIs only. Publish an API catalogue with owners, and a deprecation policy backed by usage telemetry. Measure adoption and incidents caused by API changes, and iterate. Make the paved road the easiest path rather than adding approval steps.	ch31 part::X purpose::execution
jim-ch31-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 31 · API Design Between Services · Q13</div>🟣 URI vs header vs media-type versioning: which would you pick?	For public APIs, URI versioning (<code>/v2/...</code>) is the pragmatic default: visible, easy to route, cache and document. For internal APIs with good client libraries, a header (<code>API-Version</code>, natively supported in Spring Framework 7) keeps URIs stable. Media-type versioning is the most HTTP-pure, but the least convenient for clients. More important than the mechanism: avoid versions through additive evolution, run old and new versions side by side, emit <code>Deprecation</code> and <code>Sunset</code> headers, and retire versions based on measured usage.	ch31 part::X purpose::decisions
jim-ch32-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q1</div>🔴 Pods are OOMKilled, but the heap is only 40% used. How do you diagnose it?	<code>OOMKilled</code> (exit code 137) means the kernel killed the process for exceeding the container's memory limit, so compare RSS with the limit and inventory non-heap memory. Enable NMT (<code>-XX:NativeMemoryTracking=summary</code>) and diff <code>jcmd VM.native_memory</code> over time: growth in "Other" points to direct buffers, Metaspace to class loading, Thread to too many threads. Check <code>MaxDirectMemorySize</code> (it defaults to the max heap size), Netty buffer leaks, mapped files and native libraries, and whether <code>MaxRAMPercentage</code> leaves room for non-heap memory at all. Fix the leak, set explicit limits, and alert on RSS versus the limit.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q2</div>🔴 A service hangs with idle CPU, and the thread dump reports no deadlock. What could it be?	Most likely a thread-starvation deadlock: all threads in a bounded pool are blocked waiting (in <code>Future.get</code> or <code>join</code>) for tasks that are queued behind them in the same pool. The JVM only detects lock-cycle deadlocks, so nothing is reported. The signature is every pool thread <code>WAITING</code> in <code>get</code>/<code>join</code> with a non-empty queue. Other candidates: connection-pool exhaustion (threads waiting for a connection), remote calls without timeouts (threads in socket reads), and lost wake-ups. Fix it by not blocking pool threads on same-pool work, by composing asynchronously, or by using virtual threads. Add timeouts everywhere.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q3</div>🔴 Throughput dropped after switching to virtual threads on JDK 21. Why, and how do you confirm and fix it?	Pinning. On JDK 21, a virtual thread that blocks inside <code>synchronized</code> (or a native frame) can't unmount, so it holds one of the few carrier threads. Our reproduction took 5,049 ms instead of about 75 ms. Confirm with <code>-Djdk.tracePinnedThreads</code> on JDK 21, or the JFR <code>jdk.VirtualThreadPinned</code> event. Fix it by upgrading to JDK 24+ (JEP 491 removes <code>synchronized</code> pinning), replacing <code>synchronized</code> around blocking I/O with <code>ReentrantLock</code>, or upgrading the library that does it.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q4</div>🔴 How do you find a heap leak when a full heap dump is too big or too risky in production?	Take two or three <code>jcmd GC.class_histogram</code> snapshots minutes apart, and look for <i>your own</i> classes growing steadily, alongside the generic <code>byte[]</code>, <code>String</code> and <code>HashMap$Node</code> entries that follow them. In our reproduction, <code>Session</code> went from 548,001 to 1,208,001 instances in 4 seconds. Correlate with old-gen usage after GC. Then reproduce in a lower environment, or take a heap dump from a canary or a drained instance, and use a dominator tree to find the retaining field.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q5</div>🔴 What is a GC death spiral, and how do you detect it before the OOM?	As the live set approaches the heap size, each collection frees less, so collections run more often and the application gets less time to run. In our reproduction, the paused share of wall time rose from 22% to 97% before the OOM, with back-to-back full GCs freeing almost nothing. Detect it by alerting on the fraction of time in GC and on old-gen occupancy after GC trending upwards. Mitigate it with <code>ExitOnOutOfMemoryError</code>, so the orchestrator replaces a zombie instance quickly, then find what grew.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q6</div>🔴 The database connection pool is exhausted, but the database looks idle. What's going on?	Connections are held while the application does something other than database work, usually a remote call inside a <code>@Transactional</code> method, or slow processing between queries. By Little's law, a pool's capacity is its size divided by hold time. In our reproduction, a 200 ms remote call inside the transaction caused 113 timeouts out of 300 requests, and moving it outside caused none. Fix the transaction boundaries (no remote I/O inside transactions; use an outbox for side effects), rather than enlarging the pool. Monitor connection acquire and usage times.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q7</div>🔴 One pod is at 100% CPU. Take me from the OS to the line of code.	Use <code>top -H -p &lt;pid&gt;</code> (or per-thread CPU from <code>/proc/&lt;pid&gt;/task</code>) to find the hot thread ID. Java thread names are often visible there already. Take a thread dump with <code>jcmd &lt;pid&gt; Thread.print</code> and find that ID: modern JDKs print it in decimal as <code>[tid]</code> and <code>nid=tid</code>; older ones printed <code>nid</code> in hex. Take two or three dumps to confirm it's consistently in the same code. For a fuller picture, record JFR or use async-profiler, and read the hottest stack frames. In our reproduction, this led to regex backtracking within a minute.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q8</div>🔴 p99 latency spikes every 10 minutes exactly. What are your hypotheses?	Exact periodicity points to something scheduled: synchronised cache TTL expiry causing a stampede (TTLs set at the same moment); scheduled jobs (<code>@Scheduled</code>, batch exports) competing for pools or the database; platform cycles (token refresh, metrics scrapes, log rotation, node cron jobs); or periodic GC behaviour. Prove it by aligning timestamps across metrics, GC logs, scheduler logs and database load. Fix stampedes with TTL jitter, refresh-ahead and request coalescing, and isolate heavy jobs.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q9</div>🔴 Kafka consumer lag keeps growing after a deploy, with repeated rebalances. Why?	Processing a poll batch now takes longer than <code>max.poll.interval.ms</code>, so the consumer is evicted. That triggers a rebalance, uncommitted messages are reprocessed elsewhere, and the cycle repeats. Fix it by shortening the work per poll (smaller <code>max.poll.records</code>, timeouts on enrichment calls, parallelism that preserves key order), using cooperative rebalancing (or the KIP-848 consumer protocol) and static membership, and keeping handlers idempotent. Alert on lag growth and rebalance rate.	ch32 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch32-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q10</div>🔵 How do you run a postmortem so the fixes actually stick?	Keep it blameless and evidence-based: a timeline from detection to recovery, the root cause <i>and</i> the contributing factors (why it wasn't caught earlier, why recovery took so long). Turn those into a small number of owned, dated action items, prioritising systemic ones: an alert that would have fired, a test that would have caught it, a guardrail or default that prevents the class of problem fleet-wide. Track the action items to completion in the normal planning process, share the learning beyond the team, and check later that the alert or guardrail actually works (for example, with a game day).	ch32 part::XI purpose::execution
jim-ch32-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 32 · Production Troubleshooting Scenarios · Q11</div>🟣 How do you structure your answer to an open-ended "debug this" interview question?	Clarify the scope and timeline, and stabilise first (roll back or scale if needed). Use cheap checks to narrow the space ("OOMKilled means kernel, so RSS, not heap"). State ranked hypotheses, and for each one, the evidence that would confirm or refute it. Explain the tools and what the output would look like. Propose an immediate mitigation and a permanent fix. Close with prevention: alerts, tests, guardrails and the lesson for the fleet. Thinking out loud in this structure is what interviewers grade.	ch32 part::XI purpose::decisions
jim-ch33-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 33 · Annotated Mock Interviews · Q1</div>🟣 JVM round: "After moving to Kubernetes, our GC pauses doubled and throughput fell." How do you approach it?	Clarify what else changed: JDK version, heap flags (fixed <code>-Xmx</code> vs <code>MaxRAMPercentage</code>), CPU limits, node type. Likely causes: <b>CPU throttling</b> (a low CPU limit starves GC and JIT threads; check cgroup throttling metrics), <b>the JVM seeing fewer CPUs</b> and sizing GC threads down (check <code>-Xlog:gc+init</code>, <code>ActiveProcessorCount</code>), <b>a smaller heap</b> than on the old hosts (container-aware sizing), or <b>noisy neighbours</b>. Prove it with GC logs before and after, throttling metrics, and <code>VM.flags</code>. Fix: right-size CPU requests and limits (or remove CPU limits for latency-sensitive services), set the heap explicitly, validate with a load test. Staff extension: fleet guidance for JVM container settings in the paved-road base image.	ch33 part::XI purpose::decisions
jim-ch33-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 33 · Annotated Mock Interviews · Q2</div>🟣 Design round: "Design a feature-flag service used by 300 Java services."	Requirements: flag types (booleans, percentages, targeting rules), evaluation latency (in-process, microseconds), update propagation (seconds), auditability, and safety (kill switches). Design: a control plane (UI and API, audit log, versioned rule sets) and a data plane (an SDK in each service that evaluates locally from a cached rule set, updated by streaming or polling, with a last-known-good snapshot on disk for startup). The key failure mode is the flag service being down, which must <b>never</b> break the services: they keep evaluating from their cache with safe defaults. Also cover consistent bucketing (hashing user IDs), stale-flag cleanup, governance (owners, expiry dates), and the SDK's own footprint and thread usage.	ch33 part::XI purpose::decisions
jim-ch33-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 33 · Annotated Mock Interviews · Q3</div>🔵 Behavioural: "Tell me about a decision you made that turned out to be wrong."	Pick a real, consequential decision, not a disguised strength. Structure: the context and the information you had, <i>why</i> the decision was reasonable then, what happened and how you noticed (the signals), what you did to correct course, and what you changed in how you decide (a checkpoint you now add, a reversibility assessment, earlier involvement of a specific group). Show accountability without self-flagellation, and a learning that's specific enough to be credible.	ch33 part::XI purpose::execution
jim-ch33-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 33 · Annotated Mock Interviews · Q4</div>🔴 Coding: "Implement a thread-safe LRU cache with per-entry TTL."	Clarify capacity, TTL semantics (expire after write or after access), concurrency level, and whether reads may be slightly stale. Implement a <code>HashMap</code> plus a doubly linked list with sentinels for O(1) LRU (Chapter 26 §7), store an expiry time per node, expire lazily on read, and optionally sweep. Guard it with one lock (explain why a read-write lock doesn't work: <code>get</code> mutates recency). Inject the clock and test eviction order, expiry, and concurrent puts and gets. Follow-ups: lock striping, Caffeine's design, memory bounds.	ch33 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch33-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 33 · Annotated Mock Interviews · Q5</div>🟣 Design review: "This checkout flow makes six synchronous calls in sequence. Critique it."	Availability multiplies: six dependencies at 99.9% give at most about 99.4%. Latency adds up, and tail latencies compound. Retries at several layers can amplify load. Suggestions: run independent calls in parallel with per-call budgets; make non-critical calls asynchronous (recommendations, analytics, emails via events and an outbox); cache reference data; set timeouts as a budget under the overall deadline; add circuit breakers and fallbacks for optional data; use idempotency keys for the payment step; consider whether some calls can be pre-computed. Prioritise by impact, and propose measuring the critical path with tracing.	ch33 part::XI purpose::decisions
jim-ch33-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 33 · Annotated Mock Interviews · Q6</div>🟣 Architecture: "A team proposes splitting the monolith into 12 microservices. How do you evaluate it?"	Ask what problem the split solves (deploy contention, scaling hotspots, team autonomy, reliability isolation), and what the evidence is. Check boundary quality: domain seams with low coupling, data ownership, no distributed transactions on hot paths. Weigh the costs: operations, observability, network failure modes, consistency, and the platform maturity needed. Often the better first step is a modular monolith with enforced boundaries, extracting the one or two services where independent scaling or deployment really pays. Propose a strangler-fig plan with measurable milestones (Chapter 24 §12) and an ADR.	ch33 part::XI purpose::decisions
jim-ch33-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 33 · Annotated Mock Interviews · Q7</div>🔴 Debugging: "p99 doubles every Monday at 09:00." What are your hypotheses?	Weekly and time-anchored suggests a traffic pattern (Monday-morning logins and report runs), scheduled jobs (weekly batches, compactions, backups, index rebuilds on the database), cold caches after weekend inactivity (cache TTLs or scale-down), autoscaling lag (scaled in over the weekend; slow JVM startup delays scale-out), or certificate and token renewals. Correlate with traffic, autoscaling events, scheduler logs and database maintenance windows. Typical fixes: scheduled pre-scaling, cache warmup, moving batch jobs, faster startup (Chapter 30).	ch33 part::XI purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch33-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 33 · Annotated Mock Interviews · Q8</div>🔵 Leadership: "Two senior engineers strongly disagree about Kafka vs RabbitMQ for a new system, and you're asked to decide."	Don't decide by preference or seniority. Restate the requirements both sides agree on, and turn the disagreement into explicit criteria: replay and retention, ordering, throughput, routing patterns, operational expertise, existing platform, cost. Ask each engineer to present the strongest case for the <i>other</i> option (it surfaces real trade-offs and cools emotions). If a criterion is uncertain, run a time-boxed spike that measures it. Decide, write an ADR including the dissent and the conditions that would make us revisit, and make sure both engineers have meaningful ownership in the outcome. Disagree-and-commit applies to you as well.	ch33 part::XI purpose::execution
jim-ch34-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q1</div>🟡 Redis is single-threaded. Why is it still fast, and what does single-threaded mean in practice?	It keeps data in memory in purpose-built structures, uses an event loop with non-blocking I/O, and most commands are O(1) or O(log N), so one core serves a very large number of operations per second. "Single-threaded" means commands <i>execute</i> one at a time on the main thread (I/O threads can parallelise socket reads and writes, and background threads free memory and fsync). The consequences: every individual command is atomic, and any slow command (<code>KEYS</code>, a huge <code>DEL</code>, a long Lua script) delays every other client. In the lab, one <code>KEYS *</code> over a million keys made another client wait 282 ms.	ch34 part::XII purpose::practice
jim-ch34-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q2</div>🟡 What's wrong with <code>KEYS</code>, and why can deleting one key take half a second?	<code>KEYS</code> walks the whole keyspace in one command, blocking the server (282 ms for a million keys in the lab). Use <code>SCAN</code>, which iterates with a cursor in small slices; it can return duplicates and treats <code>COUNT</code> as a hint, but it never blocks for long (worst 4.5 ms for the other client). Deleting is O(N) in the size of the value: <code>DEL</code> of a 2-million-field hash blocked everyone for 589 ms. <code>UNLINK</code> (or <code>lazyfree-lazy-user-del yes</code>) removes the key from the keyspace immediately and frees the memory on a background thread, returning in under a millisecond.	ch34 part::XII purpose::practice
jim-ch34-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q3</div>🔴 Pipelining, MULTI/EXEC and Lua: what does each give you?	Pipelining cuts round trips: the client sends many commands before reading the replies. It gives no atomicity; other clients' commands interleave. Measured at about 0.8 ms RTT: 1,174 operations a second one at a time, 413,720 pipelined. <code>MULTI</code>/<code>EXEC</code> executes the queued commands as one atomic block, but you can't read values mid-transaction and there's no rollback if a command fails at run time; with <code>WATCH</code> it becomes optimistic concurrency control (abort and retry if a watched key changed). A Lua script (or Redis 7 Function) runs atomically on the server with logic, so read-decide-write happens in one step, at the price of blocking everyone while it runs.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q4</div>🔴 Four services increment a Redis counter with GET then SET. What happens, and how do you fix it?	Lost updates: two clients read the same value and both write value+1. In the lab, 4 clients × 2,500 increments produced 3,286 instead of 10,000. Fixes, best first: let the server do the whole operation (<code>INCR</code>, <code>HINCRBY</code>, or Redis 8.4's <code>SET … IFEQ</code> for compare-and-set); a Lua script for logic that no single command covers; or <code>WATCH</code>/<code>MULTI</code>/<code>EXEC</code> with retry, which was correct but needed 13,284 retries under that contention.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q5</div>🔴 Your Redis cache starts rejecting writes with "OOM command not allowed". Why, and what do you change?	<code>maxmemory</code> was reached under <code>noeviction</code> (the default), or under a <code>volatile-*</code> policy while keys have no TTL: <code>volatile-*</code> only evicts keys with an expiry, so it behaves exactly like <code>noeviction</code> when TTLs are missing (the lab showed both failing at about 24,000 keys). For a pure cache, use <code>allkeys-lru</code> or <code>allkeys-lfu</code>, put a TTL (with jitter) on every key anyway, size memory with real per-key overhead in mind (about 1.4 KB per 1 KB value in the lab), and keep data that must not be evicted in a separate instance. Monitor <code>evicted_keys</code>, hit ratio and memory headroom for forks.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q6</div>🔴 Is a Redis lock safe?	For efficiency, yes, if done right: <code>SET key token NX PX ttl</code> to acquire, compare-and-delete to release (a Lua script, or <code>DELEX key IFEQ token</code> on Redis 8.4+). For correctness, not by itself: a holder can pause past its lease, so two clients believe they hold the lock. The lab showed it, and showed a plain <code>DEL</code> releasing somebody else's lock. Correctness needs a fencing token that the protected resource checks and rejects when stale, or a database mechanism (unique constraints, conditional updates). Redlock doesn't fix pauses.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q7</div>🔴 What can you lose with Redis persistence and replication?	With RDB only, everything since the last snapshot. With AOF <code>everysec</code>, about a second. Replication is asynchronous, so a failover can lose writes the primary had already acknowledged; <code>WAIT</code> and <code>WAITAOF</code> narrow the window but don't make Redis strongly consistent. RDB snapshots and AOF rewrites fork the process, and copy-on-write under heavy writes can nearly double memory use. If the data can't be lost, it needs another system of record, or a durable variant that commits to a replicated log before acknowledging.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q8</div>🔴 You move from a single Redis node to Redis Cluster. What breaks?	Multi-key commands, transactions and Lua scripts must touch keys in a single slot, or they fail with <code>CROSSSLOT</code>; hash tags (<code>{user:42}:cart</code>, <code>{user:42}:profile</code>) co-locate related keys, and the lab confirmed they map to the same slot (15880). Clients must handle <code>MOVED</code> and <code>ASK</code> redirects and keep a slot map. <code>SELECT</code> of other databases isn't available (Valkey 9 changed that). Hot keys and big keys now load one shard, and hash tags can concentrate a big tenant on one node. Pipelines are split per node by the client.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q9</div>🔴 Model chat messages in Cassandra.	Start from the query: "the newest N messages of a conversation, then older ones". Partition by <code>(conversation_id, day)</code> to bound partition size, cluster by a <code>timeuuid</code> in descending order so the newest messages come first, and page backwards through days. Do the arithmetic: 20,000 messages a day at 500 bytes is about 10 MB per daily partition; without the bucket, a busy conversation reaches about 11 GB in three years. Write and read at <code>LOCAL_QUORUM</code> with RF = 3, use a TTL for retention, and keep other access patterns (a user's conversations) in their own denormalised tables.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q10</div>🔴 What are tombstones, and why is Cassandra a bad queue?	A delete writes a tombstone, because data is immutable in SSTables. Compaction can purge it only after <code>gc_grace_seconds</code> (10 days by default), and only safely if repair has run, or deleted data can reappear. A queue table, where workers delete each job, accumulates tombstones at the head of the partition; every read scans them, latency climbs, and reads fail past <code>tombstone_failure_threshold</code> (100,000 by default). Use Kafka or a database queue (<code>SKIP LOCKED</code>).	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q11</div>🔴 How do you get read-your-writes in Cassandra?	Make the read and write replica sets overlap: R + W &gt; RF. With RF = 3, <code>LOCAL_QUORUM</code> writes and reads (2 + 2 &gt; 3) give it within a datacentre; <code>EACH_QUORUM</code> writes or <code>QUORUM</code> reads stretch it across datacentres at a latency cost. For compare-and-set (unique usernames, "only if still pending"), use lightweight transactions (<code>IF NOT EXISTS</code>, serial consistency), which run Paxos and cost several round trips; Cassandra 6.0's Accord adds multi-partition transactions.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q12</div>🔴 A DynamoDB table throttles while its total capacity is mostly unused. Why?	A hot partition key: each partition serves at most 3,000 RCU and 1,000 WCU, and adaptive capacity can't lift one key value above that. Common causes: a status or date used as a partition key, a global counter, a celebrity item, or a GSI keyed on a low-cardinality attribute (which, in provisioned mode, also throttles writes to the base table). Fixes: higher-cardinality keys, write sharding (<code>OPEN#0</code> … <code>OPEN#9</code>) with parallel reads, caching hot reads, and sparse indexes so only relevant items are indexed.	ch34 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch34-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q13</div>🟣 When is DynamoDB single-table design right, and when is it a mistake?	It's right when the access patterns are known and stable and related items should be fetched together: one <code>Query</code> on an item collection returns a customer and their newest orders, and overloaded GSIs serve other lookups. It's a mistake when queries are exploratory or keep changing, for analytics (export to S3 instead), or when entities belong to different teams, where a table per bounded context is clearer. The real principle is "design keys for access patterns and fetch related data in one request", not "one table"; and changing it later means new indexes or a migration with a throttled backfill.	ch34 part::XII purpose::decisions
jim-ch34-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 34 · Data Stores: Redis, Cassandra &amp; DynamoDB · Q14</div>🟣 PostgreSQL, Redis, Cassandra or DynamoDB: how do you choose?	Start from access patterns, consistency needs, scale and operating model. PostgreSQL is the default system of record: transactions, constraints, ad-hoc queries. Redis is for what fits memory and can be rebuilt or lost: caches, counters, rate limits, ephemeral coordination, always with TTLs and a cache-down plan. Cassandra suits write-heavy, partition-shaped data (time series, messaging) at scale, multi-datacentre, on infrastructure you control, if you staff its operations. DynamoDB suits AWS workloads with known access patterns that want no servers and predictable latency at any scale, priced per request. Then price the business transaction on each candidate, and name what you'd give up.	ch34 part::XII purpose::decisions
jim-ch35-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q1</div>🟡 What is RAG, and when would you use it instead of fine-tuning?	Retrieval-augmented generation retrieves passages relevant to a question from your own data (usually by vector similarity, often combined with keyword search), puts them in the prompt, and asks the model to answer from them with citations. Use it for knowledge that is private, changes often or must be traceable: updating it means re-indexing documents, not retraining. Fine-tuning changes behaviour (format, tone, a narrow skill) and is a poor way to inject changing facts. Many systems use both: a tuned or well-prompted model, grounded by retrieval.	ch35 part::XII purpose::practice
jim-ch35-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q2</div>🟡 What is an embedding, and how do you compare two of them?	A vector produced by an embedding model so that texts with similar meaning are close together. Compare with cosine similarity; if vectors are normalised to unit length, cosine similarity equals the dot product, which is cheap and SIMD-friendly. Vectors from different models (or versions) aren't comparable, so store the model identity with each vector and re-embed when you switch.	ch35 part::XII purpose::practice
jim-ch35-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q3</div>🔴 How does HNSW work, and what do M, efConstruction and efSearch trade?	It's a multi-layer proximity graph: each vector links to about M neighbours, and sparse upper layers provide long-range jumps. Search descends greedily through the upper layers, then explores the bottom layer keeping the efSearch best candidates. Larger M means better recall and more memory; larger efConstruction means a better graph and slower builds; efSearch is the per-query recall/latency dial. In the lab (50,000 vectors), efSearch 40 gave recall@10 of 0.995 in 66 µs versus 2,761 µs for an exact scan, comparing the query with under 1% of the vectors; efSearch 10 dropped recall to 0.846.	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch35-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q4</div>🔴 A tenant-filtered vector search returns 3 results when you asked for 10. Why?	The index searched first and the filter ran afterwards: the nearest neighbours overall mostly belong to other tenants, so few survive the filter. In the lab, top-40 then filtering to one tenant out of 100 returned 0.4 results on average; it took top-2,000 to fill 10, at almost the cost of a full scan, while an exact scan of the tenant's 500 rows was exact and fastest (88 µs). Fixes: filtered search inside the index (pgvector 0.8 iterative scans and similar), per-tenant partitions or indexes, or exact search when the filtered subset is small.	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch35-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q5</div>🔴 What does vector quantisation cost, and how do you get the quality back?	It stores fewer bits per dimension (float16, int8, even one bit), cutting memory and bandwidth, at some loss of ranking accuracy. In the lab, int8 vectors were 4× smaller with recall@10 of 0.981; one-bit vectors were 32× smaller with recall 0.213. Over-fetching candidates from the compressed index and re-scoring them with full-precision vectors restored recall to 1.000 (top 50 for int8, top 500 for one bit). Quantisation quality depends heavily on dimensionality and the embedding model, so measure on your data.	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch35-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q6</div>🔴 How do you chunk documents, and why use hybrid search?	Chunk along document structure (headings, paragraphs, table rows) with modest overlap, keep the heading path and source as metadata, and tune chunk size against an evaluation set: too small loses context, too large dilutes relevance and wastes tokens. Hybrid search combines dense retrieval (meaning) with keyword search (exact identifiers, error codes, names) and fuses the rankings, typically with Reciprocal Rank Fusion (1/(60 + rank) per list), optionally followed by a cross-encoder re-ranker on the top candidates.	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch35-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q7</div>🔴 How does tool calling work, and who executes the tool?	You send tool definitions (names, descriptions, JSON schemas) with the conversation; the model may reply with a structured tool call instead of text; your code validates the call, checks policy and permissions, executes it, and sends the result back as a tool message; the loop continues until the model answers. The model executes nothing. Spring AI's <code>ToolCallingAdvisor</code> and LangChain4j's AI services run the loop, but the policies (allowed tools, authorisation, approvals, step limits) are yours.	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch35-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q8</div>🔴 How do you get reliable structured output from a model?	Constrain it at the source (the provider's JSON-schema structured output, or framework support such as Spring AI's <code>.entity(Class)</code>), parse strictly, repair cheap formatting problems (code fences, prose), then validate business rules in code, because valid JSON can still be a wrong decision. On failure, re-prompt with the specific error and cap the attempts. In the lab, strict parsing failed 4 of 7 replies, repair rescued 2, and validation still rejected 2 that parsed perfectly (an amount above the order total, a different order ID).	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch35-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q9</div>🟣 How do you protect an agent against prompt injection?	Assume it will be fooled, and limit what a fooled model can do. Least-privilege tools that act with the end user's permissions; policies in code (allow-listed destinations, limits) based on your data, not the model's words; human approval for irreversible actions; isolate untrusted content (process it in a call without tools); keep secrets out of prompts; treat output as untrusted (encode, parameterise); and break the lethal trifecta (private data + untrusted content + external communication). Detection is a speed bump: in the lab, a regex blocklist caught 2 of 8 attacks and blocked a legitimate customer.	ch35 part::XII purpose::decisions
jim-ch35-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q10</div>🔴 Your Spring service calls an LLM at 50 requests per second with 6-second responses. How do you size and protect it?	Little's law: about 300 calls in flight. Use virtual threads (or a reactive client) so waiting is cheap, and a semaphore sized to the provider's request and token-per-minute limits so spikes queue or shed instead of producing 429 storms. Set connect, read and overall deadlines; retry 429 and 5xx with backoff, jitter and <code>Retry-After</code>; add a circuit breaker and a fallback (another model, a cached or non-AI answer). Stream to users for time-to-first-token. Control cost with output caps, model routing, prompt caching and per-tenant budgets, and record tokens per request.	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch35-q011	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q11</div>🔴 How do you test and evaluate an LLM feature?	Deterministic unit tests for everything around the model (tools, parsers, guardrails, policies), with a scripted model stub for loop logic. An evaluation set of real questions with expected answers and, for RAG, expected passages; metrics per stage (retrieval recall@k and MRR; extraction accuracy; faithfulness and relevance via an LLM judge with human spot checks). Run evals in CI on every prompt, model or retrieval change, and before model upgrades. In production, watch task success, user feedback, escalations, guardrail triggers and cost.	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch35-q012	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q12</div>🟣 Spring AI or LangChain4j?	On Spring Boot 4, Spring AI fits naturally: auto-configuration, <code>ChatClient</code> with an advisor chain, Micrometer observability, and MCP server support through Spring services. LangChain4j is framework-independent (plain Java, Quarkus, Helidon, Spring), with declarative AI services, guardrails and an agentic module, and it ships features quickly. Both cover models, embeddings, vector stores, tools, RAG and MCP. Choose by platform fit and team familiarity, keep provider-specific code at the edge, and keep policies in your own code so switching frameworks or providers isn't a rewrite.	ch35 part::XII purpose::decisions
jim-ch35-q013	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q13</div>🟣 What is MCP, and what's risky about connecting MCP servers?	The Model Context Protocol is an open, JSON-RPC-based standard for exposing tools, resources and prompts to AI applications, now governed by the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation; the 2026-07-28 revision made it stateless so servers scale like ordinary HTTP services. The risk: a server's tool descriptions and results flow into the model's context, so a malicious or compromised server can inject instructions, and its tools can act with whatever access you granted. Authenticate servers, grant least privilege, pin and review versions and tool descriptions, and never combine untrusted servers with private data and external actions in one agent.	ch35 part::XII purpose::decisions
jim-ch35-q014	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 35 · AI Features in Java: Spring AI, LangChain4j, RAG &amp; Guardrails · Q14</div>🔴 When is brute-force vector search the right answer?	When the collection, or the filtered subset a query actually searches, is small: tens of thousands of vectors scan in milliseconds, with perfect recall and no index to build or maintain. In the lab, scanning one tenant's 500 vectors took 88 µs with recall 1.000, beating every filtered HNSW variant; the full 50,000-vector scan took 2.8 ms. The scan also vectorises well: in §7's lab the Vector API made it 3.5× faster than a scalar loop. Use ANN indexes when collections are large and latency budgets tight.	ch35 part::XII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch36-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q1</div>🟣 Why must the idempotency key be claimed before the charge, rather than saved after it?	Because the unique constraint only protects what happens after it fires. If the key is saved after the charge, two concurrent requests both find nothing, both charge, and the constraint rejects the second <i>save</i> when the money has already moved. A crash between charge and save leaves no record at all, so a retry charges again. Claiming first (insert <code>PENDING</code> under the constraint, commit, then charge) decides ownership before any side effect: in the lab, four simultaneous copies of 1,000 payments produced about 4,000 charges with save-after (3,997 in the run shown) and exactly 1,000 with claim-first.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch36-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q2</div>🔴 A PSP call times out. What state is the payment in, and what do you do?	Unknown: the card may or may not have been charged. Record <code>UNKNOWN</code>, tell the client "processing" (<code>202</code>), and resolve it with the reconciler. While the PSP still keeps its idempotency key, re-send the same request with the same key, at the same PSP, so the PSP replays its first outcome or performs the charge exactly once. After that window, never re-send: look the charge up by your own reference, and if the search finds nothing, keep waiting for an authoritative answer, because search indexes lag. Never retry with a new key; in the lab that charged twice.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch36-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q3</div>🟣 The operation ID is derived from the merchant and the client's key. What does that protect, and what doesn't it?	If the row is lost and the client retries, a random ID would make a new PSP key and a second charge; a derived one recreates the same key, so the PSP replays the first charge. But only while the PSP still holds the key, and only at the same PSP: Stripe may prune keys after 24 hours, and in the lab a retry 26 hours after the loss charged twice. So make the claim durable first (synchronous replication, and failover only to a standby that confirmed it), keep a journal outside the database with each operation's age and PSP, and let recovery re-send only inside the key window at that PSP. Outside it, recovery only reads, and when it can't establish the age or the PSP it escalates.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch36-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q4</div>🟣 Why pin each operation to one PSP, even when you have two?	Idempotency keys are scoped to one PSP. If an operation's outcome is unknown at PSP A and you send it to PSP B, both may charge. Failover is safe only for new operations; unresolved ones must be resolved by asking the PSP that owns them.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch36-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q5</div>🔵 How do you make rollback safe for a change like this?	Answer three questions per stage. Can the old version read what the new one writes? Not by default, so ship a read shim to the old version first: it checks the new table before charging. What must flow back? Not the responses, which the shim reads in place, but the unresolved operations: drain the merchant and let the new reconciler settle them before handing traffic back, because the old code has no reconciler. When does rollback become a forward repair? Once charges have been made under the new keys, once the old table is dropped, and once a second PSP is live. Name that point in the plan, hold a go/no-go review before crossing it, and test the way back as well as the way forward.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch36-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q6</div>🔴 What does daily settlement reconciliation catch that the rest of the design doesn't?	Everything the design's assumptions miss: bugs in the reconciler, operations lost in a failover, PSP-side anomalies, amount mismatches, and charges made outside the normal path. It compares an independent source (the PSP's settlement file) with your records, so it's the only check that doesn't share your code's blind spots. Each mismatch class needs an owner and a target of zero.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch36-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q7</div>🔵 Which numbers would you put on the dashboard for this design, and why those?	One per invariant, measured independently where possible: double charges and orphan charges per day from settlement data, the age of the oldest unresolved operation, imbalanced ledger transfers, reconciler actions by result, and escalations where recovery couldn't establish an operation's age or PSP. Then client-behaviour signals: the 409 rate (aggressive retries) and the 422 rate (key-reuse bugs), plus PSP timeouts per PSP. Each one maps to an invariant or a decision, so it changes what somebody does.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch36-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q8</div>🔴 The PSP's search finds no charge for an <code>UNKNOWN</code> payment. Can you mark it <code>NOT_CHARGED</code>?	No. Absence in a search index is not evidence of absence: Stripe documents its search as eventually consistent, usually current within a minute but up to an hour behind during outages, and says not to use it for read-after-write flows. In the lab, taking an empty search as "not charged" and charging again produced two charges. Keep the operation <code>UNKNOWN</code>, keep telling the client "processing", and settle it only from an authoritative source: one the PSP documents as complete for that period, or the settlement report once it's final. If that takes too long, escalate to a person rather than guess.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch36-q009	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q9</div>🔵 How do you move merchants from the old idempotency table to the new design without charging anyone twice, in either direction?	Make each version look up the other's records before it charges: a legacy lookup in the new path, which adopts a completed payment with its response, charge ID and age, and a read shim in the old one, deployed first. Switch each merchant through a drain: no old request in flight, a backfill in one transaction, then the switch. Going back, drain again and let the new reconciler settle unresolved operations first, because the old code can't. Then prove it with a migration test in both directions: in the PostgreSQL lab, a switch without a lookup charged 100 payments again, and a rollback without a shim charged 101 again; with the lookup, the shim and the drains, nothing was charged twice.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch36-q010	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 36 · Case Study: Payments That Never Charge Twice · Q10</div>🔴 Your claim is <code>INSERT … ON CONFLICT (merchant_id, idempotency_key) DO NOTHING</code>, and a few duplicates fail with a primary-key violation under load. Why?	Because the primary key is derived from the same merchant and key, so a duplicate violates two unique constraints, and <code>ON CONFLICT</code> only absorbs conflicts on the arbiters its target names. A duplicate that races past the arbiter check can still collide on the primary key and raise an error (19 of 2,000 claims in the PostgreSQL lab). Omit the conflict target, which makes every unique constraint an arbiter for <code>DO NOTHING</code>, or stop deriving the primary key from the client's key.	ch36 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch37-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 37 · Case Study: Moving 180 Services to JDK 25 · Q1</div>🔴 The static scan flagged Guava and protobuf the same way. Why isn't a static scan enough, and what do you add?	<code>jdeps</code> reports references, not behaviour. A library can reference <code>sun.misc.Unsafe</code> and fall back when it's refused, or fail. In §3.2, Guava 32 fell back to pure Java under <code>deny</code>, and protobuf-java 3.21 failed in a static initialiser, which in a service means every later use of that class fails too. Add a CI job that runs the tests with the future default (<code>--sun-misc-unsafe-memory-access=deny</code>) so those failures appear today, and treat every startup <code>WARNING:</code> line as a finding. Count libraries from the scan, not from warnings: the warning names only the first caller.	ch37 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch37-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 37 · Case Study: Moving 180 Services to JDK 25 · Q2</div>🔴 Where should JDK-specific JVM flags live, and why does it matter for rollback?	In the image, for example in <code>JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS</code>, not in the deployment manifest. Flags are JDK-specific in both directions: JDK 21 won't start with <code>-XX:+UseCompactObjectHeaders</code> or <code>--sun-misc-unsafe-memory-access</code>, and JDK 25 won't start with <code>-Djava.security.manager=allow</code>. If the flags sit in the manifest, a rollback that switches only the image fails to start, at the moment you most need it. Keep only flags both JDKs accept in manifests, and lint every manifest against both JDKs in CI.	ch37 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch37-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 37 · Case Study: Moving 180 Services to JDK 25 · Q3</div>🔴 A service runs <code>-XX:+UseZGC</code> on JDK 21 with no other GC flags. What changes on JDK 25, and how would you gate it?	The collector algorithm. On JDK 21, <code>-XX:+UseZGC</code> alone selects the legacy single-generation ZGC (the <code>gc+init</code> log says so); on 25 ZGC is always generational, and <code>-XX:±ZGenerational</code> is ignored with a warning. Generational ZGC usually needs less CPU and memory for similar pauses, but treat it as a GC change: put the service in the pilot, run it under production-like load, and gate on pause p99, GC CPU share, allocation stalls and RSS as well as request latency. The p99 gate needs enough requests to see a tail regression (§8).	ch37 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch37-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 37 · Case Study: Moving 180 Services to JDK 25 · Q4</div>🟣 Why gate on request counts rather than bake time? What do you do for a service with 15 requests per second?	Because a percentile's noise depends on how many requests it's estimated from, not on how long you waited. In the simulation, a p99 gate with 1,000 canary requests raised false alarms on 19% of healthy releases and missed a third of 10% regressions; at 20,000 both errors were under 1%. A 15 req/s service with a 5% canary needs about 7 hours to collect 20,000 requests, so gate it on replayed or synthetic traffic in pre-production first, then bake for a day in production on signals that need fewer samples: error counts, GC, RSS and new warnings.	ch37 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch37-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 37 · Case Study: Moving 180 Services to JDK 25 · Q5</div>🔵 Which steps of the migration are one-way, and how do you sequence shared libraries?	Running on the new JDK (step A) is reversible by switching the image back, as long as flags travel with the image and nothing the new JDK wrote is unreadable by the old one. Raising the release level (step B) is one-way: JDK 21 refuses class file version 69. Shared libraries move last, because a library compiled for 25 forces every consumer onto 25. So: every consumer finishes step A, the library raises its release, then consumers raise theirs. Retiring the old images is the platform's point of no return, and it gets its own go/no-go.	ch37 part::XIII purpose::execution
