jim-ch37-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 37 · Case Study: Moving 180 Services to JDK 25 · Q6</div>🔵 Ten services are on JDK 11, and two teams say they can't move this half. What do you do?	Make it explicit rather than fight it. The JDK 11 services get their own track, because they need framework upgrades first; each one starts as an exception with an owner, a plan and a date. The two teams file exceptions: reason, plan, date, and a compensating control (a supported, patched build of their current JDK). Exceptions last 90 days, renewals need the architecture group and a director, and their cost shows on the team's showback. Then find out why they can't move: if it's capacity, the platform team can pair; if it's a missing library release, that's a risk for everyone and goes on the register.	ch37 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch37-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 37 · Case Study: Moving 180 Services to JDK 25 · Q7</div>🔵 Six months later, how do you know whether the programme was worth it?	Compare against what the ADR promised, with numbers from production: services on 25 by runtime metric, patch latency before and after, RSS and CPU per request before and after (median and worst), severe incidents caused by the move, engineer-days spent against the estimate, and exceptions still open. Then write it down as a follow-up memo, including what you'd do differently, such as whether waiting for an LTS would have been cheaper. A decision you never revisit can't teach you anything.	ch37 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch38-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 38 · Case Study: A Multi-Tenant Webhook Delivery Platform · Q1</div>🔴 One tenant's endpoint hangs. Why does every tenant slow down when deliveries share one queue?	Little's law: the requests in flight equal the arrival rate times the time each one takes. A hung endpoint receiving 10 events per second, each held until a 10-second timeout, needs 100 workers just for its own first attempts, and retries add more. With a pool of 64, the hung calls take every worker, and every other tenant's events queue behind them. In the lab, healthy tenants waited 71 seconds at the median and over 6 minutes at p99. The fix is to bound how much of the pool one endpoint can hold (a per-endpoint concurrency cap) and to serve endpoints fairly.	ch38 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch38-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 38 · Case Study: A Multi-Tenant Webhook Delivery Platform · Q2</div>🟣 Why a concurrency cap per endpoint rather than a rate limit per tenant?	Because the scarce resource is worker time, and a rate limit doesn't bound it. At 10 requests per second, a fast endpoint holds one worker, but a 10-second endpoint holds 100. A concurrency cap bounds the pool share directly, whatever the endpoint's latency, and it also paces the drain when an endpoint recovers. Rate limits still have a place, to protect a receiver that asks for one or to enforce a contract. Use both, but isolation comes from the cap.	ch38 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch38-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 38 · Case Study: A Multi-Tenant Webhook Delivery Platform · Q3</div>🔴 Fair queues already isolate tenants. What do circuit breakers add, and what do they cost?	They stop paying for an endpoint that's known to be down. With fair queues alone, the dead endpoint still holds its cap of workers timing out: 960 timed-out attempts and 2.7 worker-hours in a 10-minute outage in the lab. With breakers, 25 attempts. Queued events also stop spending their retry attempts while the breaker is open. The cost is recovery latency: the breaker only notices recovery at its next probe, so the backlog cleared after 60 seconds instead of 31. The maximum open interval trades waste against recovery time.	ch38 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch38-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 38 · Case Study: A Multi-Tenant Webhook Delivery Platform · Q4</div>🟣 What do you tell receivers about duplicates and ordering?	Duplicates will happen: a lost response, an expired lease or a rebalance causes a resend with the same <code>webhook-id</code>. So: verify the signature, keep seen IDs for at least the retry window (three days here), reply 2xx quickly and process afterwards. A replay isn't a duplicate: it arrives with a new <code>webhook-id</code> and the same <code>event_id</code>, because the tenant asked to see the event again, so receivers whose processing isn't idempotent per event should also check the <code>event_id</code>. Order isn't guaranteed, because retries reorder events. Each payload carries a per-resource sequence number, so receivers can ignore stale updates, and receivers that need the current state should fetch it through the API, treating the webhook as a hint.	ch38 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch38-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 38 · Case Study: A Multi-Tenant Webhook Delivery Platform · Q5</div>🔴 How do you stop tenants using your webhook sender to reach internal systems?	Treat it as SSRF, because the tenant chooses the URL. Resolve the host, check every resolved address against an allow-list of global unicast addresses (not a deny-list of private ranges), and connect to exactly the address you checked, ideally through an egress proxy that does all three, so DNS can't change the answer in between. Don't follow redirects. In the lab, a "loopback or private" check let through 8 of 15 addresses it should have refused, including the metadata service at 169.254.169.254 written two ways, 0.0.0.0, and IPv6 private ranges, which Java's <code>isSiteLocalAddress()</code> doesn't cover.	ch38 part::XIII purpose::mechanisms
jim-ch38-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 38 · Case Study: A Multi-Tenant Webhook Delivery Platform · Q6</div>🔵 How do you move tenants from producer-sent webhooks to the platform without double sends or lost events?	Make duplicates harmless first, then switch. Stage 1 adds a <code>webhook-id</code> derived from the event and endpoint to the producers' existing sends, so the platform will derive the same ID and receivers can dedupe across both paths. Stage 2 shadows: the platform records what it would send, and you compare. Stage 3 switches tenants with a per-tenant flag, small cohorts first. Rolling a tenant back moves new events back to the producers, while the platform drains that tenant's pending retries; shutting it down would lose them.	ch38 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch38-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 38 · Case Study: A Multi-Tenant Webhook Delivery Platform · Q7</div>🔵 A large tenant says an event never arrived. How do you answer, and what must exist before you can?	Answer from the delivery log: every attempt, with its time, the address we connected to, the status code and the latency. If there's a 2xx, show it, with the <code>webhook-id</code> they can search their own logs for. If it failed, show why and offer a replay. That needs an attempt log, kept at least as long as tenants can raise a ticket, and a replay API. Build both before the first escalation, because "we think it was sent" doesn't end the conversation.	ch38 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch38-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 38 · Case Study: A Multi-Tenant Webhook Delivery Platform · Q8</div>🟣 A tenant replays an event; the call times out and their client retries it. How do you deliver exactly one replay, distinct from the original?	Make the replay a request with its own identity. The call carries an <code>Idempotency-Key</code>, claimed as a <code>replay_request</code> row unique on (tenant, key), and the new delivery is written in the same transaction with that row's ID as its replay ID. Deliveries are unique on (event, endpoint, replay ID), so fan-out's dedupe still holds for the original (replay ID 0), and the replay's <code>webhook-id</code> is derived from (event, endpoint, replay ID), new to the receiver but stable across the replay's own retries. In the PostgreSQL lab, the old (event, endpoint) constraint silently swallowed both replays; with the replay ID, two replays made two deliveries; eleven retries of one call made one, and without a key they made eleven.	ch38 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch39-q001	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 39 · Staff &amp; Architect Artefacts: Exercises · Q1</div>🔵 What goes in the first paragraph of a one-page proposal, and why?	The ask, its cost and its return, in that order. A busy reader decides from the first paragraph whether to read on, and a proposal that opens with background makes them hunt for what you want. The rest of the page supports the first paragraph: why now, the evidence, the risks and their limits, and options the reader can choose between, including a smaller yes.	ch39 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch39-q002	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 39 · Staff &amp; Architect Artefacts: Exercises · Q2</div>🟣 Why write acceptance criteria before running the experiment?	Because criteria chosen after seeing the results bend to fit them, and then the experiment proves nothing. Writing them first also forces agreement on what "better" means: in the catalogue example, "answered p99 ≤ 200 ms, timeouts &lt; 1%, wasted queries &lt; 1%, failures within 100 ms" ruled out the design that looked like progress (unbounded virtual threads). Agreed criteria also end arguments: people can disagree about designs and still agree about thresholds.	ch39 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch39-q003	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 39 · Staff &amp; Architect Artefacts: Exercises · Q3</div>🔵 How do you record dissent in an RFC, and what happens when you lose the argument?	Write the dissent in its strongest form, so its author would sign it, then your response with evidence, then an honest status: adopted, not adopted, or open. If the decision goes against you, say so in the record and commit to making it work ("disagree and commit"), and attach a trigger that would reopen it. That's different from silent compliance: the disagreement and its condition are on record, so the organisation can learn whether you were right.	ch39 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch39-q004	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 39 · Staff &amp; Architect Artefacts: Exercises · Q4</div>🔵 What turns a list of tasks into a migration plan?	Owners who are named people; explicit dependencies, with the critical path and where the slack is; an observable "done" for each item; gates between stages; and the rollback points, especially where rollback stops being possible. A communication plan too: who hears what, and when. Without these, a plan is a wish list, and its first slip surprises everyone.	ch39 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch39-q005	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 39 · Staff &amp; Architect Artefacts: Exercises · Q5</div>🟣 A cost comparison says two options are within 2% over three years. What do you do?	Say it's a wash, then show what decides it. A sensitivity table finds the assumptions that flip the answer (in the build-versus-buy model, the vendor's price and the growth rate), and the next step is to pin those down: get a quote, agree the growth assumption. Look at the shape over time as well as the total, since a cost that grows with volume behaves differently from a fixed one. And let the non-cost constraints decide, priced where you can: data that may not leave your infrastructure, the team's experience, how reversible each option is.	ch39 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch39-q006	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 39 · Staff &amp; Architect Artefacts: Exercises · Q6</div>🟣 In the experiment, unbounded virtual threads did worse under overload than a platform pool. Why?	The bottleneck was 20 database connections. The platform pool's 200 threads accidentally limited how much work queued in front of them; virtual threads removed that limit, so far more requests waited for a connection, answers arrived after callers had given up, and the database spent its capacity on queries for nobody (237 answered in time per second, 2,309 wasted queries). Virtual threads make waiting cheap; they don't add capacity. The fix is to bound concurrency at the bottleneck (a bulkhead) and drop work whose deadline has passed. With those limits, platform threads and virtual threads both answered at the database's full capacity, 671 and 668 per second.	ch39 part::XIII purpose::decisions
jim-ch39-q007	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 39 · Staff &amp; Architect Artefacts: Exercises · Q7</div>🔵 Why keep an evidence log, and what makes an entry useful in an interview?	Because interviews probe specific decisions until they hold or crack, and memory reconstructs. A useful entry has a date, the decision, who challenged it and how, what changed as a result, a measured outcome with a source, and what you learned. The "challenged" and "changed" columns carry the most signal at staff level, because they show influence and judgement. Keep only real entries, mark anything else as practice, and fill in outcomes later with a follow-up memo.	ch39 part::XIII purpose::execution
jim-ch39-q008	<div style="font-size:0.8em;color:#57606a">Ch 39 · Staff &amp; Architect Artefacts: Exercises · Q8</div>🟣 Why does the experiment include platform threads with the same bulkhead and deadlines?	It's the control. Comparing unlimited platform threads with limited virtual threads changes two things at once, so it can't say whether the gain came from the threads or from the limits. The control row settles it: platform threads with the same limits answered 671 per second, virtual threads 668, so the limits fixed the overload. That turns one decision into two: ship the limits now, and judge virtual threads on what they do change (platform threads, pool sizing, behaviour on replayed production traffic), measured on a workload where the thread model matters, like the lab's part 2.	ch39 part::XIII purpose::decisions
